# The History of the Kings of Britain
<!-- locale: en-GB -->

*Geoffrey of Monmouth*, translated by [Ryan Allen](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-how-it-was-made.md#ryan-allen)

[All the Arthurian books](../../index.md) · **Read in:** [US English](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings.md)

## About this translation

**Written:** 1136 CE

This book is fiction. Geoffrey made up most of it and called it history, claiming he'd translated a very old book in the Britons' language that no one has ever found. Brutus of Troy, Leir and his daughters, Merlin's prophecies, and Arthur's conquest of Europe and his war on Rome never happened. The people in it who really lived, Julius Caesar, Constantine, Maelgwn, Cadwallon and the English kings, are set in stories Geoffrey made up for them. William of Newburgh called the book lies in the 1190s.

Geoffrey of Monmouth wrote the *History of the Kings of Britain* in Latin in 1136 CE, probably while he was a canon at Oxford, and offered it to Robert, earl of Gloucester. It tells the Britons' past as one line of kings, nearly two thousand years long. It runs from Brutus, the Trojan who founds Britain, through Leir, Belinus and Brennius, Vortigern, Merlin and Arthur, to Cadwaladr, the last king of the Britons, who leaves the island to the English. It's the book that made Arthur a conqueror of half of Europe, and nearly every later telling of his story starts from it. Historians read it as legend, not fact, though for four hundred years most readers took it for Britain's true past. This translation follows J. A. Giles's Latin edition of 1844.

<!-- /made-with -->

**The text.** Giles didn't print one manuscript. He built his text from nine sources, the manuscripts and printed editions he could reach, and took what he judged the best reading at each point. So the Latin this translation reads is his, not a copy any medieval reader held. More than two hundred manuscripts of the book survive. Acton Griscom and Edmond Faral each printed an edition in 1929, but neither is free to share outside the United States yet, so this translation follows Giles throughout. Every page of it was checked against a scan of Giles's book, and his page numbers stand in the text.

**Beside the other books.** Geoffrey built on two older books on this shelf. From the [*History of the Britons*](../../historia-brittonum/historia-brittonum.md) he took the Britons' descent from Troy, Vortigern and his falling tower, and Arthur's battles, and he turned its boy prophet Ambrosius into Merlin. From Gildas's [*On the Ruin of Britain*](../../de-excidio-britanniae/de-excidio-britanniae.md) he took the Romans' leaving, the Saxons' coming and the kings who ruled after Arthur, and Gildas's charge that the Britons lost their island for their sins. About 1150 CE Geoffrey went back to Merlin in a poem, the *Life of Merlin*, where Merlin lives on as a wild man in the woods after a battle, and Arthur is carried to the island of apples to be healed.

**In the Arthur story.** Geoffrey is the first to tell Arthur's life whole, and nearly every later telling starts from him, from Brutus and Leir to Merlin, Uther, Igraine and Mordred. Wherever he names someone or something the later legend makes more of, a box straight after the paragraph, headed "In the Arthur story," says what Wace, Layamon, the Welsh tales, the French romances and Malory make of it. Those boxes, like the endnotes and glossary cards that end on an asterisk, are this translation's words, not Geoffrey's.

Geoffrey joins Brutus's founding of Britain to Arthur's reign and the Britons' loss of the island, making a single story from people and events the older chronicles leave apart.

---

## Part 1: Geoffrey offers his book to Robert, and Brutus leads the Trojans to Britain

### Geoffrey tells Robert of Gloucester how he came to translate an old British book

<a id="page-1"></a>[\[Giles 1\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-1) I'd often turned many things over in my mind, and when I came to the history of Britain's kings, one thing surprised me.[^1-1] Gildas and Bede wrote about the Britons in clear, fine books, but I'd found nothing in them about the rulers who lived in Britain before Jesus Christ was born, or about Arthur and the others who followed him.[^1-2] Yet what they did deserves praise forever, and people in many lands tell it happily and by heart, as though they'd read it in writing.

> **In the Arthur story:** Geoffrey tells Arthur's life in Parts 8 to 11, from the night he's conceived at Tintagel to his last battle at Camlann, and his is the first book to tell it whole. Wace's *Roman de Brut*, Layamon's *Brut*, the French romances and Malory all start from his Arthur, and add the Round Table, Lancelot and the Grail.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

While I was thinking this over, Walter of Oxford, an archdeacon skilled in speaking and learned in foreign histories, brought me a very old book in the British language.[^1-3] It told what all of them did, in order, from Brutus, the first king of the Britons, down to Cadwaladr, the son of Cadwallon ap Cadfan, in beautifully made speeches.

> **In the Arthur story:** Brutus gives the Arthur story its frame. Wace's *Roman de Brut* and Layamon's *Brut* take their titles from him, and both count Britain's kings from Brutus down to Arthur, so Arthur rules as Brutus's heir.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

<a id="page-2"></a>[\[Giles 2\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-2) Walter asked me to, and so I've turned that book into Latin. I haven't borrowed anyone else's fine turns of speech, but kept to my own plain style and pen. If I'd filled the page with big words, my readers would get bored. They'd spend more time working out what I meant than following the story.

So, Robert of Gloucester, look kindly on my little work. Correct it with your guidance and advice. Then people will think it came not from the little spring of Geoffrey of Monmouth but from you, flavored with your Minerva's wisdom.[^2-1] Let it be known as the book of Henry I's son. That famous king of the English is your father. Philosophy has taught you the liberal arts, and your own worth has put you at the head of knights in war. That's why Britain welcomes you warmly in our time, as if it had been given a second Henry.

### Geoffrey describes the island of Britain and the five peoples who live there

Britain, the best of islands, lies in the western ocean between Gaul and Ireland. It's eight hundred miles long and two hundred wide, and its endless harvests supply everything people need.[^2-2] It's full of every kind of metal. It has broad plains, and hills good for farming, where the soil brings each crop in its season. Its forests are thick with wild animals of all sorts, with grass in their clearings for the herds to graze in turn, and flowers of many colours that give honey to the bees. It has meadows green and pleasant under high mountains, where clear springs ripple softly and send anyone lying beside them to sleep.

It's watered by lakes and by streams full of fish. Apart from the strait to the south, where ships cross to Gaul, it stretches out three famous rivers like three arms, the Thames, the Severn and the Humber, and boats bring goods along them from every nation overseas.

<a id="page-3"></a>[\[Giles 3\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-3) Britain once had twenty-eight cities. Some lie in ruins now in empty places, their walls thrown down. Others still stand whole, with churches whose towers rise beautifully high, where communities of men and women serve Yahweh as Christians do.

Five peoples live in it: the Romans, the Britons, the Saxons, the Picts and the Scots.[^3-1] The Britons held it first, from one shore to the other, until Yahweh punished their pride and they gave way to the Picts and the Saxons. Now I'll briefly tell how they came and where from; the story that follows tells it in full.

### Brutus shoots his father by accident and goes to the Trojans in Greece

After the Trojan War, Aeneas fled the ruined city with his son Ascanius and sailed to Italy. King Latinus welcomed him with honour there, but Turnus, the ruler of the Rutuli, was jealous and fought him. Aeneas won, killed Turnus, and took the kingdom of Italy and Latinus's daughter Lavinia.

When Aeneas died, Ascanius succeeded him. He founded Alba Longa on the Tiber and had a son named Silvius. Silvius fell secretly in love with a niece of Lavinia's, married her and got her pregnant. When Ascanius learned of it, he told his seers to find out the sex of the child she carried. They made sure, and said it was a boy who'd kill both his parents. He'd wander through many lands in exile, and at last rise to the highest honour. Their prophecy didn't fail them.

<a id="page-4"></a>[\[Giles 4\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-4) When her time came, she gave birth to a son and died in childbirth. The baby was given to a nurse and named Brutus. At fifteen he went hunting with Silvius and killed him by accident with an arrow. The servants were driving deer towards the two of them, and Brutus aimed at the animals but hit his father below the chest. Silvius died, and Brutus was driven out of Italy, since his family were furious at what he'd done.[^4-1]

In exile he went to Greece. There he found the descendants of Helenus, a son of Priam, held as slaves under Pandrasus, the king of the Greeks. After Troy fell, Pyrrhus had brought Helenus and many others away in chains. He'd ordered them kept captive to avenge his father, Achilles, on them. Brutus saw that they came of his own Trojan people, and stayed with them.

He soon grew so strong in war and so brave that the kings and leaders loved him more than all the young men of the land. No one was wiser, and no one fought better. Whatever gold, silver or treasure he won, he gave it all to his comrades. His fame spread to every people, and the Trojans began to flock to him. They begged him to lead them and free them from slavery to the Greeks. They said it would be easy, since they'd grown so numerous there that they were now seven thousand strong, not counting the women and children.

There was also a young Greek nobleman named Assaracus who was on their side, because his mother was Trojan. He trusted the Trojans most of all to help him hold out against the Greeks who were giving him trouble. His brother was trying to take away three castles their father had left Assaracus when he died, saying Assaracus was born to a mistress. This rival was Greek through both parents, and he'd won over the king and the other Greeks.

<a id="page-5"></a>[\[Giles 5\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-5) Brutus saw how many men there were, and that Assaracus's castles were open to him, and he felt safe saying yes to what they asked.

### Brutus leads the Trojans into the woods and writes to King Pandrasus

They chose him to lead them. He called the Trojans in from everywhere and fortified Assaracus's towns. Then he took to the woods with Assaracus and the whole crowd that followed them. Brutus wrote to Pandrasus in these words:

"Brutus, leader of what's left of Troy, sends greetings to Pandrasus, king of the Greeks. A people born of Dardanus's noble line didn't deserve to be treated in your kingdom any other way than their high birth asks, so they've gone deep into the woods. They'd rather live free like wild animals, on meat and plants, than stay your slaves any longer, however comfortable you made them.

"If this offends you, great as you are, don't blame them but forgive them, because every captive wants the same thing: to get back the standing they had. So take pity on them. Give them back the freedom they've lost, and let them live in the forest they've taken to escape you. If not, let them go with your help to the peoples of other lands."

### The Trojans ambush Pandrasus's army at a river and take his brother prisoner

<a id="page-6"></a>[\[Giles 6\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-6) When Pandrasus read the letter, he was astonished that people he'd held as slaves had grown bold enough to send him such demands. He called his nobles together and decided to raise an army and go after them. He was heading for the empty country where he thought they were, and for the town of Sparatinum, when Brutus came out with three thousand men and attacked him without warning. Brutus had heard he was coming and slipped into Sparatinum the night before, to burst out on the Greeks as they passed, unarmed and in no order.

The Trojans charged fiercely, trying to cut them down. The Greeks were stunned and scattered in every direction, and with their king in the lead they hurried to cross the Akalon, a river that ran nearby. In the crossing they were caught in the swirling current. Brutus went after them as they fled, striking them down in the water and on the bank. He rushed this way and that, glad to see them die two ways, by drowning and by the sword.

When Pandrasus's brother Antigonus saw this, he was heartbroken. He rallied his scattered soldiers into a troop and turned fast on the raging Trojans. He'd rather be killed fighting than run like a coward and drown in the muddy river. Leading them in a tight line, he urged them to stand firm, and hurled deadly spears with all his strength. But it did little or no good, because the Trojans had weapons and the rest had none. So the Trojans pressed on all the harder and made a pitiful slaughter. They didn't stop until nearly all the Greeks were dead and they'd taken Antigonus prisoner, with his companion Anacletus.

### Pandrasus lays siege to the town Brutus has left garrisoned

<a id="page-7"></a>[\[Giles 7\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-7) After the victory, Brutus left six hundred knights to guard the town and went back deep into the woods, where the Trojan people were waiting for him to protect them. Pandrasus, troubled by his own defeat and his brother's capture, spent that night gathering his scattered people. At first light he led them to besiege the town, believing Brutus had shut himself inside with Antigonus and the other prisoners.

When he got there, he studied the castle's position, divided his army into troops and placed them all around. He ordered some to keep anyone from getting out, some to divert the streams, and some to break down the walls with battering rams and other machines. They carried out his orders and did all they could to press the besieged harder. When night fell, they picked the boldest men to guard the camp and tents against a surprise attack while the rest, worn out with the work, slept.

### The besieged town sends for help, and Brutus threatens Anacletus with a sword

The town's defenders stood on top of the walls and fought back hard. They met the enemy's siege engines with their own, and all of one mind, they threw down spears and burning torches of sulfur. When the enemy put up a covered shed and began digging under it, they drove them back with Greek fire and boiling water.[^7-1] Still, short of food and worn out by the daily fighting, they sent a messenger to Brutus begging him to hurry to their help.

<a id="page-8"></a>[\[Giles 8\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-8) They were afraid they'd grow too weak to hold out. Brutus was desperate to help, but it tormented him that he didn't have enough knights to fight a pitched battle. So he made a cunning plan: to reach the enemy camp by night, get past their guards, and kill the Greeks in their sleep. He knew he couldn't do it without the agreement and aid of a Greek, so he called Anacletus, Antigonus's companion, drew his sword and spoke to him:

"My fine young man, your life and Antigonus's are over unless you do exactly what I tell you. Tonight I mean to go to the Greek camp and slaughter them by surprise. But I'm afraid their guards will see through it and stop me. Since I'd have to deal with them first, I want you to trick them for me, so I'll have a safer way in to attack the rest. Do it cleverly. Two hours after dark, go out to the siege lines and calm each of them with lies.

"Tell them you've got Antigonus out of my prison as far as a valley in the woods. Say he's hiding there in the bushes and can't get any further for the shackles you'll claim he's wearing. Then lead them to the edge of the forest as if to free him, where I'll be waiting with armed men, ready to kill them."

### Anacletus swears to help and leads the Greek guards into the woods

Anacletus saw the sword hanging over his life as Brutus spoke, and he was terrified at once. He swore to do what he was told, if he and Antigonus could live longer.

<a id="page-9"></a>[\[Giles 9\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-9) The bargain was sealed. When the hour arrived, he set off for the siege as he'd been ordered. As he came near the camp, the guards searching the hidden places ran at him from all sides. They asked why he'd come, and whether he was a traitor to the army. He pretended to be overjoyed and answered:

"I'm not here to betray my people. I've escaped the Trojans' prison and run to you, to beg you to come with me to our Antigonus. I've rescued him from Brutus's chains, but the weight of his shackles held him back. So a little while ago I told him to hide in the bushes at the edge of the woods until I found some men to bring to free him."

They weren't sure he was telling the truth, until one of them who knew him came up, greeted him, and told the others who he was. Then they didn't hesitate. They quickly called the rest, who were away, and followed him to the wood where he'd said Antigonus was hiding. As they made their way through the undergrowth, Brutus burst out with his armed troops, fell on them in their sudden terror and killed them savagely.

Then he went to the siege and split his force into three bands. He ordered each to go carefully, without a sound, to one part of the camp. Once inside, they were to hold back until Brutus had taken Pandrasus's quarters with his own men and blown his horn as the signal.

### Brutus sounds his horn, and the Trojans fall on the sleeping Greek camp

Once he'd taught them what to do, they slipped quietly in, took their places and waited for the call he'd promised. Brutus gave it without delay, once he stood in front of Pandrasus's tent, which he was burning to attack.

<a id="page-10"></a>[\[Giles 10\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-10) At the sound they drew their swords at once, went in where the Greeks lay asleep and struck again and again, walking through the camp like this without mercy. The groans of the dying woke the rest. When they saw the butchers among them, they stood stunned, like sheep a wolf has caught by surprise. They hadn't expected any danger, and had no time to take up weapons or escape. They rushed about defenseless in the middle of armed men wherever panic drove them, and the attackers cut them down at once.

Those who got away half alive, in their hurry, dashed themselves against rocks, trees and bushes and bled to death. Those with only a shield or some such cover, running for their lives over the same stones, fell fast in the dark and broke arms or legs. Those who escaped both drowned in the rivers nearby, not knowing which way to run. Hardly anyone got away unhurt. The townspeople, too, came out when they learned their comrades had come, and doubled the slaughter.

### Brutus holds Pandrasus captive and asks his men what to demand of him

Brutus, as I said, had reached the royal tent, and he took care to tie Pandrasus up and keep him safe. He was sure he'd get more of what he wanted from the man alive than dead. The troop with him kept up the killing and wiped out the part of the camp it had taken. They spent the night like this. At dawn, when daylight showed how many had fallen, Brutus was overjoyed and let his men do as they liked with the spoils. Then he took Pandrasus into the town, to wait there while he shared out the treasure.

<a id="page-11"></a>[\[Giles 11\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-11) After that he manned the castle again, had the bodies buried, gathered his troops and returned to the woods in triumph. The victory filled his men with joy, and Brutus called the elders together and asked what they'd advise him to demand of Pandrasus. Now that the king was in their power, he'd grant anything they wanted if he could go free. Their wishes differed. Some urged Brutus to ask for part of the kingdom to live in, others for permission to go and whatever they'd need for the journey.

They argued back and forth for a long time. Then one of them, named Mempricius, stood up, asked for silence and spoke while the rest listened:

"Why are you holding back over what I think is plainly good for you? There's one thing to ask for, if you want lasting peace for yourselves and your children after you: the right to leave. Suppose you spare Pandrasus's life on condition that he gives you part of Greece, and you settle among the Danaans.[^11-1] You'll never be left alone. The brothers, sons and grandsons of those you slaughtered yesterday will live mixed in with you or next door, and they'll never forget how their families died. They'll hate you forever, and the least thing will set them on to take revenge.

"You're fewer, and you won't have the strength to hold out against so many of them. If it comes to war, their numbers will grow every day and yours will shrink. So I advise you to ask him for his oldest daughter, called Innogen, as a wife for our leader. Along with her, we should demand gold, silver, ships, grain and whatever we need for the journey. If we can get that, let's leave with his consent and look for other lands."

### Brutus marries Innogen, sails from Greece and asks Diana where to settle

<a id="page-12"></a>[\[Giles 12\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-12) When he finished saying this and more like it, the whole crowd agreed with him. They urged that Pandrasus be brought out and sentenced to a cruel death if he refused. He was led in at once and set on a high seat in front of them, and told what torture he faced unless he did as they ordered. He answered:

"Since the gods have turned against me and given me and my brother Anacletus into your hands, I have to do what you ask.[^12-1] If you're refused, we'll lose our lives, which you can take or spare as you please. I think nothing's better or sweeter than life, and it's no wonder if I'll pay anything to buy it back. I obey your orders against my will. Still, I do have one comfort: I'm giving my daughter to a young man of real worth. His noble blood and the fame we've heard of show that he comes of Priam's and Anchises's line.

"Who else could have torn the exiles of Troy out of the chains of so many great princes? Who could have stood up to the Greek people with them, led so few into battle against an army so large, and brought their king away bound? Since a young man so brave could resist me, I give him my daughter Innogen. I'll give him gold, silver, ships, grain, wine, oil and whatever you think his journey needs. If you change your minds and want to settle with the Greeks, I'll give you a third of my kingdom to live in. If not, I'll do what I've promised.

<a id="page-13"></a>[\[Giles 13\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-13) "To make you feel safer, I'll stay with you as a hostage until I've done it all."

They agreed on it, and messengers were sent along every coast of Greece to gather ships. Three hundred twenty-four were gathered and brought, and loaded with grain of all kinds. The daughter was married to Brutus, and everyone was given gold and silver as their rank deserved. When it was all done, Pandrasus was let out of prison, and the Trojans sailed away from his power on a fair wind.

Innogen stood on the high stern, fainting again and again in Brutus's arms. Sobbing, she mourned leaving her parents and her country. She didn't take her eyes off the shore as long as it was in sight. Brutus comforted her tenderly, holding and kissing her, until she cried herself out and fell asleep.

They sailed for two days and a night on a good wind, and landed on an island called Leogecia. Pirates had raided it long ago, and it was empty. Brutus sent three hundred armed men ashore to see who lived there. They found nobody, but killed all kinds of wild animals in the woods. They came to a deserted city with a temple of Diana in it, where a statue of the goddess gave answers to anyone who asked.

At last they went back to the ships loaded with game, and told the others about the island and the city with its shrine. They suggested their leader go there, bring gifts, and ask her which country would give them a lasting home. Everyone agreed. Brutus took the seer Gerion and twelve elders with him, and headed out with everything needed for a sacrifice.

When they arrived, they tied ribbons around their foreheads. By the oldest rite, they set up three fires at the temple's entrance, one each to Jupiter, Mercury and Diana, and poured an offering on every one.

<a id="page-14"></a>[\[Giles 14\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-14) Brutus himself stood at Diana's altar, holding in his right hand a bowl full of wine and the blood of a white doe. He lifted his face to her statue and broke the silence with these words:[^14-1]

Goddess, mighty in the woods, terror of the wild boar,\
who may travel the paths of the sky\
and the houses of the dead: tell me what the earth holds for us,\
and say what lands you want us to live in.\
Name a sure home, where I'll worship you forever,\
where I'll build you temples with choirs of girls.

He said this nine times, then walked around the altar four times and poured the wine he held into the fire. Then he lay down on the doe's skin, spread out at its foot. He waited to fall asleep, and at last he did. It was about three hours after dark, when sleep is sweetest. He seemed to see the goddess standing over him and saying:

Brutus, past the kingdoms of Gaul, where the sun goes down,\
there's an island in the ocean, with the sea all around it.\
Giants held it once; now it lies deserted,\
and it suits your people.\
Go there: it'll be your home forever.\
Your children will find a second Troy there.\
Kings will be born there from your line,\
and the whole round world will bow to them.

Woken by this vision, Brutus wasn't sure whether it had been a dream, or the living goddess herself telling him of the land he was to go to. At last he called his men and told them in order what had happened in his sleep. They were overjoyed. They urged him to go back to the ships and sail west as fast as they could while the wind was fair, to find what Diana had promised. They wasted no time. They joined the others, put out to sea, and plowed through the waves for thirty days until they reached Africa, still not knowing where they were heading. Then they came to the Altars of the Philaeni and the place called Salinae.

<a id="page-15"></a>[\[Giles 15\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-15) They sailed between Rusicada and the mountains of Azara. Pirates attacked them there, and they were in great danger. They won, though, and grew rich on the raiders' loot.

### The Trojans sail past the Sirens, meet Corineus and land in Aquitaine

They crossed the river Malva and put in at Mauretania. Short of food and drink, they had to go ashore. They split into bands and plundered the region from one end to the other. With their holds full again, they made for the Pillars of Hercules. There sea monsters called the Sirens appeared, swam around the fleet and nearly sank it.[^15-1] They got away somehow and came to the Tyrrhenian Sea. On its shore they found four families descended from the exiles of Troy who'd fled with Antenor.

Their leader was called Corineus. He was a modest man, wise in his advice and of great courage. If he fought a giant, he'd beat him at once, as if he were fighting a boy. Seeing that they shared an ancestry, the Trojans took Corineus and the people he ruled into their company. Those people were later called the Cornish after him, and they helped Brutus more than anyone in every battle.

Then they came to Aquitaine, sailed into the mouth of the Loire and dropped anchor. They stayed there seven days and looked over the lay of the land. Goffar the Pict ruled Aquitaine then.[^15-2] When word reached him that a foreign people had landed in his kingdom with a great fleet, he sent envoys to ask whether they wanted peace or war. On their way to the ships the messengers met Corineus, who had already come ashore with two hundred men to hunt in the woods. They spoke to him at once and asked who had given him permission to enter the king's forest and take his deer.

<a id="page-16"></a>[\[Giles 16\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-16) An old law said no one could kill them without the ruler's order. Corineus answered that he didn't need anyone's say-so at all. Then one of them, named Imbertus, rushed at him and shot an arrow at him. Corineus dodged it, ran at Imbertus and smashed his head to pieces with the bow in his own hand. The rest fled, barely escaping him, and told Goffar the Pict that his envoy had been killed.

Goffar, the leader of the Poitevins, took it hard. He gathered a great army to avenge the death. When Brutus heard he was coming, he guarded the ships, ordered the women and children to stay aboard, and went out to meet them with all who were in their prime. The fighting was savage on both sides. Much of the day had gone in killing when Corineus grew ashamed that the men of Aquitaine were holding out so boldly and the Trojans weren't pressing on to win.

So he took heart again. He called his own fighters aside to the right wing of the battle, formed them into a column and charged the enemy fast. He drove into their ranks in a tight body and kept cutting them down until he'd broken through and put them all to flight. He'd lost his sword, but luck had put a double-headed axe in his hand, and every man it hit was split from top to bottom. Brutus, his companions and even the enemy were amazed at the man's daring and strength. Swinging the weapon among the fleeing troops, he terrified them with these words:

"Where are you off to, you cowards, you weaklings? Come back and fight Corineus! For shame, so many thousands running from me alone? Still, take comfort as you go: I'm the one chasing you, the man who has so often driven the Tyrrhenian giants off and thrown them down to hell three and four at a time."

### Corineus fights alone with his axe, and the Trojans drive Goffar off

<a id="page-17"></a>[\[Giles 17\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-17) At his words a count named Subardus turned back with three hundred knights and attacked him. Corineus took the attack on his shield. Not forgetting the axe in his hand, he struck Subardus on the crown of his helmet and split him from head to foot. Then he charged the rest at once, whirling it, and slaughtered them savagely. Darting this way and that, he dodged their swings and never stopped striking the enemy down. He hacked off one man's arm and hand, and cut another's shoulders from his body. He took off a third's head with one blow and slashed through a fourth's legs. They all went for him, and he fought them all.

When Brutus saw this, he ran with a troop to help, out of love for the man. A shout rose between the two peoples, strokes fell thick and fast, and both sides died without mercy. Soon the Trojans won and put Goffar the Pict and the Poitevins to flight. Goffar barely escaped and went to the other parts of Gaul, to get aid from his relatives and friends. At that time there were twelve kings in Gaul, who ruled the whole country with equal rank. They welcomed him kindly and promised with one voice to drive the foreign people who'd landed out of Aquitaine.

### Brutus plunders Aquitaine and camps where Tours now stands

Pleased with the victory, Brutus made his men rich with the spoils of the dead. He formed them into troops again and led them through the land, meaning to strip it bare and fill his ships with its wealth.

<a id="page-18"></a>[\[Giles 18\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-18) He set fire to the cities and dragged out the riches hidden in them. He ravaged the fields and slaughtered townspeople and farmers alike, wanting to wipe out the unlucky people to the last man. After ruining nearly all of Aquitaine this way, he came to where the city of Tours now stands, which, as Homer says, he later built himself.[^18-1]

Seeing the place was good for a refuge, he laid out his camp there, so he could retreat into it if he needed to. He was afraid, because Goffar the Pict had come near there with the kings and princes of Gaul and a huge army, to fight him. Once it was built, he waited there two days for Goffar, trusting in his own good sense and in the daring of the young men he led.

### Goffar and the kings of Gaul besiege the Trojans, and Corineus plans an ambush

When Goffar heard the Trojans were there, he marched day and night without stopping until he could see Brutus's camp nearby. He glared at it, smiled a little, and burst out:

"What a sad fate! These worthless exiles have even pitched their tents in my kingdom. To arms, men! Advance in tight ranks. It won't be long before we catch these weaklings like sheep and sell them as slaves through all our lands."

His whole force armed itself, formed into twelve divisions, and marched on the enemy. Brutus drew up his troops too and advanced boldly. He taught his men wisely what to do, and told them how to attack and how to stand firm. When they met, the Trojans had the upper hand at first. They made a fierce slaughter of the enemy, killing nearly two thousand, and the rest were stunned and almost turned to run.

<a id="page-19"></a>[\[Giles 19\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-19) But the side with more men usually wins. The Gauls outnumbered the Trojans thirty to one. Though they'd been beaten back at first, they regrouped and charged from all directions, cut down large numbers, and forced the Trojans back into their camp. Victorious, they besieged them there. They meant not to leave until those shut in either gave up their necks to the chains or starved slowly to death.

That night Corineus made a plan with Brutus. He'd slip out by back ways and hide in a nearby wood until morning. Then, while Brutus came out at dawn to fight, he'd come up behind the enemy with his men and attack. Brutus liked the plan. Corineus got out quietly with three thousand, as he'd said, and made for the trees.

When the next day came, Brutus drew up his army in companies, opened the camp and went out to battle. The Gauls met him at once in formation. Many thousands fell on both sides as they wounded each other, since no one spared anyone.

There was a Trojan named Turonus, Brutus's nephew. Apart from Corineus, no one there was stronger or bolder. With his sword alone he killed six hundred men, but the Gauls rushing at him cut him down sooner than he deserved. The city of Tours took its name from him, because he was buried there.

While both armies fought on hard, Corineus suddenly attacked the enemy from behind. That made the rest bolder. They pressed from the other side and struggled to break the Gauls. The Gauls were stunned by the mere shout of the Cornish at their backs. Thinking more had come than really had, they hurried to leave the field.

<a id="page-20"></a>[\[Giles 20\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-20) The Trojans chased them, struck them down as they went, and didn't stop until the victory was theirs. Though such a triumph made Brutus very glad, he was troubled too. His men grew fewer every day, while the Gauls kept growing. At last, unsure whether to keep fighting, he chose to go back to his ships while most of his people were still safe and the honour of winning was still theirs. He'd look for the island the goddess had told him of. With everyone's agreement, he went to his fleet at once. He loaded it with all the riches and treasures he'd won, then set sail. Sailing on fair winds towards it, he landed on the shore at Totnes.

### Brutus lands on Albion and names it Britain, and Corineus takes Cornwall

The island was called Albion then, and only a few giants lived there. Still, its pleasant country, its rivers full of fish and its fine forests made Brutus and his companions want to settle. They travelled through every region and drove the ones they met into caves in the mountains. Then, with their leader's consent, they divided the land by lot. They began to farm the fields and build houses, so that in a short time you'd have thought the place had always been home to people. Brutus called the island Britain after himself, and his companions the Britons, because he wanted to be remembered forever. So their language, once known as Trojan or crooked Greek, became British.[^20-1]

Corineus named the share of the kingdom that fell to him Corinea, after himself, and his people the Cornish, following Brutus's example. He could have had first choice of all the lands, but he preferred the region now called Cornwall, either from Britain's horn or from that name worn down.[^20-2]

> **In the Arthur story:** Cornwall is where Arthur's own story begins. In Part 8, Gorlois rules it as duke, and Uther comes to Igraine at Tintagel on its coast, where Arthur is conceived. In the Tristan romances and in Malory, Cornwall is King Mark's kingdom.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

<a id="page-21"></a>[\[Giles 21\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-21) He loved fighting giants, and there were more of them there than anywhere else his companions had been given. Among them was a hideous one named Gogmagog, eighteen feet tall. He was so strong that he could shake an oak and pull it up as if it were a hazel switch.[^21-1] One day, while Brutus was holding a feast for the gods at the harbour where he'd landed, Gogmagog came with twenty others of his kind and slaughtered the Britons cruelly. But the Britons gathered from all sides and won, killing all of them except Gogmagog. Brutus had ordered him kept alive, because he wanted to watch him wrestle Corineus, who was burning to fight such creatures.

Corineus was overjoyed. He got ready, threw off his weapons and challenged the giant to a wrestling match. When it began, they faced each other and locked arms, shaking the air with their panting. Soon Gogmagog gripped Corineus with all his strength and broke three of his ribs, two on the right side and one on the left.

That made Corineus furious. He summoned all his power, hoisted the giant onto his shoulders, and ran as fast as the weight let him to the nearest shore. Reaching the top of a high cliff, he shook himself free and threw the deadly monster down into the sea. Gogmagog fell on jagged rocks, was torn into a thousand pieces, and stained the waves with his blood. The place took its name from the giant's fall, and it's still called Lam Goemagot, that is, Gogmagog's Leap, today.[^21-2]

### Brutus builds New Troy on the Thames, the city Lud later renames

Once he'd looked over his kingdom, Brutus wanted to build a city. So he went around the whole land to find a good site. When he came to the river Thames, he walked along its banks and found the place that suited his plan.

<a id="page-22"></a>[\[Giles 22\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-22) He founded New Troy there. It kept that name for a long time, until it was worn down to Trinovantum.[^22-1] Afterwards Lud, the brother of Cassivellaunus who fought Julius Caesar, took the throne. He circled the city with splendid walls and with towers built with wonderful skill, and ordered it called Kaerlud, that is, Lud's City, after himself.[^22-2] That set off a great quarrel between Lud and his brother Nennius, who was angry that Lud wanted to wipe the name of Troy out of their country. Gildas the historian wrote about it at length, so I've chosen to pass over it, rather than seem to spoil with a cheaper style what so great a writer told so well.[^22-3]

> **In the Arthur story:** Julius Caesar's conquest is what Rome's claim on Arthur rests on. In Part 9, Rome demands the tribute Caesar first took from Britain, and Arthur answers that if Caesar's conquest makes Britain owe Rome, his ancestors' conquest of Rome makes Rome owe him. Wace and Layamon keep the exchange, and Malory's Book V opens on Rome's demand.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

### Brutus gives the city its laws, in the days of Eli the priest

After founding the city, Brutus settled citizens in it and gave them laws to live by in peace. At that time Eli the priest was ruling in Judea, and the Philistines had captured the Ark of the Covenant.[^22-4] Hector's sons held Troy, having driven out Antenor's descendants. In Italy the third king of the Latins, Aeneas Silvius, was on the throne. Aeneas was his father, and he was Brutus's uncle.[^22-5]

## Part 2: Brutus's sons share the island, and Leir divides his kingdom among his daughters

### Brutus's three sons divide Britain, and Humber the Hun invades Albany

<a id="page-23"></a>[\[Giles 23\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-23) Brutus and his wife Innogen had three famous children, named Locrinus, Albanactus and Camber.[^23-1] Their father died in the twenty-fourth year after he came to the island. They buried him inside the city he'd founded, divided the kingdom of Britain among them, and each went off to his own share.

Locrinus, the firstborn, took the middle of the country, later named Loegria after him.[^23-2] Camber got the part beyond the river Severn that's now Wales. For a long time afterwards it was known as Cambria after him, and that's why the people there still call themselves Cambri in British.[^23-3] Albanactus, the youngest, had the land our language today calls Scotland. He named it Albany after himself.[^23-4]

The brothers reigned in peace for a long time. Then Humber, the king of the Huns, landed in Albany.[^23-5] He fought Albanactus, killed him, and drove the people of the country to flee to Locrinus.

### Locrinus fights Humber at the river and falls in love with Estrildis

When Locrinus heard the news, he joined forces with his brother Camber, gathered all the young men of the land, and went to meet the king of the Huns near the river now called the Humber.

<a id="page-24"></a>[\[Giles 24\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-24) In the battle he put Humber to flight. Humber fled as far as the water, drowned in it, and left it his name. Locrinus gave the enemy's spoils to his companions after the victory. He kept nothing for himself but the gold and silver he found in their ships, and three girls of wonderful beauty.

One was the daughter of a king of Germany. Humber had carried her off with the other two while he was ravaging her country. Her name was Estrildis, and she was so beautiful that it was hard to find anyone to compare with her. Neither polished ivory, nor fresh snow, nor any lily was whiter than her skin. Locrinus fell in love with her and wanted her as his wife and in his bed. When Corineus found out, he was furious, because Locrinus was promised to his daughter.

### Corineus threatens Locrinus with his axe for breaking his word

So Corineus went to the king, swinging a double-headed axe in his right hand, and spoke to him like this:

"Is this how you pay me back, Locrinus, for all the wounds I took serving your father, while he fought peoples nobody knew? You'd set my daughter aside and lower yourself to marry some barbarian? You won't get away with it while there's strength in this right hand. It's killed giants all along the Tyrrhenian coast."

He shouted this again and again, swinging the axe as if to strike, until friends of both men stepped between them. Once they'd calmed Corineus down, they made Locrinus keep his promise.

### Locrinus marries Gwendolen and hides Estrildis under the city

<a id="page-25"></a>[\[Giles 25\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-25) So Locrinus married Corineus's daughter, whose name was Gwendolen. Still, he didn't forget his love for Estrildis. He had a room dug under the city of Trinovantum, shut her in it, and gave her to his closest servants to look after with honour. He wanted at least to sleep with her in secret. He was afraid of Corineus and didn't dare keep her openly, so he hid her, as I've said.

For seven whole years he visited her, and only those closest to him knew. Whenever he went to her, he pretended he was going to make a secret sacrifice to his gods. No one doubted it. Meanwhile Estrildis became pregnant and gave birth to a daughter of wonderful beauty, and named her Hafren. Gwendolen also had a child, a boy called Maddan. He was given to his grandfather Corineus to raise, and learned from his teaching.

### Locrinus leaves Gwendolen for Estrildis, and she raises Cornwall against him

Years later Corineus died, and Locrinus left Gwendolen and made Estrildis queen. Gwendolen was beside herself with rage. She went to Cornwall, gathered all the young men of that kingdom, and began to make trouble for Locrinus. At last both sides brought their armies together and fought by the Stour. There Locrinus was hit by an arrow and died.

<a id="page-26"></a>[\[Giles 26\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-26) With him dead, Gwendolen took the throne, raging like her father. She ordered Estrildis and her daughter Hafren thrown into the river that's now the Severn. She proclaimed throughout Britain that its waters should bear the girl's name. She wanted that name to last forever, because her own husband was the girl's father. So to this day it's Hafren in British, which another language has worn down to Sabrina.[^26-1]

### Gwendolen rules for her son Maddan, and his son Mempricius murders his brother

Gwendolen was queen for fifteen years after Locrinus was killed, and he'd reigned ten. When she saw her son Maddan was grown, she gave him the sceptre of the kingdom and was content with Cornwall for the rest of her life. At that time Samuel the prophet was ruling in Judea, Aeneas Silvius was still alive, and Homer was famous for his poems and his speeches.[^26-2]

Once Maddan had the sceptre, his wife gave him two sons, Mempricius and Malin. Maddan ruled in peace and with care for forty years. When he died, the two brothers quarrelled over the throne, since each was eager to have the whole island. Mempricius wanted his way, so he set up a meeting with Malin as if to make peace. But he was burning to betray him, and killed his brother in front of the men who'd come to talk.

Then he had all of Britain to himself. He ruled the people so cruelly that nearly every nobleman died at his hands. He hated his own family too, and crushed all rivals for his throne by force or by betrayal. He left his own wife, who'd given him a fine young son, Ebraucus, and gave himself up to the lust of Sodom, putting unnatural love ahead of natural pleasure.[^26-3]

<a id="page-27"></a>[\[Giles 27\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-27) At last, in the twentieth year of his reign, he was out hunting when he wandered from his companions into a valley. A pack of raging wolves surrounded him there and ate him alive. At that time Saul was ruling in Judea,[^27-1] and Eurystheus in Sparta.[^27-2]

### Ebraucus raids Gaul and founds York, Alclud and other towns

When Mempricius was dead, his son Ebraucus took over the government of Britain and held it forty years. He was a very tall man and wonderfully strong. He was the first after Brutus to take a fleet to Gaul. There he made war, killing the men of whole regions and crushing their cities. He came home in victory, rich with endless gold and silver.

After that, beyond the Humber, he founded a city he called Kaerebrauc after himself, that is, Ebraucus's City.[^27-3] At that time King David was ruling in Judea and Latinus Silvius in Italy. Gad the prophet was prophesying in Israel, with Nathan and Asaph.[^27-4] Ebraucus also built Alclud towards Albany, and the town of Mount Agned, now called the Castle of Girls, and Mount Dolorous.[^27-5]

> **In the Arthur story:** The romances make the Castle of Girls a prison for captive women. In the *Quest of the Holy Grail*, Galahad frees it from the seven brothers who hold it, and Malory tells the same.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

### Ebraucus's twenty sons sail to Germany and his thirty daughters to Italy

By his twenty wives he had twenty sons, and thirty daughters too. He ruled Britain with a strong hand for sixty years. The boys were Brutus Greenshield, Margadud, Sisillius, Regin, Moravid, Bladud, Lagon, Bodloan, Kincar, Spaden, Gaul, Darden, Eldad, Juor, Gangu, Hector, Kerin, Rud, Assarach and Buel.[^27-6]

<a id="page-28"></a>[\[Giles 28\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-28) The girls were Gloigni, Ignogni, Oudas, Guenliam, Gaudid, Angarad, Guendoloe, Tangustel, Gorgon, Medlan, Methahel, Ourar, Malure, Kambreda, Ragan, Gael, Ecub, Nest, Cheun, Stadud, Gladud, Ebren, Blagan, Aballac, Angaes, Galaes, the most beautiful woman in Britain or Gaul at the time, Edra, Anaor, Stadial and Egron.[^28-1]

Their father sent all the daughters to Italy, to Alba Silvius, who reigned after Latinus Silvius. There they were married to Trojan noblemen, whose beds the Latin and Sabine women refused.[^28-2] The sons, led by their brother Assarach, sailed with a fleet to Germany. With Alba Silvius's help they conquered its people and won the kingdom.

### Brutus Greenshield, Leil and Hudibras reign, and an eagle speaks at Shaftesbury

Brutus Greenshield stayed with his father, took the throne after him, and reigned twelve years. His son Leil succeeded him, a lover of peace and justice. Leil's reign was prosperous, and he built a city in the north of Britain called Kaerleil after him.[^28-3] At that time Solomon began building Yahweh's temple in Jerusalem, and the queen of Sheba came to hear Solomon's wisdom.[^28-4] Among the Latins, Alba's son Silvius Epitus took the throne.

> **In the Arthur story:** Kaerleil is Carlisle, and in the English romances and Malory it's one of Arthur's chief courts. There Lancelot is caught with Guinevere, and she's led out to be burned.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

Leil lived twenty-five years after he became king, but towards the end he ruled half-heartedly. Because he let things slide, civil war broke out without warning. After him Hudibras, his heir, reigned thirty-nine years. He made peace among the people again, and set up Kaerlem, that is, Canterbury. He founded Kaerguen too, that is, Winchester, and the town of Mount Paladur, now called Shaftesbury.

<a id="page-29"></a>[\[Giles 29\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-29) There an eagle spoke while the wall was being built. If I thought its words were as true as the rest, I wouldn't hesitate to set them down.[^29-1] At that time Capys, the son of Epitus, was reigning, and Haggai, Amos, Joel and Azariah were prophesying.[^29-2]

### Bladud builds Bath, teaches magic and tries to fly

Then Bladud, Hudibras's heir, took his father's place and ruled the kingdom twenty years. He built the city of Kaerbadum, now called Bath, and made hot baths there fit for people to use. He put them under the protection of the goddess Minerva. In her temple he set fires that never went out. They never sank into ash, but whenever they began to die down, they turned into balls of stone.[^29-3] (At that time Elijah prayed that it wouldn't rain on the earth, and none fell for three years and six months.)[^29-4]

> **In the Arthur story:** Geoffrey sets Arthur's great victory at Mount Badon at Bath. In Part 9, Arthur relieves the city from the Saxons besieging it, and Wace and Layamon tell the battle there after him.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

Bladud was a very clever man, and he taught magic throughout the kingdom of Britain. He didn't stop working wonders until he made himself wings and tried to fly through the upper air. He fell on the temple of Apollo in Trinovantum and was smashed to pieces.

### Leir, who has no son, plans to divide his kingdom among his daughters

When Bladud had met his fate, his son Leir was made king, and he ruled the country with a strong hand for sixty years. He built a city on the river Soar, which is called Kaerleir in British and Leircestre in the Saxons' language.[^29-5] He had no son, only three daughters, named Goneril, Regan and Cordelia. He loved them all very much, but the youngest, Cordelia, most. As he began to grow old, he thought of dividing his kingdom among them.

> **In the Arthur story:** Leir's story isn't part of Arthur's, but the chronicles that lead down to Arthur all retell it. Wace's *Roman de Brut* and Layamon's *Brut* tell Leir and his three daughters on their way down Brutus's line, and from them the story reached Shakespeare's *King Lear*.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

<a id="page-30"></a>[\[Giles 30\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-30) He meant to marry them to husbands fit to have them along with the crown. But first he wanted to know which of them deserved the bigger share. So he went to each and asked how much she loved him. Goneril answered first. She swore by the gods of heaven that her father mattered more to her than the soul in her own body. Her father said to her:

"Since you've put my old age ahead of your own life, my dear daughter, I'll marry you to whatever young man you choose, with a third of Britain."

Then Regan, the second, wanted to win her father's favour as her sister had. She swore she couldn't put her feelings any other way: she loved him above every living thing. Her father believed her. He married her off with another third of the kingdom, and the same honour he'd promised the oldest.

But Cordelia, the last, saw he'd been won over by her sisters' flattery. She wanted to test him, so she answered differently:

"Is there a daughter anywhere, Father, who'd dare say she loves her father more than as a father? I don't think there's any who'd say so, unless she were hiding the truth behind a joke. I've always loved you as a father, and I haven't changed. If you insist on squeezing more out of me, listen to how sure my love for you is, and stop asking. You're worth as much as you have, and that's how much I love you."

Her father thought she'd said what she really felt. He was furious, and wasted no time in giving his answer:

"You've looked down on your father's old age so much that you wouldn't even love me as your sisters do. So I'll look down on you. You'll never have a share in my kingdom with them. You're my daughter, so I don't say I won't find you some foreigner for a husband, if luck brings one along. But I promise you this: I'll never trouble to marry you off with the honour I gave the other two.

<a id="page-31"></a>[\[Giles 31\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-31) "Until now I loved you more than the others, and you love me less than they do."

He didn't wait. On the advice of the kingdom's nobles, he handed the two girls to two dukes, of Cornwall and of Albany, with only half the island while he lived, and the whole of Britain after his death.

Later Aganippus, king of the Franks, heard how beautiful Cordelia was. He sent messengers to Leir at once, asking for her as his wife. Her father, still angry, answered that he'd be glad to give her, but without land or money. He'd shared out his kingdom with all his gold and silver to Goneril and Regan, Cordelia's sisters.

When Aganippus heard this, he burned with love for the girl all the more, and appealed to King Leir again. He said he was rich enough already, since he held a third of Gaul. He wanted only her, so he could have heirs by her. At last the match was agreed. Cordelia was sent to Gaul and married to Aganippus.

### Leir's daughters strip him of his knights, and he sails to Gaul

A long time later, as Leir began to grow sluggish with age, the dukes he'd shared Britain with, and with it his two daughters, rose against him. They took away the kingdom and the royal power he'd held bravely and gloriously until then. Still, they came to terms. One of his sons-in-law, Maglaurus, duke of Albany, gave him a home with sixty knights, so he wouldn't live without honour.

<a id="page-32"></a>[\[Giles 32\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-32) Two years into his stay, his daughter Goneril grew angry at the size of his following. His men insulted the servants for not giving them bigger rations. She spoke to her husband, then told her father to make do with thirty knights and send the others away.

The king was furious. He left Maglaurus and went to Henwinus, duke of Cornwall, who'd married his second daughter, Regan. Henwinus welcomed him with honour, but before a year was out, the two households quarrelled. Regan lost her temper. She told her father to send all his knights away but five, who could serve him.

Her father was beside himself. He went back to his oldest daughter, hoping to move her to pity, so she'd keep him and his household. But she hadn't let go of her anger at all. She swore by the gods of heaven that he couldn't stay unless he dismissed the rest and made do with one man. She scolded him too: an old man with nothing, wanting to go about with such a crowd! Since she wouldn't give in at all, he obeyed. He let the others go and kept just a single knight.

Then he remembered the high rank he'd once had, and he hated the misery he'd been brought to. He began to think of going to his youngest daughter across the sea. But he doubted she'd want to do anything for him, since he'd treated her so shamefully, as I've said. Still, he couldn't bear his misery any longer. He crossed to Gaul. On the crossing he saw he ranked third among the lords on board. He burst out in tears and sobs:

"Fate, your rulings can never be taken back, and you keep to your fixed path! Why did you ever want to raise me to a happiness that couldn't last? It hurts more to remember joy lost than to be crushed by the hard times after it. The memory of those days weighs on me more than my troubles now. Then I had hundreds of thousands of knights around me. I tore down city walls and laid waste to my enemies' lands. Now my fall has made the very men who lay under my feet laugh at my weakness.

<a id="page-33"></a>[\[Giles 33\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-33) "Angry fortune! Will the day ever come when I can pay back the people who've fled my good days just as they run from my poverty now? Cordelia, my daughter, how true it was, what you answered when I asked you how much you loved me! You said, 'You're worth as much as you have, and that's how much I love you.'

"While I had something to give, I seemed to count for something with people who were friends not to me but to what I gave. They loved me for a while, but my presents more. When those were gone, so were they.

"But how will I dare face you, my dear daughter? Those words made me angry, and I meant to marry you off worse than your sisters. Now, after all I've done for them, they let me be an exile and a beggar."

He said this and more like it until he landed, and came to Karitia, where his daughter was.[^33-1] He waited outside the city and sent her a messenger to tell her he'd fallen into deep misery. He had nothing to eat or to wear, and begged her for mercy.

Cordelia was moved when she heard it, and she cried bitterly. She asked how many knights he had with him. The man said there were none, only a squire waiting with him outside. Then she took as much gold and silver as was needed and handed it over. She told the messenger to take her father to another city and pretend he was sick there. He was to bathe him, dress him and look after him. He was also to keep forty knights ready, well dressed and equipped. Once all that was in place, Leir was to let King Aganippus and his daughter know he'd come. The messenger went back at once and took King Leir to another city. He hid him there until he'd done everything Cordelia had ordered.

### Cordelia takes her father in, and the Franks receive him with honour

<a id="page-34"></a>[\[Giles 34\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-34) As soon as Leir had a king's clothes, gear and household, he sent a message to Aganippus and his daughter. He told them that his sons-in-law had driven him out of the kingdom of Britain, and that he'd come to them so he could win back his country with their help. They came out with their counts and nobles to meet him and received him with honour. They gave him power over all Gaul until they'd restored him to his old rank.

### Aganippus raises an army in Gaul and sails with Leir to Britain

Meanwhile Aganippus sent envoys throughout Gaul to gather every armed knight in it, so that he could help Leir win back the kingdom of Britain. When that was done, Leir crossed to Britain with Aganippus, his daughter and the army they'd gathered. He fought his sons-in-law and won.

He made the whole country obey him again, and three years later he died. So did Aganippus. Cordelia took the throne and buried her father in an underground chamber she'd ordered dug under the river Soar, in Leicester. It had been built in honour of two-faced Janus.[^34-1] Every year on his feast day, all the city's workmen began there the work they'd do in the months ahead.

### Cordelia's nephews rise against her, and Marganus and Cunedagius fight for Britain

<a id="page-35"></a>[\[Giles 35\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-35) Cordelia ruled the kingdom in peace for five years. Then her sisters' two sons, Marganus and Cunedagius, started to make trouble for her. Their mothers had married Maglaurus and Henwinus. Both young men were known for their courage. Maglaurus was Marganus's father, and Henwinus was Cunedagius's. After their fathers died, they became dukes in their place. They couldn't stand Britain being ruled by a woman.

So they raised armies and rose against the queen. They kept up their cruelty until they'd laid waste to whole regions and fought her in battle. At last they captured her and put her in prison. There, heartbroken at losing the kingdom, she killed herself.

Then the two cousins divided the island. Marganus got the part beyond the Humber, stretching towards Caithness. Cunedagius was given the rest, running west from the river.

Two years later, men who liked trouble in the kingdom came to Marganus and won him over. They told him it was a shameful disgrace for the firstborn not to rule the whole island. Stirred up by this and much else, he led an army through Cunedagius's lands and began to burn them.

That started a war. Cunedagius met him with all his people. In the battle he made a great slaughter and put Marganus to flight. Then he chased him from place to place, and at last caught him in a district of Wales. Marganus was killed there, and the locals have called it Margan after him ever since.[^35-1]

<a id="page-36"></a>[\[Giles 36\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-36) After his victory, Cunedagius became the island's sole king, and he ruled it gloriously for thirty-three years. At that time Isaiah and Hosea were prophesying, and Rome was founded by the twin brothers Remus and Romulus on the twenty-first of April.[^36-1]

### Ferrex and Porrex fight for the throne, and their mother takes revenge

When Cunedagius died at last, Rivallo, his heir, succeeded him, a peaceful, lucky young man who governed the kingdom with care. In his time it rained blood for three days, and swarms of deadly flies killed people. Gurgustius, his son, followed him; then Sisillius; then Jago, Gurgustius's nephew; then Kimarcus, born to Sisillius; and after him Gorboduc.[^36-2]

Gorboduc's two boys were named Ferrex and Porrex. As their father grew old, they quarrelled over which of them would succeed him. Porrex was the greedier, and he set a trap to kill Ferrex. When Ferrex found out, he got away and crossed to Gaul. With the help of Suardus, king of the Franks, he returned and made war on Porrex. In the battle Ferrex was killed, with the whole army that followed him.

Their mother, named Judon, was out of her mind with grief when she learned for sure of her son's death, and she turned to hating the other.[^36-3] She'd loved the one who died more. Her anger over it burned so hot that she wanted to avenge him on his brother. She waited until Porrex was asleep, attacked him with her maids, and hacked him to pieces.[^36-4] After that, civil war tormented the people for a long time. The kingdom fell under five kings, who wore each other down with slaughter.

### Dunvallo Molmutius unites Britain and makes himself a gold crown

<a id="page-37"></a>[\[Giles 37\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-37) In time a young man named Dunvallo Molmutius made his name by his own worth. His father was Cloten, who ruled Cornwall, and Dunvallo outdid every ruler in Britain in looks and daring. When he took over the country after his father died, he attacked Pinner, king of Loegria, and killed him in battle.[^37-1] Then Rudaucus of Wales and Staterius of Albany made an alliance. They led their armies into Dunvallo's lands to destroy the buildings and the farmers.

Dunvallo faced them with thirty thousand men and fought them. Much of the day went by in fighting, and still he hadn't won. So he called aside six hundred of his boldest young fighters and ordered them all to strip the armour from enemies who'd died and put it on. He threw off his own and did the same. Then he led them in among the enemy's charging troops, as if they were enemy soldiers. When he reached the spot where Rudaucus and Staterius were, he told his comrades to fall on them. They charged, killing both kings and many others with them.

Then Dunvallo was afraid his own side would cut him down, so he turned back with his band and took off the captured armour. He put his own back on, urged his army to attack, and charged fiercely himself. He soon won, and the enemy fled and scattered. Then he went through the lands of the two dead kings, destroying cities and towns, and made their people his subjects.

<a id="page-38"></a>[\[Giles 38\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-38) Once he'd conquered all of Britain, he had a gold crown made for himself and restored the island to how it had been before. He gave the Britons the laws called the Molmutine Laws, which the English still keep today.[^38-1] Among other things, he laid down what holy Gildas wrote about long afterwards.[^38-2] The temples of the gods and the cities were to be so honoured that any runaway or criminal who fled to one could leave it with their enemy's pardon. The roads leading to them, and the farmers' plows, were to be protected by the same law.

In his time robbers put away their swords and raiders were stopped. There was nobody anywhere who did violence to anyone. At last, forty years after he took the crown, he died. He was buried in the city of Trinovantum, near the temple of Concord, which he'd built to confirm his laws.

## Part 3: Belinus and Brennius fight over Britain, then march together on Rome

### Belinus and Brennius quarrel over the crown, and their friends divide the kingdom

Dunvallo's two sons, Belinus and Brennius, both wanted the throne after him, and they fell into a bitter quarrel over which of them would be crowned.[^38-3] They fought each other again and again, until friends of both stepped in and made peace. The peacemakers ruled that the kingdom should be split. Belinus would wear the crown and have Loegria, Wales and Cornwall. He was the firstborn, and Trojan custom gave the inheritance to the oldest son.

> **In the Arthur story:** Belinus and Brennius are why Arthur claims Rome. In Part 9, when Rome's envoys demand tribute, Arthur answers that Belinus and his brother took Rome long before, so Rome owes tribute to him. The English *Morte Arthure* has him give Lucius's messengers the same answer.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

<a id="page-39"></a>[\[Giles 39\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-39) Brennius, the younger, would be under his brother and hold Northumbria, from the Humber to Caithness. A treaty confirmed the terms, and for five years they ruled the country in peace and justice.

But trouble always tries to work its way into good times. Some men who made a trade of lies went to Brennius. "Why are you such a coward that you put up with being under Belinus?" they asked. "You have the same father and mother, and you're just as noble. Besides, you've proved yourself in plenty of wars. Time after time you've stood up to Cheulfus, duke of the Morini, when he landed in our country, and you've driven him out of your kingdom.[^39-1] Break this treaty that shames you. Marry the daughter of Elsingius, king of Norway, so that with his help you can win back the rank you've lost."

They won the young man over with this and much more, and he agreed. He went to Norway and married the king's daughter, as the flatterers had told him to.

### Guichtlac of Denmark attacks Brennius's fleet at sea for his bride

Meanwhile word of it reached Belinus. He was furious that his brother had acted against him like this without his permission. So he marched into Northumbria, took its cities and left his own troops in them.

Brennius heard what had happened. He gathered a large force of Norwegians, fitted out a fleet and headed back to Britain. He was making good time with a fair wind, and no worries, when Guichtlac, king of the Danes, caught up with him. Guichtlac had been in love with the girl Brennius married, and he'd been following him ever since. Grief-stricken, he'd raised an army, manned his ships and chased Brennius as fast as his sails would take him.

<a id="page-40"></a>[\[Giles 40\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-40) In the fight at sea, Guichtlac happened to capture the vessel carrying the bride, and he pulled it in among his own with grappling hooks. While the two sides were locked together on the open water, a sudden gale blew up. The storm scattered the ships and drove them onto different shores. The Danish king was carried along by the winds for five days. He landed in fear, with the girl, in Northumbria, not knowing what country chance had brought him to.

When the locals found out, they caught them and took them to Belinus, who was on the coast waiting for Brennius to arrive. With Guichtlac's ship were three others, one of them from Brennius's fleet. They told the king who they were. He was overjoyed that this had happened just when he was looking for a way to get back at his brother.

### Brennius demands his bride and kingdom back, and the brothers meet in battle

A few days later Brennius gathered his ships again and landed in Albany. There he learned that his wife and the others had been captured, and that Belinus had taken Northumbria from him while he was away. He sent messengers demanding his kingdom and his bride back. If he didn't get them, he swore he'd destroy the whole island from one sea to the other. He'd kill Belinus too, if he got the chance to face him.

When Belinus heard this, he flatly refused. He called up every knight in Britain and marched into Albany. Brennius learned that he'd been turned down and that his brother was coming against him. So he went to meet him in the forest of Calaterium, to fight him there.[^40-1]

<a id="page-41"></a>[\[Giles 41\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-41) When both armies reached the same field, each side divided its men into companies, and they closed and began the battle. It took up much of the day, since the bravest of them were locked in close combat. Blood ran everywhere, because the spears their strong arms threw dealt deadly wounds. The wounded fell among the breaking ranks like wheat under the harvesters' blades.

At last the Britons got the upper hand, and the Norwegians fled to their boats, their companies cut to pieces. Belinus chased them and slaughtered them without mercy. Fifteen thousand men died in that battle, and of the rest not a thousand got away unhurt. Brennius was lucky to find a single ship, and he made for the coast of Gaul. The others who'd come with him went into hiding wherever chance took them.

### Belinus frees the Danish king and the bride in return for tribute

After his victory, Belinus called all the kingdom's nobles to York, to decide with them what to do about the Danish king. From his prison, Guichtlac had sent him an offer. He'd put himself and the kingdom of Denmark under Belinus and pay him tribute every year, if he was allowed to go free with the woman he loved. He'd also confirm the deal with an oath and hostages. When the nobles met and heard it, they all agreed that Belinus should accept on those terms. So he did. Guichtlac was let out of prison and went back to Denmark with the girl.

### Belinus confirms his father's laws and builds roads across Britain

<a id="page-42"></a>[\[Giles 42\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-42) Belinus now held the whole island, and nobody stood in his way. He confirmed the laws his father had made, and ordered that justice be kept steadily throughout the kingdom. Above all, he commanded that the cities and the roads leading to them should have the same peace Dunvallo had laid down. But an argument came up over the highways, because nobody knew where their limits were.

The king wanted to take all doubt out of his law. So he called together all the island's builders and had a road made of stone and mortar. It was to run the length of Britain, from the Cornish sea up to Caithness, going straight to the cities along the way. A second was to cross the kingdom from west to east, from the city of St Davids on the coast of Dyfed,[^42-1] to Hamo's Port,[^42-2] leading to the towns between. He had two more laid at an angle across the land, to reach the other cities.[^42-3]

Then he gave the roads every honour and dignity, and declared them under his own law, so that whoever did violence on them would be punished. If you want to know everything he laid down about them, read the Molmutine Laws. Gildas the historian translated them from British into Latin, and King Alfred from Latin into English.[^42-4]

### Brennius, in exile, marries the Allobroges' heiress and becomes their duke

<a id="page-43"></a>[\[Giles 43\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-43) While Belinus reigned in peace and quiet, his brother Brennius was tormented with worry on the coast of Gaul, where he'd landed. It weighed on him that he'd been driven out of his country, with no way to go back and enjoy the rank he'd lost. Not knowing what to do, he went to the princes of Gaul with only twelve knights. He told each of them his bad luck, but none would help him. At last he came to Seginus, duke of the Allobroges, who received him with honour.[^43-1]

Brennius stayed on and became Seginus's closest friend at court. In everything, in peace and in war, he showed his worth, so that Seginus grew to love him like a father. He was good-looking, too, tall and lean. He'd also been taught to hunt with hounds and with hawks, as a man of his rank should be.

Since they'd become such friends, the duke decided to marry him to his only daughter. If no son was born to him after that, Brennius would have the kingdom of the Allobroges with the girl once Seginus was dead. If one was, he promised to help Brennius become king of Britain. It wasn't only the duke who wanted this, but all the lords under him, because Brennius had won their friendship too. The wedding took place at once, the country's chiefs swore loyalty to Brennius, and he was given the throne.

Less than a year later, the duke died. Then Brennius didn't hesitate to bind the country's leaders to him, though they already liked him.

<a id="page-44"></a>[\[Giles 44\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-44) He gave them the duke's treasure, which had been kept since his ancestors' time. He was generous with food too, which meant more than anything to the Allobroges, and his door was open to everyone.

### Brennius invades Britain, and their mother Conwenna steps between the brothers' armies

Once he'd won everyone's love, Brennius thought about how to get revenge on his brother Belinus. When he told the people under him, they all agreed to go with him to whatever kingdom he wanted to lead them. He soon raised a great army and made a treaty with the Gauls, so they'd let him cross their lands to Britain in peace. Then he fitted out a fleet on the coast of Normandy, put to sea and landed on the island with a fair wind.[^44-1]

When news of his landing spread, Belinus called up the young men of the whole kingdom and went to meet him in battle. The two armies were drawn up and about to clash when the brothers' mother, who was still alive, hurried out between the lines. Her name was Conwenna, and she longed to see the son she hadn't seen for so long. On trembling legs she reached the place where he stood. She threw her arms around his neck and kissed him over and over. Then she pulled open her dress and spoke to him, sobs breaking her words.

"Remember, my son, the breasts you nursed at, and your mother's womb. There the maker of all things made you a man out of what wasn't one yet, and from there he brought you into the world while I was in agony. Think of what I went through for you, and do what I ask. Forgive your brother, and hold back the anger you've started on.

<a id="page-45"></a>[\[Giles 45\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-45) "You've no reason to be angry with him. He's never done you any wrong. You complain that he drove you out of your country. But if you look closely at what happened, you won't find anything you could call unjust. He didn't drive you out to make things worse for you. He made you leave the lesser behind, so you'd rise to something better. Under him you held part of the kingdom. Once you lost it, you became his equal, as ruler of the Allobroges. What has he done, then, but raise you from a poor petty king to a great one? Besides, he didn't start the quarrel between you. You did. You counted on Norway's help and couldn't wait to begin it."

Her words and her tears moved him. He calmed down and did as she asked. Without being told, he took off his helmet and walked with her to Belinus. When Belinus saw him coming with a peaceful look, he threw down his weapons and ran to hug and kiss him. They were friends again at once. The troops laid down their arms, and the brothers headed for the city of Trinovantum. There they agreed on a plan, to lead their armies together into Gaul and bring all its lands under their rule.

### The brothers subdue Gaul and march into Italy towards Rome
