A year later they crossed to Gaul and began laying waste to the country. When the news spread among its peoples, all the petty kings of the Franks gathered and came out to fight them. Belinus and Brennius won, and the Franks fled with their companies wounded. Flushed with victory, the Britons and Allobroges kept after the Gauls as they ran, until they captured their leaders and forced them to give in. They tore down the walled cities too.

<a id="page-46"></a>[\[Giles 46\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-46) Within a year they'd conquered the whole country. At last, once every province had surrendered, they made for Rome with their entire host, plundering towns and farms across Italy.

### Rome's consuls buy peace and break it, and the brothers lay siege to Rome

At that time Rome had two consuls, Gabias and Porsenna, who governed the state.[^46-1] They saw that no people could stand against the fury of Belinus and Brennius. So, with the senate's consent, they came to them asking for peace and friendship. They also offered plenty of gold and silver, and tribute every year, if they'd be left alone to keep what was theirs. The kings took hostages and pardoned them, then led their troops into Germany.

When the Britons started making war on the Germans, the Romans regretted the treaty. Their courage came back, and they marched out to help the Germans. When Belinus and Brennius learned of it, they were furious and talked over how to fight both peoples. The Italians had come in such numbers that the brothers were alarmed. So they made a plan. Belinus stayed in Germany with the Britons to keep up the war there. Brennius went with his armies to Rome, to punish the Romans for breaking the treaty.

When the Italians learned this, they left the Germans and hurried back towards Rome to get there before Brennius. As soon as Belinus heard, he called back his army and marched all night. He found a valley the enemy would have to pass through, hid in it and waited for them.

<a id="page-47"></a>[\[Giles 47\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-47) The next day the Italians reached the place on their march. They saw the hollow flashing with armour and were stunned, thinking Brennius and the Senonian Gauls had come.[^47-1] Belinus, seeing them, fell on them in a sudden charge. The Romans were caught off guard. They were marching out of order and unarmed, and fled the field. Belinus chased them mercilessly and kept killing them until darkness came and he had to stop.

Then he went, victorious, to Brennius, who'd had Rome under siege for three days. They joined their armies and attacked the city from every side, trying to break down the walls. To do more harm still, they put up gallows in front of the gates. They told the defenders that they'd hang the hostages Rome had given unless the Romans surrendered.

But the Romans held firm. They put aside their love for their sons and grandsons and set out to protect themselves. Sometimes they smashed the brothers' siege engines with machines of their own, of the same kind or another, and at other times drove the attackers off the walls with every weapon they could throw. When Belinus and Brennius saw it, they flared up in a reckless rage and ordered twenty-four of the noblest hostages hanged where their parents could see.

That made the Romans more defiant still. Gabias and Porsenna had promised to come to their help the next day. Relying on that, they decided to go out of the city and fight. While they were drawing up their companies with skill, the two consuls arrived ready for battle, having gathered their scattered men. They came in close ranks and fell on the Britons and the Allobroges by surprise. The citizens who'd come out joined them, and at first they killed plenty.

The brothers saw their comrades cut down so suddenly and were badly shaken. They began urging their men on and putting them back into companies. Charging again and again, they forced the enemy back.

<a id="page-48"></a>[\[Giles 48\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-48) At last, with many thousands dead on both sides, the brothers won. Gabias was killed and Porsenna captured. They took the city and gave their comrades the wealth the citizens had hidden.

### Brennius stays to rule Italy, and Belinus goes home and builds

After the victory, Brennius stayed in Italy and ruled its people with a cruelty never heard of before. I haven't bothered to tell the rest of what he did, or how he died, since the Roman histories tell it. It would have made this book far too long, and going over what others have told would have taken me away from my purpose.[^48-1]

Belinus went back to Britain and ruled the country in peace for the rest of his life. He repaired the cities wherever they'd fallen down and built many new ones. Among others he founded one on the river Usk, near the Severn sea. For a long time it was named Kaerosc, and it was the chief city of Dyfed. After the Romans came, that name was dropped, and it was called the City of the Legions, after the Roman legions that used to spend the winter there.[^48-2]

> **In the Arthur story:** The City of the Legions is Caerleon, the city of Arthur's court. In Part 9, Arthur holds his great Whitsun feast there and wears his crown. The Welsh tales of Owain son of Urien, Peredur and Geraint open with Arthur at Caerleon, and Malory has him hold a feast there after he's crowned.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

In Trinovantum he also built a gate of wonderful workmanship on the bank of the Thames. The citizens now call it Billingsgate, after his name.[^48-3] Above it he raised a huge tower, and at its foot a harbour where ships could land. He brought back his father's laws everywhere in the kingdom and held to steady justice. In his days the people had more riches than any age before or since is said to have had. At last he died. His body was burned and the ashes put in a gold urn, and with great skill they set it on top of that tower in Trinovantum.

### Gurguit Barbtruc forces the Danes back to paying tribute

<a id="page-49"></a>[\[Giles 49\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-49) His son Gurguit Barbtruc succeeded him, a modest, sensible man.[^49-1] He followed his father in everything and loved peace and justice. When his neighbours rebelled against him, he called up his father's daring, fought bitter battles and brought his enemies back under the rule they owed him. Among much else, the king of the Danes, who'd paid tribute to his father, refused to pay it to him and denied he was bound to him. Gurguit took it badly. He sailed a fleet to Denmark, crushed its people in fierce fighting, killed the king and made the country subject to Britain again.

### Gurguit meets Partholón's exiles from Spain and gives them Ireland

After that victory he was sailing home by way of the Orkney Islands when he found thirty ships full of men and women.[^49-2] He wanted to know why they'd come. Their leader, named Partholón, came to him, bowed low and asked for peace.[^49-3] Partholón said he'd been driven out of Spain, and he was sailing those waters to find somewhere to live. He hoped for a small part of Britain to settle in, so he wouldn't have to roam the sea any longer, which he'd come to hate. A year and a half had already passed since he'd been exiled from his country and set out on the ocean with his people.

> **In the Arthur story:** Orkney is the kingdom of Gawain's family in the romances. Malory's King Lot of Orkney marries Arthur's half-sister Morgause, and their sons are Gawain, Agravain, Gaheris and Gareth. In Part 9, Geoffrey gives Orkney a king of its own, Gunvasius, who submits to Arthur.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

Gurguit learned that they'd come from Spain, that they were called the Barclenses, and what they wanted. So he sent men with them to Ireland, where nobody lived then, and gave it to them. Their numbers grew there, and they've held the island to this day.

<a id="page-50"></a>[\[Giles 50\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-50) Gurguit Barbtruc died in peace and was buried in the City of the Legions. After his father's death, he'd worked to adorn it with buildings and walls.

### Guithelin reigns, and his wife Marcia writes the Marcian law

Guithelin took the crown after him, and he ruled kindly and modestly all his life. His wife was a woman of high birth named Marcia, learned in every art. She was clever enough to make many discoveries no one had heard of before, and among them she drew up what the Britons called the Marcian law. King Alfred put it into English with the rest and gave it an English name.[^50-1] When Guithelin died, the government of the kingdom was left to the queen and her son, Sisillius II. He was seven years old then, too young to rule.

### Marcia rules for her young son, and Morvidus comes to the throne

So his mother, a woman of great wisdom and sense, took power over the whole island. When she died, Sisillius II was crowned and governed. After him came Kinarius, his heir, and then Kinarius's brother Danius.[^50-2] On Danius's death, the crown went to Morvidus, born to Danius and a mistress named Tangustela.

Morvidus would have been famous for his great courage if he hadn't given way to savage cruelty. When angry, he killed anyone he could, if he had a weapon at hand. He was handsome and lavish with gifts, and in all the kingdom he was the strongest fighter there was.

### Morvidus tortures his captives and goes out alone against a sea monster

<a id="page-51"></a>[\[Giles 51\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-51) In his time a king of the Moriani landed in Northumbria with a large force and began to lay waste to the country.[^51-1] Morvidus called up all the young men in his lands, marched against him and fought him. He did more in the battle on his own than most of his army. When he won, not one of the enemy escaped with their life. He had them brought to him one after another, to feed his cruelty by killing each. When he grew tired and stopped for a while, he ordered them skinned alive, and then burned.

While he was doing these and other brutal things, a disaster struck that ended his wickedness. A monster more savage than any ever heard of had come from the Irish Sea, and it kept eating the people on the coast. When word of it reached Morvidus, he went to it and fought it alone. He used up all his weapons on it for nothing. Then the monster rushed at him, opened its jaws and gulped him down as easily as a small fish.

### Gorbonianus rules justly and rebuilds the temples

Morvidus had fathered five sons, and the firstborn, named Gorbonianus, took the throne. Nobody in that time was more just or loved fairness more, or governed the people with more care. His habit was always to give the gods their due honour first, and then fair justice to the common people. He restored the temples of the gods in every city of Britain and built many new ones.

<a id="page-52"></a>[\[Giles 52\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-52) All his life the island had wealth no neighbouring country could match. He urged the farmers to work the land, and protected them from being mistreated by their lords. He made the young fighting men rich with gold and silver, so none of them needed to wrong anyone. After these and many other acts of his natural goodness, he died and was buried in Trinovantum.

### The nobles depose Archgallo, and his brother Elidurus finds him in a forest

His brother Archgallo was crowned after him, and he was his opposite in everything he did.[^52-1] He worked everywhere to bring the nobles down and raise up ordinary people, and to take from every rich man what he had, piling up endless treasure. The kingdom's lords wouldn't put up with it any longer. They rose against him and threw him off the throne. Then they made Elidurus, another of the brothers, king. Later he was called the Dutiful, for the mercy he showed Archgallo.[^52-2]

Elidurus had reigned five years when he happened to be hunting in the forest of Calaterium, and met Archgallo, now an exile. Archgallo had gone through all the countries around asking for help to win back the rank he'd lost, and found it nowhere. He couldn't stand the poverty he'd fallen into any longer, so he'd come back to Britain with only ten knights. He was searching out the men who'd been his friends before. As he passed that forest, Elidurus caught sight of him, not expecting him at all.

Elidurus ran to him, hugged him and kissed him over and over. He cried a long time over his brother's misery, then took him to the city of Alclud and hid him in his own bedroom.

<a id="page-53"></a>[\[Giles 53\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-53) Then he pretended to be sick and sent messengers through the whole kingdom, suggesting the princes under him come and visit him. When they'd all gathered where he lay, he ordered each to come in to him alone and quietly. He claimed the talk of a crowd would make his head worse. Each believed him and did as he said, coming into the house one after another. Elidurus had servants ready to take hold of each one as he came in and behead him, unless he swore loyalty to Archgallo again. He did this with every one of them, and fear made them all make peace with Archgallo.

Once the agreement was confirmed, Elidurus brought Archgallo to York. There he took the crown off his own head and put it on his brother's. That's how he got the name the Dutiful, for the loyalty he'd shown. Archgallo reigned ten years and gave up the wickedness of his first reign. He did the opposite now, raising the nobles and keeping ordinary people down. He let everyone keep their own property and did fair justice. At last he fell ill and died, and was buried in Leicester.

### Elidurus reigns again, and his brothers Ingenius and Peredurus imprison him

Elidurus was made king again and given back his old rank. He followed his oldest brother, Gorbonianus, in every kind of goodness. But the two others, Ingenius and Peredurus, gathered armed men from everywhere and went to war with him.[^53-1] They won, captured him and shut him up in the tower in Trinovantum, with guards over him. Then they divided the kingdom in two. The part west of the river Humber fell to Ingenius.

<a id="page-54"></a>[\[Giles 54\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-54) Peredurus got the rest, with all of Albany. Seven years later Ingenius died, and the whole country went to Peredurus. Once he held the sceptre, he governed kindly and modestly. People said he outdid the brothers who'd reigned earlier, and Elidurus was forgotten. But death spares no one, and it took Peredurus suddenly. At once Elidurus was brought out of prison and raised to the throne a third time. He spent all his days in goodness and justice, and when he died he left his successors an example of loyalty.

### A long line of kings follows Elidurus, among them Blegabred the singer

After Elidurus, Gorbonianus's son took the crown and followed his uncle in sense and wisdom. He gave up harsh rule and governed fairly and with mercy, never straying from the straight path. Next came Marganus II, born to Archgallo, who also learned from his family's example and kept the Britons at peace. His brother Enniaunus succeeded him, but he treated the people very differently. Five years into his reign, he was thrown off the throne. He'd chosen cruelty over justice, and it cost him his crown.

His cousin Idvallo, Ingenius's heir, was put in his place. Taught by what had happened to Enniaunus, he cared for right and fairness. Runo, Peredurus's son, succeeded him, and then Gerennus, Elidurus's.[^54-1] Catellus followed his father Gerennus; after Catellus, Coillus; after Coillus, Porrex II; after Porrex II, Cherin. Cherin left three heirs, Fulgenius, Edadus and Andragius, who reigned one after another. Next was Urianus, fathered by Andragius, then Eliud, Cledaucus, Clotenus and Gurgintius.

<a id="page-55"></a>[\[Giles 55\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-55) After them came Merianus, Bledudo, Cap, Oenus, Sisillius III and Blegabred. Blegabred outdid every earlier singer at melody and on all kinds of instruments, so people called him the god of entertainers.[^55-1] His brother Arthmail reigned after him, and after Arthmail, Eldol. Redon succeeded him, then Redechius, Samuil Penissel, Pir and Capoir. Next came Digueillus, Capoir's heir, a modest, sensible man in all he did, who did fairer justice among the people than anyone.

### Heli's son Lud rebuilds Trinovantum, which takes his name as London

Heli followed his father Digueillus and ruled forty years. He had three sons, Lud, Cassivellaunus and Nennius. Lud, the firstborn, became king when his father died. He was a great builder of cities. He rebuilt the walls of Trinovantum and ringed it with countless towers. He also ordered the citizens to put up their houses and halls there, so that no town in far-off kingdoms had finer palaces. He was eager for war and lavish with feasts.

Though he owned many cities, he loved this one above all and lived in it most of the year. So it was afterwards named Kaerlud, and then, as the name wore down, Kaerlondon. As languages changed over time, it became London, and after that Londres, in the mouths of the foreigners who landed and conquered the country.[^55-2] When he died at last, his body was buried in that city, by the gate still known after him as Parthlud in British and Ludgate in English.[^55-3] He had two sons, Androgeus and Tenvantius.

<a id="page-56"></a>[\[Giles 56\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-56) They were too young to rule, so their uncle Cassivellaunus was made king in their place. As soon as he was crowned, his generosity and courage made him so famous that word of him spread to distant lands. So it came about that the whole kingdom passed to him, and not to his nephews. But Cassivellaunus was loyal to them. He didn't want the young men left out, so he gave them a large share. Androgeus was made duke of Kent, with the city of Trinovantum, and Tenvantius got Cornwall to rule the same way. He himself, wearing the crown, ruled over them and all the island's princes.

## Part 4: Julius Caesar invades Britain, and its kings rule under Rome

### Julius Caesar sees Britain from Gaul and demands its tribute

Meanwhile, as the Roman histories tell, Julius Caesar conquered Gaul and came to the coast of the Ruteni,[^56-1] where he looked across at the island of Britain.[^56-2] He asked the men around him what country it was and who lived there. Gazing out over the ocean, he learned the names of the kingdom and its people.

"By Hercules," he said, "we Romans and the Britons come from the same stock, since we're both descended from the Trojans. After Troy fell, Aeneas was our first father, and theirs was Brutus, the son of Silvius, whose father was Ascanius, the child of Aeneas.

<a id="page-57"></a>[\[Giles 57\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-57) "But unless I'm wrong, they've sunk far below us. Living out in the ocean, beyond the edge of the world, they don't know what war is. It'll be easy to make them pay us tribute and obey the Roman people forever. First we'll order them to hand over the tax and submit to the senate like every other people, even if Rome has never set foot on their island. That way we won't spill the blood of our own family and shame the old nobility of our ancestor Priam."

He sent this demand to King Cassivellaunus in a letter. Cassivellaunus was outraged, and wrote back to him.

### Cassivellaunus writes back that the Britons will fight for their freedom

"Cassivellaunus, king of the Britons, to Julius Caesar. The greed of the Roman people is astonishing, Caesar. They thirst for every scrap of gold and silver there is. They can't even leave us alone out here among the dangers of the ocean, beyond the edge of the world, but they dare to want the wealth we've held in peace until now. That isn't enough for them either. We're to give up our freedom too, submit to them and be their slaves forever.

"You've asked for something shameful, Caesar. The Britons and the Romans share one noble blood that flows from Aeneas, and one chain of family shines between us that should join us in firm friendship. That's what you should ask of us, not slavery. We've learned to offer it freely, never to wear a slave's yoke. We're so used to being free that we don't know what it's like to obey a master. If the gods themselves tried to take our freedom from us, we'd struggle with all our strength to keep it. So be sure of this, Caesar: if you set foot on the island of Britain, as you've threatened, we'll fight for our liberty and our kingdom."

### Caesar lands in the Thames, and Nennius fights him hand to hand

<a id="page-58"></a>[\[Giles 58\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-58) When Julius Caesar read this, he got his fleet ready and waited for a fair wind, to carry out what he'd told Cassivellaunus. Once the breeze he wanted blew, he raised his sails and landed with his army at the mouth of the Thames. His ships had barely touched land when Cassivellaunus came against him with all his strength.

The king went to the town of Dorobellum,[^58-1] where he asked his nobles how to keep the enemy back. With him was Belinus, the commander of his army, whose advice ran the whole kingdom.[^58-2] His two nephews were there too, Androgeus, duke of Trinovantum, and Tenvantius of Cornwall, and three kings under him, Cridious of Albany, Guerthaeth of Gwynedd and Britael of Dyfed. These three had already stirred up the rest to fight. They advised going straight for Caesar's camp and driving him out before he took a city or a town. If he got inside the country's defenses, they said, it would be harder to be rid of him, since he'd know where he and his men could shelter.

They all agreed, and made for the shore where Julius had pitched his camp and tents. There both sides drew up their companies and closed, trading javelins and blows. Soldiers fell at once in both armies, struck deep. The ground was soaked with the blood of the dying, as if a sudden south wind had thrown back up the sea it swallowed. As the companies clashed, chance brought Nennius and Androgeus, with the men of Kent and of Trinovantum under them, up against the unit where Caesar himself fought. When they met, his own guard was nearly broken, since the Britons charged in close ranks.

<a id="page-59"></a>[\[Giles 59\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-59) In the tangle of fighting, Nennius got his chance to face Julius. He rushed at him, overjoyed to land even one blow on so great a man. Caesar saw him coming, caught the attack on his shield, and struck him on the helmet with his bare sword as hard as he could. He raised his arm to strike again and give him a deadly wound. But Nennius was ready for it and put up his own shield, where Caesar's blade stuck fast after it slid off the helmet with all its force. Then the press of troops drove the two apart before Caesar could pull it out.

So Nennius had Caesar's sword. He threw away his own and hurried at the enemy with it. The enemies he struck with it either lost their heads or were left too badly wounded to hope to live. At last Labienus, a tribune, came against him in his fury, and Nennius killed him at the first blow.[^59-1]

Most of the day had passed. The Britons kept charging in close ranks and attacking boldly. Yahweh favoured them, and they won.

Caesar fell back into his camp and his ships, his Romans cut to pieces. That night he gathered the rest and went aboard, glad to have the sea to guard him. His comrades talked him out of fighting any longer, and he took their advice and sailed back to Gaul.

### Cassivellaunus rewards his men, while his brother Nennius lies wounded

Cassivellaunus was overjoyed at the victory. He thanked the god, called together the men who'd won it with him, and gave each one rich gifts to match his courage. But he was miserable too, since his brother Nennius lay badly wounded and might not live.

<a id="page-60"></a>[\[Giles 60\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-60) Julius had given Nennius a wound in their fight that nothing could heal. Fifteen days after the battle, death crept up on him and he died. He was buried in Trinovantum, by the north gate. They gave him a king's funeral, and laid Caesar's sword in his coffin with him, the one he'd kept caught in his shield in the fight. Its name was Crocea Mors, Yellow Death, because nobody it wounded got away alive.[^60-1]

### Caesar goes back to Gaul and buys the rebellious Gauls' peace

When Julius turned tail and landed on the coast of Gaul, the Gauls plotted a revolt and threw off his rule. They thought he'd been weakened so badly that his forces were nothing to fear anymore. Everyone was telling the same story, that the whole sea was already swarming with the ships of Cassivellaunus, coming after Julius as he fled. That made the Gauls bolder, and they set about working out how to drive Caesar from their lands.

Julius got wind of it, and didn't want to risk an uncertain war with so fierce a people. Instead he opened his treasure and went to each of their nobles, to win them back one by one with gifts. He promised the common people their freedom, men who'd lost their inheritance their lands back, and even the slaves their liberty. Before, he'd raged like a lion and taken everything from them. Now he bleated humbly like a gentle lamb, and was glad he could give it all back. He kept up the flattery until he'd made peace with them all and won back the power he'd lost. Meanwhile, not a day went by that he didn't think about his flight and the Britons' victory.

### Cassivellaunus sets iron stakes in the Thames against Caesar's ships

<a id="page-61"></a>[\[Giles 61\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-61) Two years later Julius got ready to cross the ocean again and take revenge on Cassivellaunus. When Cassivellaunus found out, he fortified his cities everywhere, rebuilt broken walls and set armed knights at every port. Where Caesar would have to sail up the Thames to reach Trinovantum, he drove stakes of iron and lead, as thick as a man's thigh, into the riverbed below the water, so that Julius's ships would run onto them. Then he gathered all the island's young men and camped by the coast, waiting for the enemy.

### Caesar sails up the Thames a second time, and his ships strike the stakes

Once Julius had everything he needed, he put to sea with countless soldiers, burning to slaughter the people who'd beaten him. He'd surely have done it if he could have reached land with his fleet unharmed, but he couldn't. As he sailed up the Thames towards the city, his ships struck the stakes and were suddenly in danger. Soldiers drowned by the thousands as the water poured in through the holes in the hulls and swallowed them. When Caesar saw it, he swung his sails around hard and hurried to land. Those who'd lived through it barely scrambled ashore with him. Cassivellaunus watched from the bank. He was glad so many had drowned, but sorry the rest were safe. He gave his own troops the signal and charged the Romans.

<a id="page-62"></a>[\[Giles 62\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-62) The Romans had suffered in the Thames, but once on land they held off the Britons' charge bravely. With courage for a wall, they did plenty of damage, but they lost more than they killed. The drowning had cost them many, so fewer of them marched, while more Britons kept arriving every hour, until they had three times the Romans' numbers. So the Britons wore them down and won.

When Caesar saw he was beaten, he fled to his ships with a few men and reached the safety of the sea, as he'd hoped. With a good wind behind him, he raised his sails and made for the coast of the Morini. There he went into a tower he'd built at a place called Odnea before this second crossing to Britain.[^62-1] He didn't trust the Gauls, who were fickle and might turn on him again, as I've told you they did when he first ran from the Britons. That's why he'd built the tower as a refuge, so he could hold out against a rebellious people if they rose against him.

### At the feast, Androgeus's nephew and the king's nephew quarrel over a match

Cassivellaunus was overjoyed at his second victory. He ordered all the nobles of Britain to come to Trinovantum with their wives, to give the gods of their fathers the thanks they were owed for letting them beat so great a commander. They all came at once and offered sacrifices of many kinds, killing animals in their thousands. They sacrificed forty thousand cows there and a hundred thousand sheep. There were more birds of every sort than anyone could easily count, and thirty thousand deer, boar and other creatures of the woods besides.

<a id="page-63"></a>[\[Giles 63\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-63) Once they'd honoured their gods, they ate what was left, as people did at sacrifices. Then they spent the rest of the day and the night at all kinds of games. During them, two young men of high rank, the king's nephew and Duke Androgeus's, wrestled each other and quarrelled over who'd won. Hirelglas was the king's, and the other was Cuelinus.[^63-1] They traded insults, and then Cuelinus grabbed a sword and cut off Hirelglas's head. The court was thrown into an uproar, and word flew to Cassivellaunus.

Cassivellaunus was shaken by the killing. He ordered Androgeus to bring Cuelinus to him by midmorning, ready to take whatever sentence the nobles laid down, so that Hirelglas's death would be avenged if he'd been killed unjustly. Androgeus didn't trust the king's intentions. He answered that he had his own court, and any claim against his men should be settled there. If the king wanted justice for Cuelinus's act, he'd find it in Trinovantum, as the old custom was.

Cassivellaunus wasn't satisfied, so he threatened Androgeus, swearing to burn and plunder his lands unless he gave way. Androgeus was angry and put off doing as he asked. Cassivellaunus was just as furious, and hurried to lay waste the duke's country. Every day Androgeus went to him through his relatives and friends, begging him to give up his anger. When nothing he did could calm the king's rage, he turned over different ways to resist him. At last, with no other hope left, he decided to ask Caesar for help, and wrote him a letter.

"Androgeus, duke of Trinovantum, once wished Julius Caesar dead, and now wishes him well. I'm sorry I acted against you when you fought my king.

<a id="page-64"></a>[\[Giles 64\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-64) "If I'd kept out of it, you'd have beaten Cassivellaunus. Since his victory he's grown so proud that he's set on driving me out of his lands, though it was through me that he won. Is this how I should be repaid? I let him have my inheritance, and he's trying to take it from me. I put him back on his throne a second time, and he wants to throw me down. I gave him all of it by fighting against you. I swear by the gods in heaven I haven't earned his anger, unless refusing to hand my nephew over to an unjust death deserves it.

"So that you can see it more clearly, I'll tell you what happened. We were holding a feast to the gods of our fathers, in joy at our victory. After the sacrifices, our young men put on games against each other. Among the rest, our two nephews wrestled, following the others' lead. When mine won, the other flared up in unjust anger and rushed to strike him. My boy dodged the blow and caught the hand that held the sword, trying to wrench it away. Then the king's nephew fell on the point, was run through and died.[^64-1]

"When the king heard of it, he ordered me to give up the winner, to be put to death for murder. When I refused, he came into my lands with his whole army and has given me no end of trouble. So I beg your mercy and ask your help. Through you, let me have my rank back, and through me, you'll have Britain. Don't doubt me in this, since there's no betrayal in it. That's how people are: enemies become friends, and men who ran away go on to win."

### Caesar lands with Androgeus's help, and Androgeus attacks Cassivellaunus from behind

<a id="page-65"></a>[\[Giles 65\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-65) When Julius Caesar had read this, his advisers told him not to go to Britain on the duke's word alone, unless Androgeus sent hostages so he could land more safely. Androgeus gave him his son Scaeva at once, with thirty noble young men of his family. With them in his hands, Caesar felt safe. He called his companies back together, and with a fair wind landed at the port of Richborough.[^65-1]

Meanwhile Cassivellaunus had begun to besiege Trinovantum and lay waste the towns around it. But when he heard Julius had come, he left the siege and hurried to meet him. As he came into a valley near Canterbury, he saw the Roman army pitching its camp and tents there. Androgeus had led them to that spot so they could fall on the city by surprise.

The Romans spotted the Britons coming and armed quickly, setting their men out in companies. On the other side the Britons put on their armour too and formed up in their units. But Androgeus hid in a wood nearby with five thousand soldiers, ready to come to Caesar's help with a sudden, secret attack on Cassivellaunus and his army. As soon as the two sides met, they wasted no time hurling weapons and dealing deadly blows. The companies clashed, and blood ran everywhere. The wounded fell in both armies like leaves from the trees in the fall.

While they were fighting, Androgeus came out of the wood and attacked the main line of Cassivellaunus from behind, the one the whole battle hung on. The Romans' charge had just torn into it on one side, and now its own countrymen were crushing it on the other, so it couldn't hold its ground. Its troops scattered, ran and left the field.

<a id="page-66"></a>[\[Giles 66\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-66) Nearby stood a hill with a rocky top, thick with hazel bushes. Once he'd got the worst of it, Cassivellaunus fled there with his men. He reached the summit and defended himself bravely, killing the enemies who came after him. Androgeus's companies and the Romans had chased him, cutting down his followers as they ran. They climbed the slope and struck again and again, but couldn't win. Its rocks and steep top shielded the Britons, so they could charge down from the summit and slaughter their attackers.

So Caesar besieged the hill all through the night that had now come on, shutting off every way out. He meant to starve the king into surrender, since he couldn't beat him in battle. What a wonderful people the Britons were then! They'd twice put to flight the man who'd conquered the whole world. Beaten back themselves, they still held out against the man nobody on earth could resist, ready to die for their country and their freedom. That's why Lucan wrote of Caesar, in their praise:[^66-1]

He turned in terror and showed his back to the Britons he'd come looking for.

By the end of the second day, Cassivellaunus had nothing left to eat, and he was afraid he'd be starved into Caesar's prison. So he sent a message asking Androgeus to make peace for him with Julius, so that the honour of the family he came from wouldn't be wiped out by his capture. He also told Androgeus he'd done nothing to be wanted dead for, even if he'd caused the duke trouble.

When the messengers told him this, Androgeus said, "A prince who's as meek as a lamb in war and as fierce as a lion in peace doesn't deserve to be loved. By the gods of heaven and earth! My lord, who used to give me orders, is begging me now. Does he want me to patch things up for him now, and submit to Caesar, when it was Caesar who asked him for a truce before? He should have seen that the man who helped him drive so great a commander out of his kingdom could bring him back again. I shouldn't have been treated so unfairly, since I could do him that service then and this harm now.

<a id="page-67"></a>[\[Giles 67\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-67) "A lord who insults or wrongs the comrades who win his battles is a fool. No victory is the commander's alone. It's won by the men who shed their blood fighting for him. Still, I'll make peace for him if I can, since he's paid in full for what he did to me, now that he's begging me to forgive him."

### Androgeus begs Caesar to spare Cassivellaunus, and Britain pays tribute

Then Androgeus hurried to Julius, threw his arms around his knees and said, "You've had revenge enough on Cassivellaunus. Have mercy on him. What more is there to do, except have him submit and pay tribute to Rome?"

Caesar said nothing, so Androgeus spoke again. "All I promised you, Caesar, was to help bring Britain under you once Cassivellaunus was defeated. Now he is, and Britain is yours with my help. What more do I owe you? The maker of all things forbid that I should ever let my lord be put in prison or in chains while he begs my mercy and makes amends for the wrong he did me. It won't be easy to kill Cassivellaunus while I'm alive, and I won't be ashamed to help him if you don't take my advice."

Julius was afraid of Androgeus, and relented. He made peace with Cassivellaunus, on condition that Britain pay a yearly tribute of three thousand pounds of silver. After that Julius and Cassivellaunus became friends and gave each other gifts. Caesar spent the winter in Britain and crossed back to Gaul when spring came. Some time later he gathered soldiers of every kind from all over and marched on Rome against Pompey.[^67-1]

### Tenvantius reigns, then Kymbelinus, and Christ is born

<a id="page-68"></a>[\[Giles 68\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-68) Seven years after that, Cassivellaunus died, and he was buried in York. Tenvantius, duke of Cornwall and Androgeus's brother, succeeded him, since Androgeus had gone to Rome with Caesar. Once crowned, Tenvantius ruled the kingdom with care. He was a warlike man, and kept justice strong. After him his son Kymbelinus rose to the throne, a brave knight brought up by Augustus Caesar.[^68-1] He'd grown so friendly with the Romans that, though he could have held back their tribute, he paid it of his own free will. In those days our lord Jesus Christ was born, whose precious blood bought back the human race, chained until then by the devils.

### Guiderius refuses Rome's tribute, and Claudius lands at Portchester

Kymbelinus ruled Britain ten years and fathered two sons, the firstborn named Guiderius and the other Arvirargus.[^68-2] When his life's days were done, he left the kingdom to Guiderius. Guiderius refused the Romans the tribute they demanded, so Claudius, who'd become emperor, came over. With him was the commander of his army, Hamo, whose advice decided what battles to fight.[^68-3] They landed at the city of Portchester and began walling up its gates and keeping the citizens from getting out. They meant to starve them into surrender, or else kill them without mercy.

> **In the Arthur story:** Arvirargus is the king of the Glastonbury legend. The monks of Glastonbury told from the 1200s on how Joseph of Arimathea came to Britain after Christ's death, and how a pagan king, Arviragus, gave his companions the island where they built the first church, the place where the monks said they'd found Arthur's grave.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

### Hamo puts on British armour to reach Guiderius, and Arvirargus takes up the fight

<a id="page-69"></a>[\[Giles 69\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-69) When word spread that the emperor Claudius had come, Guiderius gathered every armed knight in the kingdom and marched on the Roman army. He joined battle and attacked the enemy fiercely, killing more with his own sword than most of his men did. Claudius was already making for his ships, and the Romans were nearly scattered. Then cunning Hamo threw off his own armour and put on British gear, and fought against his own side as if he were British. He urged the Britons to give chase, promising them a quick victory.

He'd learned their language and their ways, since he'd been brought up in Rome among British hostages. Little by little he worked his way close to the king, and when he found an opening, he cut him down before Guiderius suspected a thing. Then he slipped away through the enemy's ranks and got back to his own side with his shameful victory.

When Guiderius's brother Arvirargus saw him dead, he took off his own gear at once and put on the king's. He went up and down the field urging the Britons to stand firm, as if he were Guiderius himself. Not knowing their king had fallen, they did as he urged, held out, fought on and killed plenty. In the end the Romans broke in two and fled the field in disgrace. Claudius made for the safety of his fleet, and Hamo for the woods, since he had no room to reach the ships.

Arvirargus thought Claudius was running with Hamo, and hurried after him. He followed from place to place until he caught them on the shore, at the place now called Southampton after this same Hamo.[^69-1] There was a harbour there fit for vessels to land, and merchant ships lay at anchor in it. Hamo was trying to get aboard them when Arvirargus came on him unexpectedly and killed him on the spot.

<a id="page-70"></a>[\[Giles 70\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-70) It's been called Hamo's Port from then to this day.

### Claudius besieges Arvirargus in Winchester and offers him his daughter

Meanwhile Claudius gathered his men again and attacked the place I've named, which was Kaerperis then and is now Portchester. He soon tore down its walls, beat the citizens into submission and went after Arvirargus, who'd already gone into Winchester. He laid siege to the city and tried to take it with all kinds of war machines. When Arvirargus saw he was trapped, he drew up his companies, opened the gates and came out to fight.

He was about to charge when Claudius sent messengers to him to ask for peace. Claudius feared the king's daring and the Britons' strength. He'd rather win them over with good sense than risk a battle he might lose. So he offered terms, and promised the king his daughter, if only he'd recognise the kingdom of Britain as under Roman rule. The older men urged Arvirargus to drop the war and accept Claudius's promises. It was no shame, they said, to be under the Romans, since they ruled the whole world. Persuaded by this and much more, he took their advice and submitted to Claudius. The emperor soon sent to Rome for his daughter, and with Arvirargus's help brought Orkney and the other nearby islands under his rule.

### Arvirargus marries Venissa, and Claudius founds Gloucester

<a id="page-71"></a>[\[Giles 71\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-71) When the winter was over, the envoys came back with the girl and handed her to her father. Her name was Venissa,[^71-1] and she was so beautiful that everyone who saw her was amazed. Once she was his lawful wife, Arvirargus loved her so passionately that he put her above everything else. Wanting to make the place where he'd married her famous, he urged Claudius to build a city there that would keep the memory of so great a wedding for all time to come.

Claudius agreed, and ordered one built that has been called Kaerglou after him to this day, that is, Gloucester.[^71-2] It stands on the bank of the Severn, on the border of Dyfed and Loegria. Some say, though, that it took its name from Duke Gloius, Claudius's son, born there, who ruled Dyfed after Arvirargus. Once it was built and Britain at peace, Claudius went back to Rome and left Arvirargus to rule the islands. At that time the apostle Peter founded the church of Antioch, then came to Rome and was bishop there.[^71-3] He sent Mark the Evangelist to Egypt to preach the gospel he'd written.[^71-4]

### Arvirargus grows proud and stops paying Rome, and Vespasian is sent

Once Claudius had gone, Arvirargus began to show good sense and courage. He rebuilt cities and towns and ruled his people so fairly that kings far away feared him. But pride came after, and he looked down on the power of Rome.

<a id="page-72"></a>[\[Giles 72\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-72) He refused to obey the senate any longer and claimed everything for himself. When the news reached Rome, Claudius sent Vespasian to make peace with Arvirargus or bring him back under Rome.[^72-1] Vespasian was about to land at Richborough when Arvirargus met him and kept him out of the harbour. He'd brought so many armed men that the Romans were terrified and didn't dare come ashore for fear of his attack. So Vespasian pulled back, turned his sails and landed on the coast at Totnes. From there he went to besiege Kaerpenhuelgoit, which is called Exeter.

He'd besieged it seven days when Arvirargus came up with his army and gave battle. That day both armies were badly cut up, but neither won. The next morning Queen Venissa made peace between the two leaders, and they sent their men over to Ireland. When the winter was over, Vespasian went back to Rome, and Arvirargus stayed in Britain.

As he grew old, he came to love the senate. He ruled his kingdom in peace and quiet. He confirmed the laws of the old tradition and made new ones, and gave great gifts to every man of worth. His fame spread through all of Europe. The Romans loved and feared him, so that in Rome people talked of him more than of any other ruler. That's why Juvenal, in his book, tells of a blind man who spoke to Nero about a turbot someone caught:[^72-2]

You'll take some king, or Arvirargus\
will tumble from his British chariot.

Nobody was fiercer in war or gentler in peace, more cheerful, or freer with gifts. When his days were done, Arvirargus was buried in Gloucester, in a temple he'd built and dedicated in Claudius's honour.

### Marius fights Rodric's Picts and gives them Caithness

<a id="page-73"></a>[\[Giles 73\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-73) His son Marius succeeded him, a man of remarkable good sense and wisdom. During his reign a king of the Picts named Rodric came from Scythia with a great fleet and landed in Albany, the north of Britain, and began laying it waste. Marius gathered his people and marched against him. He fought him, killed him and won. Then he raised a stone to mark his victory, in the region later called Westmorland after him, and the words carved on it keep his memory to this day.[^73-1]

With Rodric dead, Marius gave the beaten people who'd come with him a part of Albany to live in, named Caithness. It was deserted, and no one had lived there or farmed it for a long time. The Picts had no women with them, and asked the Britons for their daughters and nieces. But the Britons wouldn't marry their girls to such people. Turned down, the Picts crossed to Ireland and found wives there, and the children born to them made their numbers grow. But enough of this, since I don't mean to write the history of the Picts, or of the Scots, who come from them and the Irish.[^73-2]

Once Marius had brought the whole island into perfect peace, he set out to win the love of the Roman people, paying the tribute they demanded. Following his father's example, he also kept justice, law and order and every decent thing throughout his kingdom.

### Coillus, raised in Rome, pays its tribute and keeps the peace

<a id="page-74"></a>[\[Giles 74\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-74) When Marius's life had run its course, his son Coillus took the throne.[^74-1] He'd been brought up in Rome from childhood, had learned the Romans' ways and become their close friend. He paid them tribute too, and shied away from opposing them, since he saw the whole world under them and their power greater than any land or region's. By paying what was asked, he held what he had in peace. No king showed the nobles more respect, because he either left them alone or kept giving them gifts.

### Lucius asks Pope Eleutherius for baptism, and Britain becomes Christian

Coillus had one son, named Lucius. When his father died and Lucius was crowned, he followed his father's goodness in everything, and people said he was Coillus all over again. Wanting his end to be better than his beginning, he sent letters to Pope Eleutherius asking to receive Christianity from him.[^74-2] The miracles that Christ's young soldiers were working among the nations had opened his mind. He longed for the true religion, and got what he'd asked for.

Learning how devout Lucius was, the blessed pope sent him two very holy teachers, Fagan and Deruvian.[^74-3] They preached that Yahweh's word had become flesh, and baptised the king into the faith of Christ. People of every nation came running at once to follow suit. Washed clean in the same font, they were given back the kingdom of heaven.

<a id="page-75"></a>[\[Giles 75\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-75) Once the blessed teachers had wiped out paganism from nearly the whole island, they dedicated the temples founded in honour of many gods to Yahweh alone and its saints, and filled them with communities of clergy. Britain then had twenty-eight high priests, with three chiefs over them, whose authority the other judges of morals and the temple servants were under.[^75-1] On the pope's orders, the teachers rescued these too from idolatry. In place of each of the twenty-eight they put a bishop, and in place of each of the three, an archbishop.

The three had had their seats in the noblest cities, London, York and the City of the Legions, whose old walls and buildings show it stood on the river Usk, in Glamorgan.[^75-2] Once these were rid of superstition, the bishops were put under them, each with its own dioceses. Deira and Albany, which the great Humber cuts off from Loegria, were under York's archbishop. Loegria and Cornwall were under London's. The Severn divides those two from Cambria, that is, Wales, which was under the City of the Legions.

### Fagan and Deruvian return to Rome, and the pope confirms what they did

Once everything was in order, the two bishops went back to Rome, and had the most blessed pope confirm what they'd done. Then they came back to Britain with many others, whose teaching soon made the Britons strong in the faith of Christ. Their names and what they did can be found in the book Gildas wrote about the victory of Aurelius Ambrosius. Since he set it all out so clearly, there was no need for me to tell it again in a poorer style.[^75-3]

> **In the Arthur story:** Aurelius Ambrosius is Arthur's uncle in the legend. Gildas names him Ambrosius Aurelianus, the leader of Roman family who rallied the Britons against the Saxons. Later in this book Geoffrey makes him King Constantine's son and Uther's older brother, who comes home from Brittany to take back his father's throne, and Wace and Layamon follow him. Robert de Boron's *Merlin* gives him the byname Uther takes after him.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

## Part 5: Constantine is born in Britain, and Maximianus takes its army to Brittany

### Lucius gives the temples' lands to the churches, and he has no heir

<a id="page-76"></a>[\[Giles 76\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-76) Meanwhile the glorious King Lucius saw the true faith honoured throughout his kingdom, and he was overjoyed.[^76-1] He turned the lands and estates the idols' temples had held to a better use, and let the churches of the faithful keep them. Since he felt he owed them still more, he added bigger fields and houses to them. He also freed them from every tax and duty. In the middle of this and the rest of his work, he died in the city of Gloucester. He was buried with honour in its chief church, a hundred and fifty-six years after Christ was born.[^76-2] He'd had no child to succeed him, so after his death the Britons fell out among themselves, and Rome's power grew weak.

### Rome sends Severus, who conquers part of Britain and fights Fulgenius

When the news reached Rome, the senate sent the senator Severus with two legions to bring the country back under Roman rule. As soon as he landed, he attacked the Britons and made part of them submit.

<a id="page-77"></a>[\[Giles 77\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-77) The rest he couldn't conquer, so he harassed them with brutal attacks, until he drove them out beyond Deira into Albany. But led by Fulgenius, they fought back with all their strength. Again and again they cut down huge numbers of the Romans and of the Britons on Rome's side.[^77-1] Fulgenius hired every island people he could find to help him, and so he often came home victorious. The emperor took his raids hard, and ordered a wall built between Deira and Albany to keep him from coming any closer. They shared the cost and ran it from one sea to the other, and for a long time afterwards it kept enemies out.[^77-2]

When Fulgenius couldn't hold out any longer, he sailed to Scythia, to win back his rank with the Picts' help. There he raised all the country's young men and came back to Britain with a huge fleet, and laid siege to York. As the news spread among the other peoples, most of the Britons deserted Severus and went over to Fulgenius. Even so, Severus didn't give up. He called together the Romans and the Britons who'd stayed loyal, marched to the siege and fought Fulgenius. The fighting was fierce. Severus was killed with many of his men, and Fulgenius was badly wounded and died of it. Severus was buried at York, which his legions held.[^77-3]

He left two sons, Bassianus and Geta. Geta's mother was Roman, and Bassianus's was British.[^77-4] After their father's death, the Romans made Geta king, favoring him because he was Roman on both sides. The Britons wouldn't have him, and chose Bassianus, who was theirs through his mother's blood. So the brothers fought it out. Geta was killed, and Bassianus took the kingdom.

### Carausius talks the senate into giving him a fleet and makes himself king

<a id="page-78"></a>[\[Giles 78\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-78) At that time there was a young man in Britain named Carausius, born of the lowest class. He'd proved his courage in many battles, and he went to Rome to ask the senate for permission to guard Britain's coasts with ships against barbarian raids. If they let him, he promised, he'd win so much that he'd do more for Rome than if they gave him the kingdom of Britain. He fooled the senate with his promises and got what he'd asked for, and came back to Britain with letters under the senate's seal.

Soon he'd brought ships together and taken on a strong force of young men. He put to sea and sailed around every coast of the kingdom, throwing the people into an uproar. Meanwhile he landed on the neighbouring islands, wasting their fields, tearing down their cities and towns and robbing the people of all they had. While he did this, everyone greedy for other men's goods flocked to him, until in a short time he had an army no neighbouring ruler could have stood against.

His success went to his head. He told the Britons to make him their king, and he'd kill the Romans, wipe them out and free the whole island from the foreigners. They did, and at once he fought Bassianus, killed him and took over the government. The Picts had betrayed Bassianus. His mother's brother, Duke Fulgenius, had brought them into Britain, and they should have helped him, but Carausius's promises and gifts bought them. In the battle itself they turned away from Bassianus and charged his own comrades. The rest were stunned and couldn't tell friend from enemy, and they quickly scattered. So the victory went to Carausius.

<a id="page-79"></a>[\[Giles 79\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-79) Once he'd won, he gave the Picts land to live on in Albany, where they lived mixed with the Britons from then on.

### Rome sends Allectus against Carausius, and the Britons raise Asclepiodotus against Allectus

When Rome heard that Carausius had taken the kingdom, the senate sent its envoy Allectus with three legions to kill the tyrant and bring Britain back under Roman rule. He fought Carausius as soon as he landed, killed him and took the throne.[^79-1] Then he made the Britons suffer terribly, because they'd abandoned Rome and joined Carausius. The Britons couldn't bear it. They made Asclepiodotus, duke of Cornwall, their king. Together they went after Allectus and dared him to come out and fight.

Allectus was in London then, holding a feast for the gods of his fathers. When he learned Asclepiodotus was coming, he left the sacrifice, marched out against him with all his strength and fought a fierce battle. But Asclepiodotus got the upper hand. He scattered Allectus's companies and put them to flight, and in the chase he killed many thousands, King Allectus among them.

After this victory, Allectus's colleague Livius Gallus called the Romans who were left into the city. He shut the gates and manned the towers and the other defenses, meaning to hold out against Asclepiodotus that way, or at least escape the death that hung over him. When Asclepiodotus saw it, he quickly surrounded the place. He sent messengers to every duke in Britain. He'd killed Allectus and many of his soldiers, he told them. Now he had Gallus and the rest of the Romans under siege in London. He begged each of them to hurry to his help.

<a id="page-80"></a>[\[Giles 80\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-80) It would be easy, Asclepiodotus said, to wipe the Romans out of Britain, if they all attacked together while the enemy was shut in. At his call came the men of Dyfed, Gwynedd, Deira and Albany, and all the rest of the Britons. When they'd all gathered around him, he had countless siege machines made, to bring down the city's walls. Every brave and bold man obeyed at once and attacked the city fiercely. They soon broke through, forced their way in and began slaughtering the Romans.

When the Romans saw they were being cut down without letup, they urged Gallus to surrender himself and them, and beg Asclepiodotus to let them leave alive. All but a single legion had already been killed. Gallus agreed and handed himself and his soldiers over to Asclepiodotus. But while Asclepiodotus was thinking about sparing him, the men of Gwynedd came, formed up and beheaded them all in one day. They did it inside the city, beside a stream that was later named after Gallus, in both British and English.[^80-1]

### Asclepiodotus reigns ten years, and Diocletian's persecution reaches Britain

With the Romans beaten, Asclepiodotus took the kingdom's crown and set it on his own head, with the people's consent. For ten years he ruled the country in peace and true justice. He kept robbers and bandits in check.

During his reign the emperor Diocletian's persecution began. It nearly wiped out Christianity in the whole island, which had stayed unbroken and pure since King Lucius's time. Maximian Herculius, the commander of that tyrant's army, had taken charge,[^80-2] and on his orders every church was torn down, and all the holy books that could be found were burned in the town squares.

<a id="page-81"></a>[\[Giles 81\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-81) The chosen priests were slaughtered with the faithful in their care, and they hurried in crowds, each trying to be first, to the lovely kingdoms of heaven, as if going home. So Yahweh showed us its mercy.[^81-1] In the time of persecution it lit the brightest lamps of holy martyrs, as a free gift, so the British people wouldn't be completely darkened by the thick gloom of black night. Their graves and the places where they suffered would now fire a great love of Yahweh in those who saw them, if the barbarians' bitter partition hadn't taken them from the Britons.[^81-2]

Among the men and women who stood firm with great courage in Christ's battle line were Alban of Verulamium, and Julius and Aaron, citizens of the City of the Legions. Out of love, Alban hid his confessor Amphibalus in his own house when the men hunting him were about to catch him, and then gave himself up to die.[^81-3] In this he imitated Christ, who lays down his life for his sheep.[^81-4] The other two were torn apart in ways unheard of, and went without delay, with the trophy of martyrdom, to the fine gates of Jerusalem.[^81-5]

### Coel of Colchester rises against Asclepiodotus, and Rome sends Constantius

Meanwhile Coel, duke of Kaercolvin, that is, Colchester, rose against King Asclepiodotus.[^81-6] He fought him, killed him and put on the crown himself.[^81-7] When the senate heard, they were glad the king was dead, since he'd made trouble for Rome's power at every turn. They also remembered what losing the kingdom had cost them. So they sent the senator Constantius, who'd conquered Spain for them, a wise and bold man who'd worked harder than anyone to make Rome greater.

<a id="page-82"></a>[\[Giles 82\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-82) When Coel, the Britons' duke, heard Constantius was coming, he was afraid to fight him, since word had it no king could stand against him. So as soon as Constantius landed on the island, Coel sent envoys to him to ask for peace and promise to submit, on condition that he keep the kingdom of Britain and pay Rome nothing beyond the usual tribute. Constantius agreed. Hostages were handed over, and the treaty was confirmed. A month later Coel fell gravely ill, and he died within eight days.

Once he was dead, Constantius took the crown himself, and Coel's daughter, named Helena, as his wife.[^82-1] She was more beautiful than any girl in the land, and no woman anywhere was thought more skilled in music or learning. Her father had no other child to take the throne, so he'd worked to teach her well enough to rule after him. After the wedding she gave Constantius a son, and he named him Constantine.

> **In the Arthur story:** Constantine is part of why Arthur claims Rome. In Part 9, when Rome demands tribute, Arthur answers that Constantine, Helena's son, and after him Maximianus, both of his family, wore Britain's crown and then took Rome's throne, so Rome owes Britain, not the other way round. Malory's Arthur makes the same claim at the start of his war on Rome. Arthur's own grandfather, in Part 6, is another Constantine.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

Eleven years later Constantius died at York, and left the kingdom to Constantine.[^82-2] Once on the throne, within a few years the young king showed great courage and was as fierce as a lion, and he kept justice among his people. He stopped robbers' greed, put down cruel lords and worked to bring back peace everywhere.

### Romans driven out by Maxentius ask Constantine for help

At that time there was a tyrant in Rome named Maxentius. He was trying to strip every noble and decent citizen of their inheritance, and was crushing Rome under the worst kind of rule.

<a id="page-83"></a>[\[Giles 83\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-83) As things got worse, those he'd driven out fled to Constantine in Britain, and he welcomed them with honour. Once many had come to him, they turned him against Maxentius, and kept putting it to him:

"How long will you leave us to suffer this disaster and exile, Constantine? Why do you wait to bring us home? You're the only one of our people who can give back what we've lost, by driving out Maxentius. What ruler can compare with the king of Britain, in the strength of his tough soldiers or his wealth of gold and silver? We beg you, give us back our property, give us back our wives and children. March on Rome with us and your army."

### Constantine takes Rome, and Octavius rises against the Romans in Britain

Stirred by this and more, Constantine marched on Rome and conquered it. Later he ruled the whole world.[^83-1] He brought Helena's three uncles with him, Leolinus, Trahern and Marius, and made them senators.[^83-2] Meanwhile Octavius, duke of the Gewisse, rose against the Roman governors who'd been left in charge of the island, killed them and took the throne.[^83-3]

When Constantine heard, he sent Helena's uncle Trahern with three legions to win Britain back for Rome. Trahern landed on the shore near Portchester, which the Britons call Kaerperis, attacked it and captured it within two days. When word spread through all the peoples, King Octavius called up every armed man on the island. He met Trahern not far from Winchester, in a field known in British as Maisuria, and beat him.

<a id="page-84"></a>[\[Giles 84\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-84) Trahern got his cut-up soldiers back to his ships, sailed to Albany and set about laying it waste. When King Octavius heard, he brought his companies together again, followed him and fought him in the region later called Westmorland, but this time he lost and fled. Seeing he'd won, Trahern chased Octavius. He gave him no rest until he'd taken his cities and his crown.

Octavius, sick with worry at losing his kingdom, sailed to Norway to get help from King Gombertus. Before he went, he'd ordered his friends to work with all their might to kill Trahern. The earl of a free town, who loved Octavius more than anyone, lost no time obeying.[^84-1] One day he hid with a hundred men in a wooded valley on the road Trahern was taking out of London. They fell on Trahern as he went by and killed him among his companions.

When Octavius heard, he came back to Britain, scattered the Romans and won back his throne. In a short time he'd gained such strength and so much gold and silver that he feared no one. From then on he held the kingdom of Britain happily until the days of Gratian and Valentinian.

### Octavius has only a daughter, and his advisers argue over who should reign

At last Octavius was worn out by old age.[^84-2] Wanting to settle his people's future, he asked his advisers which of his family they'd want made king after he died. He had only one daughter, and no son to leave the country to. Some urged him to marry her, with the kingdom, to one of the Roman nobles, so they'd have peace that would hold.

<a id="page-85"></a>[\[Giles 85\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-85) Others thought his nephew Conan Meriadoc should be crowned, and the girl married to a prince from abroad, with gold and silver for her dowry. While they argued, Caradocus, duke of Cornwall, came forward. He advised them to invite the senator Maximianus and give him the princess with the throne, so they'd have lasting peace.[^85-1] Maximianus's father was British, since Leolinus, Constantine's uncle, the one I mentioned earlier, had fathered him.[^85-2] But by his mother and his birth he was Roman, and he had royal blood on both sides. That was why Caradocus promised a steady peace. He knew Maximianus had a claim to Britain both as one of the emperors' family and as a son of the Britons.

> **In the Arthur story:** Conan Meriadoc is the first king Geoffrey gives Brittany, and Brittany stays close to Arthur. In Part 9, its king Hoel, Arthur's nephew, crosses the sea to help him against the Saxons and fights beside him against Rome. In the Tristan romances and Malory, Tristan marries his second Isolde, Isolde of the White Hands, in Brittany, and Breton storytellers carried tales of Arthur into France.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

When the duke of the Cornish gave this advice, the king's nephew Conan, who longed with all his heart for the throne, was furious, and threw the whole court into an uproar over it. But Caradocus wouldn't give up his plan. He sent his son Mauricius to Rome to tell Maximianus about it. Mauricius was tall, handsome, brave and bold. Whatever he said, he'd prove in single combat if anyone said otherwise. When he came to Maximianus, he was welcomed properly and honoured above his fellow soldiers.

At that time there was great unrest between Maximianus and the two emperors, Gratian and his brother Valentinian, because they'd refused him the third of the empire he'd asked for. So when Mauricius saw how badly they'd treated Maximianus, he said to him:

"Why are you afraid of Gratian, Maximianus, when there's a way open for you to take his throne? Come with me to the island of Britain, and you'll wear its crown. King Octavius is weighed down by age and sickness, and wants nothing but to find someone like you to give his kingdom to, with his daughter. He has no son, and he's asked his nobles who should marry his only child and rule after him. To do as he wished, the lords decided the crown and the girl should be yours, and they've sent me to tell you.

<a id="page-86"></a>[\[Giles 86\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-86) "So come with me and see this through. You'll be able to go back to Rome with Britain's gold and silver, and the many warlike soldiers who live there. You can drive out the emperors and conquer Rome. That's what your cousin Constantine did, and several more of our kings who won the empire."

### Maximianus lands at Southampton, and Conan's army meets him there

Maximianus agreed and set out for Britain. On the way he took the cities of the Franks, piling up gold and silver as he went and recruiting soldiers from all sides. Then he put to sea, and with a fair wind he landed at Hamo's Port. When the king heard, he was terrified, thinking an enemy army had come. He called his nephew Conan, and told him to call up every armed man on the island and march against them. So Conan raised all the kingdom's young men and came to Hamo's Port, where Maximianus had pitched his tents.

When Maximianus learned such a crowd was coming, he was in agony, not knowing what to do. He had fewer companies with him, and feared both the size and the daring of the force against him, since he had no hope of peace. He called together the older men and Mauricius, and told them to say what should be done. Mauricius answered:

"We mustn't fight so many warlike soldiers. We didn't come here to win Britain by war. We have to ask for peace and permission to stay, until we know the king's mind. Let's say the emperors sent us, and that we're bringing their orders to Octavius, and calm these people with warm words."

<a id="page-87"></a>[\[Giles 87\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-87) Everyone agreed. Mauricius took twelve grey-haired nobles, wiser than the rest, with olive branches in their right hands, and went to meet Conan. When the Britons saw these respected old men holding up the sign of peace, they stood up for them and made way, so the envoys could reach the leader more easily. Standing in front of Conan Meriadoc, they greeted him on behalf of the emperors and the senate. They said Maximianus had been sent to King Octavius, to bring him orders from Gratian and Valentinian.

Conan answered, "Then why does such a crowd follow him? That doesn't look like envoys. It looks like an enemy arriving, set on doing harm."

Mauricius said, "It wouldn't be right for so great a man to travel without the honour of companions, especially since Rome's power and what his ancestors did make many kings hate him. If he travelled with only a few men, Rome's enemies might kill him. He comes in peace and asks for it in return, and what he's done should make you believe it. Ever since we landed, we've behaved so as to harm nobody. We've paid our way like friends, buying what we needed, and taken nothing from anyone by force."

Conan couldn't decide whether to make peace or fight. Then Caradocus, duke of Cornwall, came up, and the other nobles too, and talked him out of attacking after such a request. Though he'd rather have fought, he laid down his arms and agreed to a truce. He brought Maximianus to Octavius in London, and told him everything in order.

### Caradocus urges Octavius to give his daughter and kingdom to Maximianus

<a id="page-88"></a>[\[Giles 88\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-88) Then Caradocus, duke of Cornwall, took his son Mauricius with him, had everyone standing around sent away, and spoke to the king.

"What your most loyal men have long wanted has come to you, as Yahweh arranged it.

"You told your nobles to advise you what to do about your daughter and your kingdom, since at your age you can't rule your people much longer. Some thought the crown should go to your nephew Conan, and the girl be married well somewhere else. They were afraid our people would be wiped out if a prince who spoke another language took the throne. Others would give the kingdom to her and to some noble who talks as we do, to succeed you when you die. But most urged you to send for a man of the emperors' family and give her to him with the crown. They promised a firm, lasting peace would come of it, with Rome's power to protect them.

"Now Yahweh has seen fit to bring you this young man, a Roman with the Britons' royal blood too.

"On my advice, don't put off marrying the girl to him. Even if you refused, what right would you have against his claim to Britain? He's Constantine's cousin and our King Coel's nephew, and nobody can deny that Coel's daughter Helena held this kingdom by right of inheritance."

When Caradocus had said this, Octavius agreed, and with everyone's consent gave Maximianus Britain and the princess on the spot. When Conan Meriadoc saw it, he was angrier than words can say. He withdrew to Albany and set about raising an army to harass Maximianus. Once he had enough men, he crossed the Humber and laid waste every region on both sides of it.

<a id="page-89"></a>[\[Giles 89\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-89) When Maximianus heard, he called up all his forces, marched out against Conan, beat him and returned in triumph. But Conan didn't give up. He brought his men together again and went on wrecking the provinces. So Maximianus took the field again, and they fought on, now one winning and now the other. At last, with great harm done on both sides, their friends made peace between them.

### Maximianus grows proud, invades Armorica and promises it to Conan

Five years later Maximianus grew proud, because of the endless gold and silver that flowed to him daily. He made ready a huge fleet and gathered every armed man in Britain. The kingdom of Britain wasn't enough for him; he wanted to conquer Gaul as well. So he crossed the sea and went first to Armorica, which is now called Brittany, and began making war on the Gauls who lived there. The Gauls came against him under Inbaltus and fought, but most of their army was cut up, and they fled. Duke Inbaltus had fallen, with fifteen thousand armed men who'd come from all over the land.

Having done such slaughter, Maximianus was overjoyed, knowing the country would fall easily now that so many were dead. He called Conan aside, away from the ranks, and said with a little smile:

"We've conquered one of the strongest kingdoms of Gaul, and we can hope for the rest. Let's hurry to take the cities and towns, before word of this danger flies into farther Gaul and calls every people to arms. If we can hold this one, I've no doubt we'll bring all Gaul under our power.

<a id="page-90"></a>[\[Giles 90\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-90) "So don't be sorry you let me have the kingdom of the island of Britain, though you'd hoped it would be yours. Whatever you lost there, I'll make up to you here. I'll make you its king, and it'll be a second Britain. We'll drive out the people living in it and fill it with our own. The land is rich in grain, its rivers are full of fish, its woods are lovely and its pastures pleasant everywhere. In my opinion there's no country anywhere more delightful."

At this Conan bowed his head and thanked him, and promised to serve him loyally as long as he lived.

### The Britons take Rennes and empty the land of its men

After this they called their companies together, marched on Rennes and took it the same day. The citizens had heard how brutal the Britons were and what had happened to the dead. They fled in a hurry, leaving their wives and children behind. Others did the same throughout the country, so the way lay open for the Britons. Wherever they went in, they killed all the males and spared only the women. At last, with every region emptied of its people, they put soldiers from Britain in the cities and towns. They also built forts on high ground here and there.

Word of Maximianus's cruelty spread through the rest of Gaul's provinces, and the dukes and princes were all terrified, until they had no hope left but in vows to the gods. They fled from every district into the cities and towns, and anywhere that offered safety. Seeing he was feared, Maximianus grew bolder still, and hurried to swell his army with lavish gifts. He took on anyone he knew was good at grabbing other men's goods, and never stopped making them rich, now with gold, now with silver, now with other presents.

### Maximianus settles Britons in Armorica and conquers Gaul and Germany

<a id="page-91"></a>[\[Giles 91\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-91) Then Maximianus gathered as great a host as he thought would be enough to conquer all Gaul. But he put off carrying his cruelty farther for a while, until he'd settled the kingdom he'd taken and filled it with Britons. He gave orders that a hundred thousand ordinary people from the island of Britain be brought to him, and thirty thousand soldiers besides, to guard them from enemy attack in the country where they'd live. When all this was done, he spread them all over Armorica, made it a second Britain and gave it to Conan Meriadoc.[^91-1]

He himself went on with the rest of his comrades into farther Gaul, and conquered it after hard fighting, and all Germany too, winning every battle. He set up the throne of his empire at Trier, and raged so against the two emperors, Gratian and Valentinian, that he killed one and drove the other out of the city of Rome.[^91-2]

### Conan holds Armorica and sends to Britain for wives for his men

Meanwhile the Gauls and the people of Aquitaine kept attacking Conan and the Britons in Armorica. He held them off, paid them back in kind and bravely defended the country he'd been given. Once the victory was his, he wanted to give his comrades wives, so they'd have heirs to hold the land forever.

<a id="page-92"></a>[\[Giles 92\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-92) So they'd never mix with the Gauls, he decided women should come from the island of Britain to marry them. He sent messengers to Britain, to Dionotus, king of Cornwall, who'd succeeded his brother Caradocus on the throne, to ask him to see to it. Dionotus was noble and very powerful, and Maximianus had left Britain in his care while he was busy with his wars.
