Dionotus had a daughter of wonderful beauty named Ursula, and Conan wanted her more than anything.[^92-1]

### Dionotus gathers eleven thousand noble daughters, and the fleet sails for Armorica

When Dionotus got Conan's message, he was eager to do as Conan asked. He gathered eleven thousand girls of noble birth from all over the provinces, and sixty thousand more born to the common people, and ordered them all to meet in the city of London. He also had ships brought from coasts everywhere, to carry them across to the husbands waiting for them. Many in so large a crowd liked the plan, but most didn't, since they loved their parents and their country better. Some, perhaps, would have chosen virginity over marriage, and sooner died among any people at all than get rich this way. Each wanted something else, if she could have had her wish.

When the fleet was ready, the women went aboard and sailed down the Thames to the sea. At last they turned their sails towards Armorica, but winds rose against them and soon scattered the whole company. The ships were in danger out on the water, and most of them sank. Those that came through it were driven onto islands of barbarians, and the unknown people there either killed the women or made them slaves.

<a id="page-93"></a>[\[Giles 93\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-93) They'd fallen in with the wicked army of Wanius and Melga, who on Gratian's orders were crushing the coastal peoples and Germany with cruel slaughter.[^93-1] Wanius was king of the Huns and Melga of the Picts. Gratian had taken them on as allies and sent them into Germany to harass Maximianus's supporters. Raging along the coasts, they met the young women who'd landed there. Seeing how beautiful they were, they wanted to have sex with them. When the girls refused, the savages fell on them and slaughtered most of them without mercy.[^93-2]

Then Wanius and Melga, the leaders of the Picts and the Huns, who were on Gratian's and Valentinian's side, learned that Britain had been emptied of every armed soldier. They hurried there, joined forces with the neighbouring islands and landed in Albany. Forming up, they invaded the kingdom, which had no ruler and no one to defend it, and cut down the helpless common people.

As I said, Maximianus had taken with him every young man fit for war who could be found, and left behind only farmers, unarmed and with nobody to lead them. When Wanius and Melga saw they could put up no fight at all, they made great slaughter, and never stopped laying waste the cities and the countryside, as if they were raiding sheep pens.

When news of this disaster came to Maximianus, he sent Gracianus Municeps with two legions to help. As soon as they reached the island, they fought the enemy, cut down great numbers and drove the rest into Ireland.[^93-3] Meanwhile Maximianus was killed in Rome by Gratian's friends, and the Britons he'd taken with him were slaughtered or scattered. Those who could escape went to their countrymen in Armorica, which by now was called the second Britain.[^93-4]

## Part 6: The Romans leave, Vortigern brings in the Saxons, and his tower keeps falling

### Gracianus Municeps rules as a tyrant, and the Britons beg Rome for a legion

<a id="page-94"></a>[\[Giles 94\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-94) When Gracianus Municeps heard of Maximianus's death, he took the crown and made himself king.[^94-1] After that he was such a tyrant to the people that they formed into mobs, rushed at him and killed him. As the news spread to other lands, the enemies I spoke of came back from Ireland, bringing the Scots, the Norwegians and the Danes with them. They burned and plundered the country from one sea to the other.

Under these raids and their crushing weight, envoys went to Rome with letters.[^94-2] In tears they begged for an armed force to avenge them, and vowed to be Rome's subjects forever if the enemy were kept far off. A legion was soon sent, forgetting past wrongs. It crossed the ocean to the island on ships and fought the raiders at close quarters. It cut down great numbers of them, drove them all out of the land and freed the people from such savage slaughter.

Then the legion told the Britons to build a wall between Albany and Deira, from one sea to the other. Once manned, it would frighten off the enemy and keep the citizens safe.[^94-3] Albany had been laid waste by the barbarians who kept landing there, and any raider found shelter in it. So the natives raised public and private money, set to work and finished the wall.

### The Romans leave, and Archbishop Guithelin tells the Britons to defend themselves

<a id="page-95"></a>[\[Giles 95\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-95) Then the Romans told the country they couldn't keep coming on such hard campaigns. Rome's standards and so great an army shouldn't be dragged over land and sea for a few roving bandits who were no soldiers. Instead the Britons should get used to arms, and fight like men for their soil, their property, their wives and children. Their freedom and their lives mattered even more.

To give them this warning, the Romans called every fighting man on the island to London, since they were getting ready to go home. When all were gathered, Guithelin, archbishop of London, was given the floor.[^95-1] He spoke to them like this:

"The princes here have told me to speak to you, but I'm driven to tears more than to a fine speech. I pity you, helpless and weak since Maximianus stripped this kingdom of every soldier and all its young men. You were what remained: common people who knew nothing of war, busy with other work, farming and trading in all kinds of ways.

"So when enemies came from other nations, they drove you from your farms like sheep wandering with no shepherd, until Rome's power gave you back your land.[^95-2] Will you always hope in someone else's protection? Won't you arm your hands with shields, swords and spears against bandits who'd be no stronger than you, if it weren't for your laziness? The Romans are tired of the endless journeys that wear them out fighting the enemy for you. They'd rather give up all the tribute you pay than go on being worn out like this by land and sea.

<a id="page-96"></a>[\[Giles 96\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-96) "You were only the common people while your army was here. Do you think that means you've lost your humanity? Aren't people born out of order, so a farmer's son becomes a knight, and a knight's child a farmer? A fighting man even comes from a merchant, and a dealer from a soldier. Though each springs from the other this way, I don't think either loses what makes them human. So since you're men, act like it. Call on Christ to give you courage, and defend your freedom."

When he finished, the crowd roared so loudly you'd have said a sudden boldness had filled them.

### Wanius and Melga attack again, and the farmers on the wall can't hold it

After this the Romans gave the frightened people strong advice, and left them patterns for making weapons. They also had towers set at intervals along the ocean's southern shore, where their ships were kept, to watch the sea, since the barbarians were feared from there. But it's easier to make a hawk out of a kite than a learned man out of a farmer overnight. Pouring deep learning into such men is like throwing pearls in front of pigs.[^96-1]

As soon as the Romans said goodbye and left, as if never to return, the leaders I spoke of, Wanius and Melga, came out again from the ships that had carried them to Ireland. They brought the rest of their companies, the Scots and the Picts, with the Norwegians, the Danes and the others they'd hired. They took all Albany up to the wall. Knowing the Romans had gone and refused to come back, they set about wrecking the island more boldly than ever.

Against them lazy farmers were posted on top of the fortifications. They were no good for fighting, and too frightened even to run, their hearts trembling. Day and night they wasted away on their stupid seat. Meanwhile the enemy's hooked weapons never stopped. They dragged the miserable people from the walls and dashed them to the ground.

<a id="page-97"></a>[\[Giles 97\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-97) An early death at least did this for those it snatched away: by dying quickly they escaped the pitiful sufferings hanging over their brothers and their children. This was divine vengeance for their past crimes, and for the loss of so many fighting men through Maximianus's madness. If those soldiers had been there in such a disaster, no people could have attacked without being put to flight. That was plain while they stayed: they added far-off lands to their power and held Britain in peace. That's what happens when a kingdom is left to farmers to defend.

What more is there to say? The citizens abandoned their cities and the high wall. Once again they fled. Once again they scattered, more hopelessly than ever. Once again the enemy hunted them, and once again the slaughter came faster and crueler. Their enemies tore the pitiful people apart, as wolves do lambs.

So once again the desperate survivors wrote, this time to Agitius, a man of Roman power. Their letter said: "To Agitius, three times consul, the groans of the Britons." A little further on they complained: "The barbarians push us back to the sea, and the waves drive us back to them. Between them we die one of two deaths, drowned or slaughtered."[^97-1] They got no help for it, and went home sadly to tell their countrymen they'd been turned away.

### Guithelin crosses to Brittany to ask King Aldroen for help

So they talked it over, and Guithelin, archbishop of London, crossed over to Brittany, to ask his fellow Britons there for help.[^97-2] In those days it was called Armorica or Letavia.[^97-3] Its king then was Aldroen, the fourth after Conan Meriadoc, who'd been given the country by Maximianus, as I said before. Seeing so respected a man, Aldroen welcomed him with honour and asked why he'd come. Guithelin said:

<a id="page-98"></a>[\[Giles 98\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-98) "You know well enough, and it could move you to tears, how much the Britons, your own people, have suffered since Maximianus stripped us of our soldiers and had them settle this kingdom of yours. I hope you hold it in long peace. Every people of the islands around has risen against the little that's left of us. They've emptied our land of all its wealth, until its whole nation has lost its food, except what hunting brings in. Nobody stood in their way, since not one strong man or fighter of ours remained. The Romans have grown tired of us and refused us their help altogether.

"Shut out from any other hope, we've come to your mercy. We beg you to bring your forces and protect the land that's yours by right from the barbarians' attacks. Who else should wear the crown of Constantine and Maximianus against your will, when your grandfather and his father wore it? Get your fleet ready and come. Look, I'm putting the kingdom of Britain into your hands."

Aldroen answered, "There was a time when I wouldn't have refused the island of Britain, if anyone had offered it to me. I don't think any other country was ever more fertile while it had peace and quiet. But now that trouble has struck it, it's worth less, and the other princes and I hate it. Worst of all, the Romans' power has done it such harm that nobody can hold a lasting rank there without losing their freedom under the yoke of slavery. Who wouldn't rather have less somewhere else and be free, than have its riches as a slave?

"I rule this kingdom of mine with honour, and serve no lord above me. I chose it over every other nation, since I govern it in freedom. Still, my grandfathers and their fathers had a right to the island. So I'll give you my brother Constantine and two thousand soldiers, to free the country from the barbarians' attacks and, god willing, take its crown for himself.

<a id="page-99"></a>[\[Giles 99\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-99) "Constantine is strong in war and in all the other skills a knight should have. I won't hesitate to put him in your care with the number I named, if you'll take him. I'd better say nothing of a larger force, since the Gauls threaten trouble every day."

As soon as he finished, the archbishop thanked him. Then he called Constantine and smiled at him. "Christ conquers! Christ rules! Christ reigns!"[^99-1] he said. "Here's the king of deserted Britain. Only let Christ be with us, and here's our defence, our hope and our joy."

What more is there to say? Ships were made ready on the shore, soldiers were picked from all over the kingdom, and they were handed over to Guithelin.

### Constantine lands at Totnes, is made king and fathers Constans, Aurelius and Uther

When all was prepared, they put to sea and landed at the port of Totnes. They lost no time gathering the young men left on the island, fought the enemy and, thanks to the holy man's merits, won. After that the Britons who'd been scattered flocked in from everywhere. They held an assembly at Silchester, made Constantine their king and set the kingdom's crown on his head.[^99-2]

They also gave him a wife of noble Roman family, brought up by Archbishop Guithelin. She had three sons with him, named Constans, Aurelius Ambrosius and Uther Pendragon. Constans, the firstborn, he handed over to the church of Amphibalus in Winchester to become a monk. The other two, Aurelius and Uther, he put in Guithelin's care to bring up.

> **In the Arthur story:** Uther Pendragon is Arthur's father in every telling, from Geoffrey, Wace and Layamon to the French romances and Malory. Constans is the monk king Vortigern makes and then has murdered. Robert de Boron's *Merlin* calls him Moine, the French for monk, and gives the name Constans to his father, the old king, instead.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

<a id="page-100"></a>[\[Giles 100\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-100) Ten years later, a Pict who'd been in Constantine's service came to him as if to talk in private, took him aside alone into the bushes and killed him with a knife.[^100-1]

### Vortigern offers to take the monk Constans out of his abbey as king

After Constantine's death the nobles quarrelled over who should be raised to the throne.[^100-2] Some called for Aurelius Ambrosius, some for Uther, and some for other close relatives. While they argued, now one way and now another, Vortigern, earl of the Gewisse, who wanted the crown more than anything, went to the monk Constans. He said to him:

> **In the Arthur story:** Vortigern is the king the whole legend blames for letting the Saxons into Britain. Gildas calls him only the proud tyrant, and Bede and the *History of the Britons* name him. Geoffrey gives him the story every later telling keeps: the murdered monk king, Hengist's daughter, the tower that won't stand and the boy Merlin. In Wace, Layamon and Robert de Boron's *Merlin* he dies burning in his own tower when Constantine's sons come home.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

"Your father's dead, and your brothers are too young to be made king. I don't see anyone else of your family the people would choose. So if you'll take my advice and add to my lands, I'll turn the people towards putting you on the throne. I'll take you out of that habit, though your order forbids it."

When Constans heard this, he was overjoyed, and swore to give Vortigern whatever he knew he wanted. So Vortigern took him, dressed him in royal robes, brought him to London and made him king, but with little support from the people. Archbishop Guithelin had died by then, and no other bishop would dare anoint someone taken out of a monastery.[^100-3] Still, Constans didn't go without the crown for that. Vortigern set it on his head himself, in the bishop's place.

### Vortigern runs the kingdom for Constans and plans to take the throne

<a id="page-101"></a>[\[Giles 101\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-101) Once he'd been made king, Constans handed the whole running of the country to Vortigern. He put himself so far under his advice that he did nothing without his say. His feeble mind made him do it, since in the monastery he'd learned to manage something other than a kingdom.

When Vortigern saw this, he began to plan how to take the throne himself. He'd wanted it above everything all along, and he could see the moment coming when his wish could easily be carried out. The whole country had been put in his hands, and Constans, who was called king, was only the shadow of a prince. He was never stern and never did justice, so neither his own people nor the peoples around him feared him. His two little brothers, Uther Pendragon and Aurelius Ambrosius, were still lying in their cradles, too young to be raised to the throne.

By bad luck, besides, the kingdom's older nobles had died, and Vortigern alone seemed cunning, wise and a good counsellor. The rest were nearly all boys and men barely grown. Their fathers and uncles had been killed in the wars before, and they held their lands as best they could.

Knowing all this, Vortigern thought how he could most easily push the monk Constans off the throne and take his place. But he chose to put it off until he'd first brought the different peoples further under his power and friendship. So he began asking Constans to give him the royal treasure to keep, and his cities with their forts. The rumour was, he said, that the islands nearby meant to attack.

<a id="page-102"></a>[\[Giles 102\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-102) When he'd got them, he put his own men everywhere, to keep those towns loyal to him. Then, working out the betrayal he'd planned, he went to Constans and told him he should make his household bigger, so as to stand up better to the enemies coming.

Constans said, "Haven't I put everything in your hands? Do whatever you want, as long as they stay loyal to me."

Vortigern said, "I've been told the Picts want to bring the Danes and the Norwegians against us, to cause us all the trouble they can. So I'd advise you, and I think it's the best plan, to keep some of the Picts at your court, to go between you and the rest. If it's true they've started to rebel, they'll find out their friends' plots and tricks for you, and you'll avoid them more easily."

Here was a hidden enemy's secret betrayal. He didn't advise it for Constans's safety, but because he knew the Picts were a fickle people, ready for any crime. Drunk or angry, they'd need little pushing to turn on the king and kill him without a second thought. If that happened, his way to the throne would be open, as he'd so often wanted.

So he sent messengers to Scotland and invited a hundred soldiers of the Picts, and took them into the king's household. Once they were there, he honoured them above everyone and made them rich with all kinds of gifts. He gave them more food and drink than they could take, until they thought of him as their king. They went through the streets singing his praise: "Vortigern deserves to rule! Britain's sceptre should be his, not Constans's." At this Vortigern tried all the harder to load them with presents, to please them more.

Once he'd won their love completely, he got them drunk and told them he wanted to leave Britain to look for more land. The little he had, he said, couldn't pay even fifty soldiers.

<a id="page-103"></a>[\[Giles 103\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-103) Then, acting sad, he went off to his lodging and left them drinking in the hall. At this the Picts were more upset than you'd believe, thinking what he'd said was true. They grumbled to each other, "Why do we let this monk live? Why don't we kill him, so Vortigern can have the throne? Who else should succeed him? The man who never stops making us rich deserves the crown, honour and every rank there is."

### The Picts break into Constans's room, and Vortigern has them beheaded

After this they broke into the bedroom, attacked Constans, killed him and carried his head to Vortigern. When Vortigern saw it, he burst into tears as if he were sad, though he'd never been happier. Still, he called the citizens of London, since it had happened there. He had all the traitors tied up and then beheaded, for daring such a crime.

Some thought Vortigern had plotted the betrayal, and that the Picts would never have begun without his consent. Others had no doubt at all, and cleared him of the charge. In the end nobody knew for sure. The men bringing up the two brothers, Aurelius Ambrosius and Uther Pendragon, fled with them to Brittany, afraid Vortigern would kill them. King Budic II took them in there and raised them with the honour due to them.

### Vortigern crowns himself, and the Picts and Aurelius's return threaten him

<a id="page-104"></a>[\[Giles 104\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-104) Seeing he had no equal in the kingdom, Vortigern set the crown on his own head and put himself over his fellow princes. At last his betrayal came out, and the peoples of the neighbouring islands rose against him. The Picts had brought them into Albany. They were furious that their comrades had been killed over Constans, and meant to take revenge on him.

So Vortigern lived in daily dread, losing men in every fight. Fear came at him from the other side too, of Aurelius Ambrosius and his brother Uther Pendragon, who'd fled to Brittany because of him, as I said. Each day the rumour filled his ears that they were grown men now, had built a huge fleet and meant to come back to the kingdom that was theirs by right.

### Hengist and Horsa land in Kent with three ships and are brought to Vortigern

Meanwhile three fast boats, the kind we call long ships, landed in Kent, full of armed soldiers, with two brothers, Horsa and Hengist, as their leaders.[^104-1] Vortigern was at Dorobernia then, which is now called Canterbury, since he made a habit of visiting that city often. Messengers told him that strangers, very tall men, had landed in great ships. He gave them his peace and had them brought to him.[^104-2]

> **In the Arthur story:** Hengist is the Saxon leader of every later telling. Wace and Layamon follow Geoffrey's story of him, and in Robert de Boron's *Merlin* he's Hangus, and Uther kills him. English tradition made Hengist and Horsa the founders of the kingdom of Kent.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

As soon as they arrived, his eyes went first to the two brothers, since they stood out above the others in birth and looks.[^104-3] Then he looked over the rest too, and asked what country they were from and what had brought them to his kingdom.

<a id="page-105"></a>[\[Giles 105\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-105) Hengist answered for them all, being the oldest and wisest.

"Noblest of kings, we were born in the land of the Saxons, one of the regions of Germany. We've come to offer our service to you or some other ruler. We were driven out of our homeland for no other reason than that the kingdom's custom required it. Among us, when there are too many people, the princes of the different provinces meet and order all the young men of the kingdom to come to them. Then they draw lots and choose the best and strongest, who must go abroad to make a living in other lands. That way the country they were born in is freed of the extra crowd.

"Lately our people grew too many again, so the princes met, drew lots and chose these young men you see here. They ordered them to obey the custom laid down long ago, and put us two brothers at their head, me, Hengist, and him, Horsa, since we come from a line of leaders. So, obeying laws that have held from the beginning, we put to sea and came to your kingdom, with Mercury to guide us."

At Mercury's name Vortigern looked up, and asked what religion they had. Hengist said, "We worship our fathers' gods, Saturn, Jupiter and the others who rule the world, but Mercury most of all. In our language we call him Woden. Our ancestors set aside the fourth day of the week for him, and it's still called Wednesday after him.[^105-1] Next to him we worship the most powerful of the goddesses, Frigg. They gave her the sixth day too, which we call Friday after her."[^105-2]

<a id="page-106"></a>[\[Giles 106\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-106) Vortigern answered, "I'm truly sorry about your belief, which might better be called unbelief. But I'm glad you've come, since Yahweh, or someone else, has brought you just when I need you. My enemies press me on every side. If you'll share the labour of my wars with me, I'll keep you in my kingdom with honour, and make you rich with all kinds of gifts and land."

The barbarians agreed at once, the treaty was sealed, and they stayed at his court. Shortly after, the Picts poured out of Albany with a huge army and began wasting the north of the island. When Vortigern heard, he gathered his soldiers and marched across the Humber to meet them. As the two sides closed, they fought a fierce battle. The Britons hardly needed to fight, since the Saxons with them battled so bravely that the enemy, who used to win, were quickly put to flight.

### Hengist is given land and asks to send to Germany for more men

Having won through them, Vortigern added to their gifts, and granted their leader Hengist many fields in Lindsey, to keep himself and his comrades. Hengist was shrewd and sly. Seeing how friendly the king was to him, he went to him and said:

"My lord, your enemies trouble you on every side, and few of your countrymen love you. They all threaten you, and say they'll bring Aurelius Ambrosius from Brittany to throw you out and make him king. If you like, let's get word to our country and call soldiers over from there, so we'll have more men to fight.[^106-1] But there's one thing I'd beg of your kindness and judgement, if I weren't afraid of being turned down."

Vortigern said, "Then send your envoys to Germany, and invite whoever you want. Ask me for what you'd like, and you won't be refused."

<a id="page-107"></a>[\[Giles 107\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-107) Hengist bowed his head and thanked him. "You've made me rich with large estates and fields," he said, "but not with the honour my birth deserves, since I come from a line of leaders. I should have been given a city or a town besides, so your kingdom's nobles would think more of me. I ought to have an earl's rank or a prince's, as I'm descended from both."

Vortigern said, "I'm forbidden to give you gifts like that, because you're foreigners and pagans. I don't know your ways and customs well enough yet to make you equal with my own people. Even if I counted you as my own citizens, I couldn't start giving such things, since my nobles would be against it."

Hengist said, "Then grant me, your servant, as much of the land you've given me as a single strap can go around, so I can build a fort there to take shelter in if I need to. I'm loyal to you, now and always, and in your service I'll do what I mean to do."

The king was moved by his words and granted what he asked. He also told him to send messengers to Germany, so the soldiers called from there could hurry to help. Once the embassy had left for Germany, Hengist took a bull's hide and cut it into one long strap. With it he went around a rocky place he'd picked with great care, and inside that line he began to build.[^107-1] Once built, the fort was named after what had marked it out. Later it was called Kaercorrei in British and Thancastre in English, and in Latin we call it the castle of the strap.[^107-2]

### Hengist's daughter Rowena offers Vortigern a cup, and he asks for her

<a id="page-108"></a>[\[Giles 108\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-108) Meanwhile the messengers came back from Germany with eighteen ships full of picked soldiers. They also brought Hengist's daughter, Rowena, whose beauty seemed second to none. After they arrived, Hengist invited King Vortigern to his house, to see the new building and the troops just landed. The king went at once, without show. He praised the work done so quickly, and took the men who'd been called over into his service.

> **In the Arthur story:** Rowena's toast is where the legend dates Britain's loss from, since Vortigern gives the Saxons Kent for her. The *History of the Britons* tells the same feast without naming her, and Wace and Layamon keep her and her toast. Playwrights of the 1600s put the scene on the stage, and painters have shown Vortigern taking her cup ever since.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

At the end of the royal feast, the girl stepped out of her room carrying a gold cup full of wine. She came up close, knelt to the king and said, "*Laford King wacht heil!*"

When he saw the girl's face, he was struck by her beauty, and burned with desire. He asked his interpreter what she'd said and how he should reply. The man said, "She called you lord king and honoured you with a word of welcome. What you should answer is '*Drinc heil.*'"

So Vortigern answered that way, and told the girl to drink. He took the cup from her hand, kissed her and drank. From that day to this, the custom has lasted in Britain that at a feast, the one drinking greets the person beside them with the first words, and the one handed the drink after answers with the second.[^108-1]

Drunk on all kinds of liquor, Vortigern fell in love with the girl as Satan entered his heart, and asked her father for her. Satan really had gotten into him: he was a Christian who wanted to sleep with a pagan woman.[^108-2] Hengist was shrewd, and saw at once how fickle the king was. He talked over the king's request with his brother Horsa and the other older men with him.

<a id="page-109"></a>[\[Giles 109\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-109) They were all of one mind: the girl should be handed over to Vortigern, and they should ask him for the province of Kent in return. So without delay she was given to the king, and Kent to Hengist, though Guoyrancgonus, the earl who ruled it, knew nothing of it.[^109-1] Vortigern married the pagan woman that same night, and she pleased him beyond measure. Because of it he very quickly fell out with his nobles and his sons. He'd already fathered three, named Vortimer, Catigern and Pasgen.[^109-2]

### Germanus and Lupus preach, and Hengist asks to bring in Octa and Ebissa

At that time Saint Germanus of Auxerre and Lupus of Troyes came to preach Yahweh's word to the Britons.[^109-3] Their Christianity had been corrupted, partly by the pagans the king had brought among them, and also by the Pelagian heresy, whose poison had infected them for a long time.[^109-4] Through the holy men's preaching the true faith was restored to them, since they shone each day with miracles. Yahweh worked many wonders through them, and Gildas wrote them up in fine style.[^109-5]

Once the girl had been given to the king, as I said, Hengist said to him, "I'm your father now, and I ought to be your adviser. Don't pass over my advice, since with my people's strength you'll beat all your enemies. Let's invite my son Octa as well, with his brother Ebissa. They're warriors. Give them the lands in the north of Britain by the wall, between Deira and Scotland. They'll hold off the barbarians' attacks there, so you'll stay in peace on this side of the Humber."

Vortigern did as Hengist asked, and told the Saxons to call over whoever they knew could be of use.

<a id="page-110"></a>[\[Giles 110\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-110) Envoys went at once, and Octa, Ebissa and Cherdic came with three hundred ships full of armed men. Vortigern welcomed them all kindly and gave them great gifts, since through them he beat his enemies and won every battle. Hengist kept bringing over more boats too, a few at a time, and his numbers grew daily.

When the Britons saw it, they feared the Saxons would betray them, and told the king to drive them out of his kingdom. Pagans shouldn't mix with Christians or be let in among them, they said, because Christian law forbids it. Besides, so many had come that the citizens were afraid of them. Nobody could tell any more who was Christian and who wasn't, because the pagans had married their daughters and their relatives. With these complaints they urged the king not to keep them, in case they turned on the citizens with some betrayal. But Vortigern wouldn't take their advice, as he loved the Saxons above all other peoples because of his wife.

When the Britons saw this, they deserted Vortigern at once and together made his son Vortimer king.[^110-1] He did as they wished, and began driving out the barbarians any way he could, attacking them and making fierce raids on them. He fought four battles with them and won them all.[^110-2] The first was on the river Darent. The second was at the ford of Episford, where Horsa and Catigern, Vortigern's other son, met. They both fell, each giving the other a deadly wound. The third was on the coast. The enemy fled to their ships like women, and sheltered on the island of Thanet. Vortimer besieged them there, and attacked them daily in fights at sea.

When they couldn't stand the Britons' attacks any longer, they sent King Vortigern, who'd been with them in all their battles, to his son Vortimer. They asked permission to leave and to go back safely to Germany. While the two talked, the Saxons boarded their boats, left their wives and children behind and sailed home to Germany.

### Vortimer gives back what was taken, and Rowena has him given poison

<a id="page-111"></a>[\[Giles 111\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-111) Once he'd won, Vortimer began giving back the lands taken from the citizens, loving and honouring them, and putting the churches back in order as Saint Germanus told him. But the devil was jealous of his goodness at once. He got into the heart of Rowena, Vortimer's stepmother, and urged her to plot his death. She tried every poison she knew, and had one of his household, bought with countless gifts, give him a poisoned drink.

As soon as that famous warrior drank it, he was struck by a sudden sickness so bad that he gave up hope of living. He lost no time calling all his soldiers to him. He told them death was coming, and shared out his gold, his silver and all his ancestors had piled up. As they cried and sobbed, he comforted them, teaching them he was going the way everyone has to go. Then he urged the bold young warriors who used to stand by him in his wars to fight for their country and work to defend it from enemy attack.

With the greatest daring he ordered a bronze pyramid made and set up at the port where the Saxons used to land. When he was dead, his body was to be buried on top of it, so the barbarians would see his grave, turn their sails and head back to Germany. None of them would dare come near, he said, if they even saw his tomb. What daring, to want to be feared after his death by those he'd terrified alive! But once he was dead, the Britons did something else, and buried his body in the city of Trinovantum.[^111-1]

### Vortigern calls Hengist back, and Hengist comes with three hundred thousand men

<a id="page-112"></a>[\[Giles 112\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-112) After his son died, Vortigern was given back his kingdom.[^112-1] Moved by his wife's begging, he sent messengers to Hengist in Germany, telling him to come back to Britain, but privately and with a small following. He feared that if Hengist came any other way, fighting would break out between the barbarians and the citizens. But when Hengist heard Vortimer was dead, he gathered three hundred thousand armed men, made a fleet ready and sailed back to Britain.

When the coming of such a host was reported to Vortigern and the kingdom's princes, they were furious. They talked it over and decided to fight the Saxons and drive them off the shores. Hengist's daughter had messengers tell him so, and he thought at once about how best to meet it. He went over all kinds of schemes, and chose one above the rest: to attack the people of the land under cover of peace and betray them.

He sent envoys to the king and had them say that he hadn't brought so many men to stay in the kingdom, or to do the country any harm. He'd only led them over because he'd thought Vortimer was still alive, and meant to hold him off with them if Vortimer attacked. Now that he had no doubt Vortimer was dead, he put himself and his people in Vortigern's hands. Vortigern could keep as many of that great number as he liked in his kingdom, and the rest could go back to Germany without delay. If Vortigern agreed, Hengist asked him to name a day and a place where they could meet and settle everything as Vortigern wished.

<a id="page-113"></a>[\[Giles 113\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-113) When this was reported to the king, he was delighted, since he hadn't wanted to let Hengist go. In the end he ordered the Britons and the Saxons to gather near the monastery of Ambrius on the first of May, which was close now, to settle these things there.

Once both sides had agreed, Hengist turned to a new betrayal. He told his comrades to keep long knives hidden in their shoes. When the Britons were talking most freely, he'd give them this signal: *Nemet oure saxas*. At that, the Saxons were to grab the Britons standing next to them, draw their blades and cut their throats quickly.[^113-1]

The appointed day came, and they all met at the place named and began their talks on making peace. When Hengist saw the right moment for his betrayal, he shouted, "*Nemet oure saxas!*" and at once grabbed Vortigern and held him by his cloak. As soon as they heard the signal, the Saxons drew their knives and fell on the princes standing near them, who'd planned nothing of the kind. They cut the throats of about four hundred and sixty barons and earls.[^113-2] Saint Eldad later buried their bodies in the Christian way, not far from Kaercaradane, which is now called Salisbury. They lie in the cemetery beside the monastery of Ambrius, the abbot who'd founded it long before.[^113-3]

The Britons had all come without weapons, thinking only of making peace, so the men who'd come to betray them could kill them unarmed all the more easily. Still, the pagans didn't get away with it, since many of them were killed while they cut down the rest. The Britons tore stones and sticks out of the ground, and struck the traitors down defending themselves.

### Eldol fights the Saxons off with a stake, and Vortigern buys his life

<a id="page-114"></a>[\[Giles 114\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-114) Eldol, earl of Gloucester, was there. When he saw the betrayal, he picked up a stake he happened to find and fought back with it. Each blow broke whatever bone it landed on, and sent the man straight to his death.[^114-1] He smashed one man's head, another's arms, another's shoulders, many men's legs, and frightened them all. He didn't leave the place until he'd killed seventy men and worn out his stake. But since he couldn't hold out against such numbers, he got away from them and made for his own city.

Plenty fell on both sides, but the Saxons won, because the Britons had come unarmed, expecting nothing of the kind, and so could put up less of a fight. Once the Saxons had finished their wicked work, they didn't want to kill Vortigern. They tied him up, threatened him with death and demanded his cities and forts for his life. He granted them whatever they wanted at once, so they'd let him go alive. Once he'd sworn to it, they untied him. They marched first on the city of London and captured it. Then they took York and Lincoln, and Winchester too, laying waste every province on their way. They fell on the citizens everywhere like wolves on sheep their shepherds have left.

When Vortigern saw such slaughter, he withdrew into Wales, not knowing what to do against that wicked people.

### Vortigern's tower keeps sinking, and his messengers find the fatherless boy Merlin

<a id="page-115"></a>[\[Giles 115\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-115) At last Vortigern called his seers, asked their advice and told them to say what he should do.[^115-1] They told him to build himself a very strong tower to keep him safe, since he'd lost his other forts. So he searched all over for the right place to set it, and came at last to Snowdonia.[^115-2] There he brought together masons from different countries and ordered the tower raised. The builders gathered and began laying its foundations. But whatever they put up one day, the ground swallowed the next, so they couldn't tell where their work was going.

When Vortigern heard, he asked his seers again to tell him the cause. They said he should look for a young man with no father, and when he'd found him, kill him, so the mortar and the stones could be sprinkled with his blood. That, they said, would make the tower's foundation stand firm. At once messengers were sent through every province to find such a person.[^115-3]

When they came to the city later called Kaermodin, they saw young men playing in front of the gate, and joined the game.[^115-4] Tired from the journey, they sat down in the ring, watching for what they were after.

At last, when much of the day had gone, a quarrel suddenly broke out between two of the boys, named Merlin and Dabutius. As they argued, Dabutius said to Merlin, "Why do you fight with me, you fool? We'll never be of the same rank. I'm descended from kings on both sides of my family. As for you, nobody knows who you are, since you have no father."

> **In the Arthur story:** This is Merlin's first appearance in the Arthur story. Geoffrey made him by giving the *History of the Britons*' fatherless boy, who says his name is Ambrosius, the name of Myrddin, a prophet of Welsh poems. Later in this book he foretells Britain's future, brings the Giants' Dance from Ireland and gives Uther another man's shape for the night Arthur is conceived. Robert de Boron's *Merlin* makes him a devil's son saved by his mother's faith, and in the French romances and Malory he raises Arthur and is shut away at last by the woman he teaches.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

At this the messengers looked up. They stared at Merlin and asked the people standing around who he was.

<a id="page-116"></a>[\[Giles 116\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-116) They said his father was unknown, but his mother was the daughter of the king of Dyfed, and lived among the nuns in Saint Peter's church in that same city.

### Merlin's mother tells Vortigern how a spirit came to her

The messengers hurried to the city's governor and ordered him, on the king's behalf, to send Merlin and his mother to Vortigern. As soon as the official learned why they'd come, he sent them both to the king, to do with them as he pleased. When they were brought to him, Vortigern received the woman with care, knowing she was of noble birth. Then he began asking her what man she'd conceived by.

She said, "I swear on my life and yours, my lord king, no man I've known fathered this child in me. But I know one thing. When I was with the other girls in our rooms, someone used to come to me looking like a very handsome young man. Again and again he held me tight in his arms and kissed me. After staying with me a little while, he'd suddenly disappear, leaving nothing of himself to see.

"Many times he spoke to me when I sat alone, and never showed himself. After he'd come to me like this for a long time, he had sex with me often in a man's shape, and left me pregnant. You can be sure, my lord, I've known no other man who fathered this boy."

The king was amazed, and had Maugantius called, to tell him whether what the woman said could happen. Maugantius was brought, heard everything in order and said to Vortigern:

<a id="page-117"></a>[\[Giles 117\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-117) "In the books of our wise men and in many histories, I've found that plenty of people were born this way. As Apuleius writes about the god of Socrates, spirits live between the moon and the earth, and we call them incubus demons.[^117-1] They have something of human nature and something of the angels'. When they want to, they take on our shape and have sex with women. Perhaps one of them appeared to this woman and fathered this young man in her."

### Merlin challenges the seers to say what lies under the tower

Merlin heard it all, then went up to the king and said, "Why have my mum and I been brought to you?"[^117-2]

Vortigern said, "My seers told me to look for a man with no father, so my building would stand once it was soaked with that man's blood."

Then Merlin said, "Tell them to come here in front of me, and I'll prove they've made up a lie." The king was astonished at his words, and at once had his wise men come and sit facing Merlin.

Merlin said to them, "You don't know what's stopping the foundation of the tower they've begun. You advised that my blood be poured into the mortar, as if that would make the work hold at once. But tell me, what lies under the base? There's something under it that won't let it stand." The seers were terrified, and fell silent.

Then Merlin, who was also called Ambrosius, said, "My lord king, call your workmen and have them dig. You'll find a pool down there that keeps the tower from standing."[^117-3] When they dug, there it was under the ground, and it was what had made the work give way. Merlin Ambrosius went up to the seers again and said, "Tell me, you lying flatterers, what's under the water?" They couldn't answer a word.

<a id="page-118"></a>[\[Giles 118\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-118) Again he said to the king, "Have the pool emptied by channels, my lord, and at the bottom you'll see two hollow stones, and in them two dragons asleep." Vortigern believed him, since he'd told the truth about the water, and had it done. When he found it just as Merlin said, he admired him above everyone. All who stood there were astonished too at such wisdom in him, and thought there was something divine in him.

## Part 7: Merlin tells Vortigern what the two dragons under the pool mean

### Bishop Alexander of Lincoln asks Geoffrey for Merlin's prophecies

I hadn't yet reached this point in the history when talk of Merlin spread, and people of my own day pressed me from every side to publish his prophecies.[^118-1] Alexander, bishop of Lincoln, urged me most. He was a man of the deepest faith and wisdom. Nobody else, in the Church or outside it, had so many knights and noblemen serving him, drawn to him by his gentle goodness and his generous gifts. I chose to please him above anyone, so I translated the work and sent it to him with this letter.[^118-2]

### Geoffrey sends Bishop Alexander his Latin of the prophecies

<a id="page-119"></a>[\[Giles 119\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-119) Bishop Alexander of Lincoln, my affection for you, noble as you are, has driven me to translate Merlin's prophecies from British into Latin before finishing the history I'd begun of the kings of the Britons.[^119-1] I'd meant to complete that first and take this work on afterwards. With both tasks on me at once, I feared my mind wouldn't be equal to either.

> **In the Arthur story:** Merlin's prophecies were the most widely read part of Geoffrey's work, copied and believed across Europe, quoted by chroniclers of his own day and put into Icelandic verse. Wace's French telling of the history leaves them out, though it says it knows of them. Their way of letting animals stand for kings set a fashion in political prophecy that lasted four hundred years.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

But I was sure your fine, subtle judgement would forgive me. So I've set my rough country pen to these pages, and I've put a language you don't know into words for you, in a plain tune.

I'm very surprised you chose to hand this to so poor a writer. Your rod of office rules so many men more learned and richer than I am, who could please the ears of your Minerva with a grander song.[^119-2] Leaving aside every wise man on the island of Britain, you're the one, I'm not ashamed to say, who'd sing better than all of them on a bold lyre, if your high rank didn't call you to other work.

Since you've wanted Geoffrey of Monmouth to play his pipe in this prophecy, don't refuse his tunes your favour. If he's played anything out of order or wrong, bring it back into harmony with your Muses' cane.[^119-3]

### The white and the red dragon fight, and Merlin weeps and prophesies

Vortigern, king of the Britons, was sitting on the bank of the drained pool when two dragons came out of it. They closed on each other and fought a terrible battle, breathing out fire.

<a id="page-120"></a>[\[Giles 120\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-120) The white one got the upper hand and chased the red to the far end of the lake. Stung at being driven off, the loser charged and forced the winner back. While the two struggled, the king told Merlin Ambrosius to say what their fight meant. At once Merlin burst into tears, breathed in the spirit of prophecy and said:

"Pity the red dragon, the people of Britain. Its end is coming fast. The white is the Saxons you invited, who'll take over its caves and crush it. Its mountains will be made level with the valleys, whose rivers will run with blood. Religion will be wiped out, and the churches will lie in ruins for all to see.

"In the end the crushed people will rise and stand up to the foreigners' cruelty. The boar of Cornwall will bring help and trample their necks under his feet.[^120-1] The islands of the ocean will fall under his power, and he'll own the forests of Gaul. Rome will fear his fury, and how he ends will be uncertain. He'll be praised by every people, and his story will feed storytellers.

"Six of his line will hold the sceptre after him, but after them the worm of Germany will rise.[^120-2] A wolf of the sea will raise it up, and the forests of Africa will go with it.[^120-3] Religion will be destroyed again, and the bishops' chief seats will move. London's honour will bow to Canterbury,[^120-4] and the seventh shepherd of York will be seen in the kingdom of Brittany.[^120-5] St Davids will wear the pallium of the City of the Legions,[^120-6] and the preacher of Ireland will lose his voice because of a baby growing in a womb.[^120-7] A rain of blood will fall, and a terrible famine will strike people.

"When these things come, the red will mourn, but after its long struggle it'll grow strong. Then disaster will hurry on the white, and the buildings in its gardens will be torn down. Seven kings will be killed, and one of them will be made a saint. Mothers' bellies will be cut open, and babies will come too early. There'll be a great slaughter of people, to bring the natives back. Whoever does this will put on a bronze man and stand guard over London's gates a long time, riding a horse of the same metal.[^120-8]

### The red dragon turns on itself, and the white dragon comes back

<a id="page-121"></a>[\[Giles 121\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-121) "After that the red dragon will go back to its old ways and work at tearing itself apart.[^121-1] Then Yahweh's vengeance will come, since the earth will fail its farmers.[^121-2] Plague will strike the people and empty all the nations. The survivors will leave their native soil and sow the lands of strangers.[^121-3] A blessed king will fit out a fleet, and be counted the twelfth among the saints in the hall.[^121-4] The kingdom will be miserably empty, and the threshing floors will turn back into barren woods.[^121-5]

"The white dragon will rise again and invite a daughter of Germany.[^121-6] Our gardens will be filled again with foreign seed, and the red will waste away at the edge of the pool. Then the worm of Germany will be crowned, and the prince of bronze buried. A limit has been set for the new king that it'll never fly past. It'll live in unrest under others' power for a hundred and fifty years, then rule for three hundred. Then the north wind will rise against it and snatch away the flowers the west breeze grew. There'll be gilding in the churches, and the edge of the sword won't stop.

"The dragon of Germany will barely keep its caves, since it'll be punished for its betrayal. At last it'll thrive for a little while, but Normandy's tithe will hurt it. A people will come in wood and iron coats and take revenge for its wickedness.[^121-7] They'll give the old inhabitants back their homes, and the foreigners' ruin will be plain. The white dragon's seed will be rooted out of our gardens, and what's left of its line will be cut down by a tenth. They'll bear the yoke of slavery, and wound their mother with hoes and plows.[^121-8]

### Two dragons follow, then a lion of justice and its cubs

"Two dragons will come next. One will be choked by envy's arrow, and the other will come back hidden under the shadow of a name. Then a lion of justice will follow.[^121-9] At its roar the towers of Gaul will shake, and the island's dragons with them. In those days gold will be wrung from the lily and the nettle, and silver will flow from the hooves of cattle. Men with curled hair will dress in fleeces of many colours, and their outward clothing will show what's inside them. The feet of barking dogs will be cut off. Wild animals will have peace, and human kindness will put an end to punishment. The shape of trade will be split, and half will be round.

<a id="page-122"></a>[\[Giles 122\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-122) "The greed of kites will die, and the wolves' teeth will be blunted. The lion's cubs will be changed into fish of the sea,[^122-1] and its eagle will build its nest on Mount Aravius.[^122-2] Gwynedd will turn red with a mother's blood, and the house of Corineus will kill six brothers. The island will be soaked with tears at night, and so everyone will be stirred up to anything.

"Poor Normandy: the lion's brain will be poured out in you, and with his body torn, he'll be carried away from his native soil.[^122-3] His heirs will try to fly above the heights, but the favour of new men will be raised up.[^122-4] Mercy to the wicked will hurt the one who holds power, until he puts on his father. So, armed with a boar's teeth, he'll climb over the mountaintops and the shadow of the helmeted one. Albany will be angry, call up its allies and set about shedding blood. A bit will be put in its jaws, made in the gulf of Brittany. The eagle of the broken treaty will gild it, and be glad of its third nesting.

"The cubs will wake roaring, leave the woods and hunt inside city walls. They'll do great slaughter among those who stand in their way, and cut out the tongues of bulls. They'll chain the necks of the ones who roar, and bring back the days of their grandfathers.

"Then from the first to the fourth, from the fourth to the third, from the third to the second, the thumb will roll in oil. The sixth will tear down the walls of Ireland and level its woods into open country. He'll bring its different parts together into one, and be crowned with the lion's head. His beginning will give way to wandering desire, but his end will fly up to heaven. He'll rebuild the saints' houses across the lands, and set shepherds in fitting places. He'll dress two cities in two palliums, and give virgins the gifts fit for them. So this king will earn Yahweh's favour, and be placed among the blessed.

"From him will come a lynx that sees through everything, and it'll threaten its own people with ruin. Through it Normandy will lose both islands and be stripped of its old honour. Then the citizens will return, since the foreigners will fall out among themselves.

### A white old man on a white horse, and Cadwaladr calls Conan

<a id="page-123"></a>[\[Giles 123\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-123) "An old man white as snow, on a horse the same colour, will turn the Periron aside, and mark out a mill over it with a bright rod.

"Cadwaladr will call Conan, and take Albany as his ally.[^123-1] Then the foreigners will be slaughtered, and the rivers will run with blood. Then the springs of Brittany will burst out, and they'll wear Brutus's crown. Wales will be filled with joy, and the oaks of Cornwall will grow green. The island will be called by Brutus's name, and the strangers' name will die out.

"From Conan will come a warlike boar, who'll test the edge of his tusks in the forests of Gaul. He'll cut down all the tallest trees, but protect the smaller. Arabia and Africa will fear him, since he'll drive his charge on into farthest Spain.

### A goat with golden horns, a boar of trade and a tree over London

"A goat of the castle of Venus will follow, with golden horns and a silver beard. It'll breathe so much mist from its nostrils that the whole face of the island will be clouded over. There'll be peace in its time, and the crops will multiply in the rich soil. Women will move like snakes as they walk, and all their steps will be full of pride. The camps of Venus will be built again, and Cupid's arrows won't stop wounding.[^123-2]

"All the springs will turn to blood, and two rulers will fight a duel over a lioness from the ford of the staff. Every field will run wild, and people will give themselves up to lust without end. Three ages will see all this, until the kings buried in the city of London are brought to light. Famine will come back. Plague will come back, and the citizens will mourn their emptied cities.

"A boar of trade will come, and call the scattered herds back to their lost pastures. Its breast will be food for the needy, and its tongue will quench the thirsty. Rivers will flow from its mouth and wet people's dry throats.

"Then a tree will grow over London's tower. It'll be content with only three branches, yet the spread of its leaves will shade the whole face of the island. The north wind will come against it as an enemy, and with its cruel blast tear off the third. The two left will take its place, until one smothers the other with its foliage.

<a id="page-124"></a>[\[Giles 124\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-124) "Then the survivor will hold the ground of both, and shelter foreign birds. The native ones will find it harmful, since for fear of its shadow they'll lose their freedom to fly. An ass of wickedness will follow, quick against goldsmiths but slow against the greed of wolves. In those days oaks will burn in the woods, and acorns will grow on linden branches.

"The Severn sea will run out through seven mouths, and the river Usk will boil for seven months. Its fish will die of the heat, and snakes will be born from them. Bath's baths will go cold, and their healing waters will bring death. London will mourn twenty thousand dead, and the Thames will turn to blood. Monks will be called to marry, and their cry will be heard in the Alps.

### Three springs break out at Winchester, and a girl dries them with her breath

"Three springs will break out in the city of Winchester, and their streams will cut the island into three parts. Whoever drinks from the first will live long and never be weighed down by sickness. From the second they'll waste away with a hunger nothing fills, with pale, frightened faces. From the third they'll die suddenly, and no grave will take their bodies.

"Wanting to escape such a greedy thing, people will try to hide it under different coverings. Anything laid on top will take the form of another body. If earth is thrown on, it'll turn into stones, those into wood, that into ashes, and those into water.

"So a girl will be sent out from the city of Cnut's forest to bring a cure.[^124-1] Once she's tried every art, she'll dry up the harmful springs with her breath alone. Then, after she's refreshed herself with the healthy water, she'll carry Celidon Wood in her right hand, and in her left the battlements of London's walls.

<a id="page-125"></a>[\[Giles 125\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-125) "Wherever she goes, she'll leave footprints of sulfur burning with a double flame. Their fumes will wake the Ruteni and cook food for the creatures under the sea. She'll stream with pitiful tears and fill the land with dreadful cries. A stag with ten points will kill her. Four of them will wear golden crowns, and the other six will turn into the horns of wild oxen, whose awful sound will shake the three islands of Britain.

"The Forest of Dean will wake, break out in a human voice and cry: 'Come, Wales, take Cornwall to your side, and tell Winchester the earth will swallow it. Move the shepherd's seat to where the ships put in, and let the rest of the limbs follow the head. The day is hurrying on when the citizens will die for the crime of breaking their oaths. The whiteness of the wool did harm, and so did the variety of its dyes. Pity the people who swore falsely, since a famous town will fall because of them.'

"The ships will be glad of such growth, and two will become one. A hedgehog loaded with apples will rebuild the city, and birds from different woods will fly together at their scent. He'll add a huge palace, and ring it with six hundred towers. So London will envy it, and make its own walls three times as strong. The Thames will surround it on every side, and word of the work will cross the Alps. The hedgehog will hide its apples inside the city, and dig roads underground.

"In that time stones will speak, and the sea between Britain and Gaul will shrink to a narrow strait. People on each shore will hear each other, and the island's land will grow wider. The secrets under the waves will be revealed, and Gaul will shake with fear.

### A heron's nest hatches a fox, a wolf and a bear

"After this a heron will come out of the forest of Calaterium and fly around the island for two years. With its cry at night it'll call the flocks together, and gather every kind of bird to itself. They'll fall on the fields people have sown, and eat up all the grain of the harvest. Famine will follow, and a terrible plague after it.

"When the disaster has passed, the hateful creature will go to the valley of Galabes and raise it into a high mountain. On its top it'll plant an oak, and build among its branches.

<a id="page-126"></a>[\[Giles 126\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-126) "Three eggs will be laid in the nest. Out of them will come a wolf, a bear and a fox. The last will eat her mother and put on an ass's head. Wearing that monster's shape, she'll frighten her brothers and drive them far off into Normandy. There they'll stir up a tusked boar, sail back with him and go after her. When the fight begins, she'll pretend to be dead and move him to pity. He'll soon come to the body, and as he stands over it, breathe on her eyes and face.

"But she won't have forgotten her old cunning. She'll bite his left foot and tear it clean off. Then, with a leap, she'll snatch his right ear and his tail, and hide in the mountain caves.

"The boar, tricked, will look for the wolf and the bear and ask them to give him back his lost parts. Once they've taken up his case, they'll promise him two of her feet, her ears and her tail, to make him a pig's limbs from them. He'll agree and wait to be put back together.

"Meanwhile the fox will come down from the mountains, turn herself into a wolf and go to him as if for a talk, then slyly eat him whole. After that she'll change into a boar and wait for her brothers, as though she had no limbs. When they come, she'll kill them too with a sudden bite, and wear the lion's head as a crown.

### A serpent circles London, and the mountain ox's herds drink the Thames dry

"In her days a serpent will be born, threatening death to all who live. It'll be long enough to coil all the way around London, and will swallow everyone who passes by.

"The mountain ox will put on the head of a wolf, and whiten his teeth in the Severn's forge. He'll gather the herds of Albany and of Wales to himself, and they'll drink the Thames dry. The ass will call the long-bearded goat, and borrow its shape. So the mountain ox will be angry, call the wolf, and turn into a horned bull against them. Once he's given himself up to cruelty, he'll eat their flesh and bones, but he'll be burned on top of Urian. The ashes of the pyre will become swans, which will swim on dry land as if in a river.

<a id="page-127"></a>[\[Giles 127\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-127) "They'll swallow fish with smaller ones inside, and people the same way. When old age comes on them, they'll turn into sea wolves and lay traps beneath the waves. They'll sink ships and pile up plenty of silver.

"The Thames will flood again. Calling the rivers together, it'll burst past the limits of its bed, hide the nearby cities and overturn the mountains in its way. Full of trickery and wickedness, it'll take the spring of Galaes to itself. From it quarrels will break out, stirring the men of Gwynedd to war. The oaks of the forests will gather and fight the rocks of the Gewisse. The raven will come with the kites and eat the bodies of the dead.

"An owl will nest on the walls of Gloucester, and an ass will hatch there. The serpent of Malvern will raise it, and stir it to many tricks. Taking the crown, it'll climb to the heights and frighten the people of the land with its horrible braying.

"In its days the mountains of Panchaia will shake, and the provinces will be stripped of their woods.[^127-1] A worm with fiery breath will come and burn the trees with the vapor it sends out. Seven lions will come out of it, disfigured with goats' heads. They'll corrupt women with the stench of their nostrils, and make their own wives common property. No father will know his own son, since they'll mate like cattle.

### A giant of wickedness rides the dragon of Worcester

"Then a giant of wickedness will come, who'll terrify everyone with the sharpness of his eyes. The dragon of Worcester will rise against him and try to destroy him. But in the fight it'll be beaten and crushed by the winner's evil. The giant will climb onto its back, strip off his clothes and sit there naked. It'll carry him high up, rear back and lash him. Getting his strength back, he'll break its jaws with his sword. At last the dragon will get tangled under its own tail and die poisoned.

"After him the boar of Totnes will follow, and crush the people with cruel tyranny. Gloucester will send out a lion to harry the tyrant in many battles, trample him underfoot and frighten him with open jaws. In the end it'll quarrel with the kingdom and climb over the backs of the nobles. A bull will join the fight and strike him with its right foot.

<a id="page-128"></a>[\[Giles 128\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-128) "It'll drive him through the inns of the land, but break its horns on the walls of Oxford. The fox of Kaerdubalum will avenge the lion, and eat the bull up whole with her teeth. The adder of Lincoln will coil around her, and announce its presence to many dragons with a horrible hiss. Then they'll clash, to tear each other apart. The winged one will overpower the wingless one, and fix its claws in the other's poisoned cheeks. Others will join in, and they'll kill each other.

"A fifth will succeed the dead, and break the rest with all kinds of schemes. He'll climb onto one's back with a sword and cut its head off its body. Stripping off his clothes, he'll mount another, and grip its tail with his right hand and his left. He'll beat it naked, after achieving nothing clothed. He'll torment the rest from behind and drive them all over the kingdom.

### A roaring lion, a white giant and the charioteer of York

"A roaring lion will come, so fierce that everyone will fear it. It'll bring fifteen parts together into one, and hold the people alone. A giant will shine the colour of snow, and grow into a white people. Luxuries will weaken the princes, and they'll suddenly be turned into animals. Among them a lion will rise, swollen with human blood. A reaper with a sickle will be set under it in the grain, and while he struggles to reap, it'll crush him.

"The charioteer of York will calm them. He'll drive out his master and climb into the chariot he's driving. Drawing his sword, he'll threaten the east, and fill the tracks of his wheels with blood. Then he'll become a fish in the sea, and, called back by a snake's hiss, he'll mate with it. From them will be born three bulls flashing like lightning. After eating up their pastures, they'll change into trees.

"The first will carry a whip of vipers, and turn his back on the younger. That one will try to snatch it, but he'll be taken by the last. They'll look away from each other, until they've thrown away the poisoned cup. The farmer of Albany will follow him, with a serpent threatening his back. He'll set about turning the soil over, so the fields of the land will be white with grain. The snake will work to spread its poison, so the grass won't grow into harvests. A deadly plague will wipe out the people, and the city walls will stand empty.

<a id="page-129"></a>[\[Giles 129\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-129) "Gloucester will be given as the cure, and it'll put forward the foster daughter of the one with the whip.[^129-1] She'll carry the scales of medicine, and the island will soon be renewed. Then two will hold the sceptre in turn, and a horned dragon will serve them. One will come in iron and ride a flying serpent. With his body naked he'll sit on its back, and lay his right hand on its tail. The seas will be stirred by his shout, and he'll strike fear into the second.

"So the second will ally himself with the lion, but a quarrel will break out and they'll fight. They'll go down under each other's blows, but the animal's fury will win. Someone will come with a drum and a lyre, and soothe the lion's rage. So the nations of the kingdom will be at peace, and they'll call him to the scales. Given a seat, he'll set about the weighing, but he'll stretch out his hands to Albany.

"So the lands of the north will mourn, and open the doors of the churches. The standard-bearing wolf will lead the troops and wrap its tail around Cornwall. A knight in a chariot will stand against it, and turn that people into a boar. Then it'll lay waste the provinces, but hide its head in the depths of the Severn.

"A man will embrace a lion in gold, and its gleam will blind the eyes of those who look. Silver will shine all around, and trouble the different wine presses. Once they're filled, people will get drunk, and, turning from heaven, look to the earth. The stars will turn their faces away from them and confuse their usual course. Angry, they'll let the crops dry up, and hold back the sky's rain. Roots and branches will swap places, and it'll be so strange it'll seem a miracle.

### The sun, the moon and the stars leave their courses

"The sun's brightness will fade in Mercury's amber, and the sight will fill people with horror. Stilbon of Arcadia will change his shield, and Mars's helmet will call Venus.[^129-2] It'll cast a shadow, and Mercury's fury will pass its bounds. Iron Orion will bare his sword. Phoebus of the sea will torment the clouds.[^129-3] Jupiter will leave his lawful paths, and Venus will desert her appointed lines. Saturn's dull grey light will fall as rain, and kill people with its curved sickle.[^129-4] The twelve houses of the stars will cry to see their guests run past like this.[^129-5]

<a id="page-130"></a>[\[Giles 130\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-130) "Gemini will give up their usual embrace, and call the urn to the springs. The weights of Libra will hang crooked, until Aries puts his curved horns under them. The tail of Scorpio will breed lightning, and Cancer will quarrel with the sun.[^130-1] Virgo will climb on the back of Sagittarius, and darken her virgin flowers. The moon's chariot will disturb the zodiac, and the Pleiades will burst into tears. None of them will go back to their duties, but Ariadne will hide behind her closed door on its ledges.[^130-2] At the stroke of a ray the seas will rise, and the dust of the ancients will come back to life. The winds will clash with a terrible blast, and make their sound among the stars."

## Part 8: Merlin brings the Giants' Dance from Ireland, and Uther comes to Igraine at Tintagel

### Vortigern asks Merlin how he'll die

Merlin's prophecies, these and plenty more, amazed everyone standing there, since his words could be taken so many ways.[^130-3] Vortigern admired them above all the rest, and praised both the young man's understanding and what he predicted. Vortigern had never heard anyone of that age speak like this. So, wanting to know how his life would end, he asked Merlin to tell him what he knew.

Merlin said, "Run from the fire of Constantine's sons, if you can. They're getting their ships ready now. They're leaving the shore of Brittany and spreading their sails over the sea. They'll make for the island of Britain, attack the Saxons and bring that wicked people under them. But first they'll shut you in a tower and burn you.

<a id="page-131"></a>[\[Giles 131\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-131) "You betrayed their father, to your own harm, and invited the Saxons in. You brought them over to protect you, but they've come to punish you. Two deaths hang over you, and it isn't clear which you'll escape first. On one side the Saxons will lay waste your kingdom and come for your life. On the other, two brothers will land, Aurelius and Uther Pendragon, who'll try to make you pay for their father's death. Find a place to hide, if you can. Tomorrow they'll hold the shore at Totnes.

"The Saxons' faces will run red with blood. Hengist will be killed, and Aurelius Ambrosius crowned. He'll bring the peoples peace and rebuild the churches, but he'll die of poison. His brother Uther Pendragon will follow him as king, and his days too will be cut short the same way. Your descendants will have a hand in that betrayal, and the boar of Cornwall will devour them."[^131-1]

The next day, as soon as it was light, Aurelius Ambrosius landed with his brother and ten thousand soldiers.[^131-2]

### Aurelius Ambrosius is anointed king and marches on Vortigern

When news of his coming spread, the Britons who'd been scattered in such a disaster gathered from everywhere. With their countrymen around them again, their spirits rose.

They called the clergy together and anointed Aurelius king, and submitted to him, as their custom was. When they urged him to attack the Saxons, he talked them out of it, since he wanted to go after Vortigern first. The betrayal of his father hurt him so much that he felt he'd done nothing until he'd paid Vortigern back. So, eager to do what he'd set his heart on, he turned his army towards Wales and made for the fort of Genoreu, where Vortigern had fled to be safe. It stood in the land of Ergyng, above the river Wye, on a hill called Cloarius.

<a id="page-132"></a>[\[Giles 132\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-132) When Aurelius reached it, he remembered the betrayal of his father and his brother, and said to Eldol, earl of Gloucester, "Look at the walls of this city, my noble friend. Can they keep Vortigern from me, and stop me burying the point of my sword in his guts? He's earned his death, and I think you know he has. He's the wickedest man alive, and deserves torments past words!

"First he betrayed my father Constantine, who'd freed him and the country from the Picts' raids. Then my brother Constans, made king by him only so he could destroy him. Then, when his own cunning marked him out for all to see, he brought the pagans in among his countrymen, to wipe out everyone who stood by me. But he walked, off his guard, into the trap he'd set for those loyal to him. Yahweh allowed it.

"When the Saxons found out how vile Vortigern was, they threw him out of the kingdom, and nobody should be sorry for that. What I think we should mourn is this. The wicked people that Vortigern invited have wiped out our noble citizens and laid waste our rich country. They've torn down our holy churches and all but blotted out Christianity from one sea to the other. So now, my countrymen, act like men! Take your revenge first on the one who brought all these evils on us. Then let's turn our weapons on the enemy hanging over us, and free our country from their greed."

At once they got to work with every kind of siege engine, trying to break down the walls. In the end, when everything else failed, they set the place on fire. Once the flames caught, they didn't die down until they'd burned up the tower, and Vortigern with it.

### Hengist's Saxons fear Aurelius and draw back beyond the Humber

<a id="page-133"></a>[\[Giles 133\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-133) When this was reported to Hengist and his Saxons, Hengist was afraid, since he knew how brave Aurelius was. Aurelius had so much strength and daring that in Gaul no one dared fight him. Whenever he met an opponent, he either knocked him off his horse or broke his spear to pieces. Besides that, he was generous with gifts, devoted to worship, modest in everything, and above all he never lied. He was strong on foot, stronger on horseback, and skilled at leading an army. While he was still living in Brittany, the news of all this had flown again and again to the island.

So the Saxons feared him, and drew back beyond the Humber. There they fortified cities and towns, since that country had always been open to them as a refuge. Scotland was close, and protected them, ready as it always was to do the citizens any harm it could. That region was hard to live in and empty of people, so it had given foreigners a safe shelter. Its position had left it open to the Picts, the Scots, the Danes, the Norwegians and all the others who'd landed to lay waste the island. So, trusting in the land next door, they'd fled towards it, to withdraw into it if they had to, as if into their own camp.

When Aurelius was told this, it made him bolder, and he began to hope he'd win. He quickly called the citizens together, made his army bigger and set off for the north. As he went through the provinces and saw them in ruins, it hurt him, most of all the churches torn down to the ground. He promised to rebuild them if he won.

### Hengist marches against Aurelius at Maisbeli

<a id="page-134"></a>[\[Giles 134\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-134) When Hengist learned he was coming, he got his nerve back and picked out his best fighters. He fired up each one, urging them all to stand up to the enemy together like men, and not to be afraid of fighting Aurelius.
