He told them Aurelius had only a few Britons from Brittany with him, no more than ten thousand. The island's Britons he counted for nothing, after beating them in battle so often. So he promised his followers victory, and safety in their greater numbers, as there were about two hundred thousand armed men with him.

With them all fired up like this, he went to meet Aurelius on a plain called Maisbeli, which Aurelius had to cross. He meant to make a sudden, stealthy attack and catch the Britons unprepared. But Aurelius knew of it, and didn't put off going to the field because of it. He went on all the faster.

When Aurelius saw the enemy, he drew up his troops. He ordered three thousand of those from Brittany to stay on horseback, and set the rest in line mixed with the islanders. He put the men of Dyfed on the hills, and those of Gwynedd in the woods nearby, so that if the Saxons fled that way, there'd be soldiers there to meet them.

### Eldol remembers the betrayal, and the armies meet

Meanwhile Eldol, earl of Gloucester, came to the king and said, "One day would be enough for me, in place of every day of my life, if Yahweh let me fight Hengist. One of us would fall, once we got at each other with swords.

<a id="page-135"></a>[\[Giles 135\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-135) "I remember the day we met as if to make peace. While we talked about an agreement, he betrayed everyone there and had them killed with knives, all but me.[^135-1] Four hundred sixty barons and earls fell that day. They'd all come unarmed. In such danger Yahweh put a stake in my hands, and I defended myself with it and escaped."

So Eldol talked. But Aurelius urged his men to put all their hope in Christ, then to attack their enemies boldly and fight together for their country.

Hengist, on the other side, was drawing up his troops. As he lined them up, he showed them what to do in battle, and as he taught them, he went from one to the next, to give them all the same daring. At last the companies were in place, and the armies met. They traded blows, and the blood flowed freely. On both sides, the Britons and the Saxons were wounded and died. Aurelius urged on the Christians, Hengist the pagans. All through the fighting, Eldol kept trying to get at Hengist, but he couldn't.

Hengist saw the Saxons falling and the Britons winning, as Yahweh wanted.

At once Hengist fled and made for the town of Kaerconan, now called Conisbrough. Aurelius went after him, killing or enslaving all the Saxons he found on the way. When Hengist saw Aurelius was following him, he wouldn't go into Kaerconan. He gathered his people into companies and got ready to fight again. He knew the walls couldn't hold out against Aurelius, and all his safety lay in sword and spear.

When Aurelius came up, he drew up his own men too, and attacked fiercely. The Saxons stood together, and both armies dealt deadly wounds. Blood ran everywhere, and the cries of the dying drove the living to rage. In the end the Saxons would have won, if the horsemen from Brittany hadn't come. Aurelius had placed them there, as he'd done in the first battle.

<a id="page-136"></a>[\[Giles 136\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-136) When those riders came, the Saxons gave way, and once they were split up, they could hardly come together again. Then the Britons pressed harder, and attacked the enemy as one. Aurelius never stopped urging his men on, wounding those who stood against him, chasing those who ran, and so putting heart into his own side. Eldol did the same, running here and there and dealing his enemies terrible wounds. But whatever he did, he burned for the chance to fight Hengist.

### Eldol takes Hengist by the nose-guard of his helmet

As the troops charged one way and another, the two happened to meet, and began trading blows. What fighters they were, beyond all others! As they struck at each other with their swords, sparks flew, as if they were making thunder and lightning. For a long time it wasn't clear which of them was stronger. At times Eldol pressed and Hengist gave ground, and at times it went the other way.

While they fought like this, Gorlois, duke of Cornwall, came up with the company he led, attacking the troops on every side. When Eldol saw him, he grew more confident. He grabbed Hengist by the nose-guard of his helmet and, with all his strength, dragged him in among the Britons. Eldol, overflowing with joy, gave a great shout: "Yahweh has given me what I wanted! Strike down the savages in front of you, men, cut them down! Victory's in your hands. You've won, now that Hengist's beaten."

> **In the Arthur story:** Gorlois is the husband of Arthur's mother. The French *Merlin* and Malory leave him unnamed, the duke of Tintagel, and Malory makes him the father of Igraine's three daughters, Morgause, Elaine and Morgan le Fay, Arthur's half-sisters.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

All the while, the Britons kept up their attack on the pagans, charging again and again. When they were driven back, they rallied and stood again. They didn't let up until they'd won.

<a id="page-137"></a>[\[Giles 137\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-137) The Saxons fled wherever their rush carried them. Some made for the cities, some for the wooded hills and some for their ships. Hengist's son Octa went to York with the larger part of them. His relative Eosa made for the city of Alclud, and fortified it with countless armed men.

### Bishop Eldad judges Hengist as Samuel judged Agag

After his victory Aurelius took Kaerconan, the town I named above, and stayed there three days. Meanwhile he had the dead buried, the wounded cared for and the tired rested, and refreshed with all kinds of comforts. Then he called the leaders together and told them to decide what to do with Hengist.

Eldad, bishop of Gloucester, Eldol's brother, a man of the greatest wisdom and faith, was there. When he saw Hengist standing in front of Aurelius, he told the others to be quiet, and spoke like this: "Even if everyone tried to set this man free, I'd cut him to pieces. I'd follow the prophet Samuel. He had Agag, king of Amalek, in his power, and hacked him apart, saying, 'You've robbed mothers of their children, so today I'll make yours childless among women.' So do the same with Hengist, who's another Agag."[^137-1]

So Eldol took his sword, led Hengist out of the city, cut off his head and sent him to the underworld. But Aurelius, moderate in everything as he was, had him buried and a mound of earth heaped over his body, in the pagan way.

### Octa gives himself up at York, and Eldad pleads for mercy

<a id="page-138"></a>[\[Giles 138\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-138) Next Aurelius led his army to York, to defeat Hengist's son Octa. Octa, besieged there, doubted he could hold out and defend the city against such a host. So after talking it over, he came out with the noblest of the men with him, carrying a chain in his hand and sand on his head.[^138-1] He presented himself to the king with these words.

"My gods are beaten, and I can't doubt that Yahweh, your god, rules. It's made so many noblemen come to you like this. So take us, and this chain. Unless you show mercy, keep us bound. We're ready, of our own will, for any punishment."

Aurelius was moved to pity, and called for a judgement on what to do with them. The leaders didn't agree. Then Bishop Eldad stood up to give his opinion: "The Gibeonites came to the Israelites of their own will, asked for mercy and got it.[^138-2] Should we Christians be worse than the Jews, and refuse it? They ask for it, so let them have it. The island of Britain is wide, and empty in many places. Let's make a treaty with them, and let them at least live where nobody else does, and serve us forever."

The king agreed with Eldad, and had mercy on them. Moved by Octa's example, Eosa and the others who'd fled came too, and were pardoned. So he gave them the region beside Scotland, and made a treaty with them.

### Aurelius restores the kingdom and mourns the lords at Kaercaradoc

<a id="page-139"></a>[\[Giles 139\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-139) With the enemy beaten, the king called the earls and princes of the kingdom to York, and ordered them to restore the churches the Saxons had destroyed. He himself began rebuilding the archbishop's cathedral there, and the province's other bishops' seats.

Fifteen days later, after putting builders of every kind to work in different places, he went to London, which the enemy's raids hadn't spared. Saddened by its ruin, he called back what citizens were left from everywhere, and set about restoring it. There he put his kingdom in order and revived the laws that had lapsed. He gave back to the grandsons what their grandfathers had lost. Estates whose heirs had died in that great disaster he handed to his comrades in arms. His whole mind was on restoring the kingdom, reforming the churches, renewing peace and law, and making justice work.

Then he went to Winchester to restore it like the rest. Once its rebuilding was under way, Bishop Eldad advised him to go to the monastery near Kaercaradoc, now called Salisbury, where the earls and princes wicked Hengist had betrayed were buried.[^139-1] There was a house of three hundred monks there on Mount Ambrius, who they say founded it long ago. As Aurelius looked around the place where the dead lay, he was moved to pity and burst into tears. Then, falling into all kinds of thoughts, he considered how to make the place memorable. He thought the turf that covered so many noblemen who'd died for their country deserved to be remembered.

### Merlin tells Aurelius to send for the Giants' Dance in Ireland

<a id="page-140"></a>[\[Giles 140\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-140) So he called together carpenters and stonemasons from everywhere, ordering them to use their minds. They were to invent a new kind of building, to stand forever in memory of such men. They all doubted their own skill and refused.

Then Tremounus, archbishop of the City of the Legions, came to Aurelius and said, "If anyone can take on your order, it's Merlin, Vortigern's prophet. I don't think there's another man in your kingdom with a brighter mind, whether at predicting the future or at building machines. Have him come and use his gifts, so the work you want will stand."

Aurelius asked plenty of questions about him, then sent messengers through the different regions of the country to find him and bring him back. They travelled the provinces and found him in the land of the Gewisse, at the spring of Galabes, where he often used to go. They told him what they wanted and brought him to the king. Aurelius was delighted to see him, and told him to tell the future, eager to hear wonders.

Merlin said, "Secrets like these mustn't be revealed unless there's the greatest need. If I told them to amuse people or to show off, the spirit that teaches me would fall silent, and leave me when I needed it."

Since he'd refused everyone, the king wouldn't press him about the future, and spoke to him about the work he had in mind. Merlin said, "If you want to honour these men's graves with something that lasts for all time, send for the Giants' Dance on Mount Killaraus in Ireland.[^140-1] There's a building of stones there that nobody of this age could raise, unless knowledge helped his skill. They're huge, and no man's strength is a match for them. If they're set up around this place the way they stand there, they'll last forever."

> **In the Arthur story:** The Giants' Dance is Stonehenge, and Geoffrey is the first to tie it to Merlin. Wace and Layamon retell how Merlin brought its stones from Ireland, and the French *Merlin* has him raise them at Salisbury over the Britons killed in battle. Malory leaves the stones out.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

### Merlin says the stones heal, and Uther sails for Ireland

<a id="page-141"></a>[\[Giles 141\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-141) At Merlin's words Aurelius burst out laughing. "How could it be done," he said, "bringing such great stones from so far a kingdom, as if Britain had none good enough for the work?"

Merlin said, "Don't laugh, my lord king. I'm not just talking. They hold a mystery, and heal all kinds of sickness. Long ago giants brought them from the far end of Africa and set them up in Ireland while they lived there. They meant to make baths among them when they fell ill. They'd wash the stones and pour the water into the pools, and the sick were cured. They mixed it with herbs too, and the wounded healed. There isn't one there without some healing power."

When the Britons heard this, they decided to send for the stones, and to make war on the people of Ireland if they held them back. In the end Uther Pendragon, the king's brother, was chosen to see to the business, with fifteen thousand armed men. Merlin himself went too, so it could all be handled with his knowledge and advice. Once their ships were ready, they put to sea and reached Ireland with good winds.

### Uther fights Gillomanius, and Merlin takes down the Giants' Dance

At that time Gillomanius, a young man of remarkable courage, was king of Ireland. When he heard the Britons had landed in Ireland, he gathered a large army and went to meet them.

When Gillomanius learned why they'd come, he laughed in front of his men and said, "No wonder a cowardly people like the Saxons could lay waste the island of the Britons, when they're such idiots.

<a id="page-142"></a>[\[Giles 142\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-142) "Who's ever heard of anything so stupid? Are the rocks of Ireland better than Britain's, that our kingdom should be challenged to fight for them? Arm yourselves, men, and defend your country. While I'm alive, they won't take even the smallest stone of the Dance from us."

When Uther saw them ready for battle, he charged them at full speed with his army. The Britons won at once. They cut the Irish to pieces and killed them, and put Gillomanius to flight. After their victory they made for Mount Killaraus, and when they reached the building, they were overjoyed and amazed.

With everyone standing around it, Merlin came up and said, "Put your backs into it, young men. In taking these stones down, you'll find out whether knowledge gives way to strength, or the other way around."

At his order they all set to work together with every kind of gear, and tried to take the Dance down. Some got ropes ready, some cords and some ladders, but they couldn't manage it at all. When they all failed, Merlin burst out laughing and put his own machines together. With everything it needed in place, he brought the whole ring down more easily than anyone would believe. Then he had them carried to the ships and loaded aboard, and they sailed back towards Britain, delighted. With fair winds they landed, and got ready to raise the stones over the men's graves.

When Aurelius was told, he sent messengers all over Britain. He ordered the clergy and the people summoned to gather on Mount Ambrius, to honour the burial ground with joy and ceremony. At his order the bishops and abbots came, and men of every rank who were under him. When everyone gathered on the appointed day, Aurelius put the crown on his head and celebrated the feast of Pentecost like a king, and spent the next three days celebrating without a break.[^142-1]

<a id="page-143"></a>[\[Giles 143\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-143) Meanwhile he gave the lands and offices that stood empty to the men of his household, to reward them for their service. The two archbishops' seats, York and the City of the Legions, had lost their shepherds. Wanting to provide for them as the people advised, he gave York to Samson, a famous man known for his great devotion. Yahweh had chosen Dubricius to do the City of the Legions good, so Aurelius put Dubricius there.

Once the king settled these and other matters in his kingdom, he told Merlin to raise the stones brought from Ireland around the burial ground. Merlin obeyed, and set them up around it just as they'd stood on Mount Killaraus in Ireland, and proved that knowledge beats strength.

### Pasgen, Vortigern's son, brings the Germans against Aurelius

At the same time Pasgen, Vortigern's son, who'd fled to Germany, was stirring up every fighting man of that kingdom against Aurelius Ambrosius, since he wanted to avenge his father.[^143-1] He promised them endless gold and silver if he brought Britain under him with their help. Once his promises won over all the young men, he got a great fleet ready, landed in the north of the island and began laying it waste. When this was reported to the king, he gathered his army, went to meet them and challenged the raging enemy to battle. They came to the fight willingly and took on the citizens, but by Yahweh's will they were beaten and put to flight.

### Pasgen returns with Gillomanius, and Eopa offers to poison Aurelius

<a id="page-144"></a>[\[Giles 144\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-144) Routed, Pasgen didn't dare go back to Germany. He turned his sails, went to Gillomanius in Ireland and was taken in. When he told the king his bad luck, Gillomanius felt sorry for him and promised him help, complaining of the wrong done him by Uther, the brother of Aurelius, when he came for the Giants' Dance. At last they made a treaty, got their ships ready, boarded them and landed at St Davids.

When this got out, Uther Pendragon raised a force of armed men and went into Wales to fight them. His brother Aurelius lay sick in the city of Winchester and couldn't go himself. When Pasgen, Gillomanius and the Saxons with them found this out, they were overjoyed, thinking the kingdom of Britain would be easy to conquer because of the king's illness.

While the people were muttering about it, one of the Saxons, named Eopa, went up to Pasgen and said, "What gifts will you give the man who kills Aurelius Ambrosius for you?"

Pasgen said, "If only I could find someone with that in mind! I'd give him a thousand pounds of silver, and my friendship as long as I live. If luck lets me win the kingdom's crown, I'll make him a centurion too, and swear to it."[^144-1]

Eopa said, "I've learned the British language, and I know the people's ways. I'm skilled in medicine. So if you'll do what you promise, I'll pretend to be Christian and British. I'll get in to see the king as if I were a doctor, and make him a drink he'll die of.

<a id="page-145"></a>[\[Giles 145\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-145) "To get in more quickly, I'll pass myself off as a very devout monk, learned in all the Church's teaching."

After Eopa said this, Pasgen made a pact with him, and swore to keep his side of it. So Eopa shaved his beard and the crown of his head, and put on the habit. Loaded with his jars of medicines, he set out for Winchester. When he got into the city at last, he offered his services to the king's attendants, and won their favour, since there was nothing they were looking for more than a doctor. He was welcomed and brought to Aurelius, and promised him his health back if the king would try his remedies. At once he was told to make one. He mixed poison into it, and handed it over.

Aurelius took it and drank it down. Straight away the cursed savage told him to lie under his covers and sleep, so the hateful drink would work better. The king did just as Eopa said, and fell asleep as if he'd wake up well. The poison soon spread through the pores and veins of his body, and death followed his sleep, the end that spares nobody. Meanwhile the wicked Eopa slipped away among the crowd, and was nowhere to be found at court.

While this was happening at Winchester, a star of wonderful size and brightness appeared, with a single ray. At the end of it was a ball of fire spread out in the shape of a dragon. From its mouth came two beams. One of them seemed to stretch beyond the lands of Gaul, and the other, curving towards the Irish Sea, ended in seven smaller rays.

### A comet appears, and Merlin reads it for Uther

<a id="page-146"></a>[\[Giles 146\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-146) When the star appeared, everyone who saw it was struck with fear and wonder. Uther, the king's brother, who was leading his army into Wales against the enemy, was badly frightened too. He went to every wise man he could find, to have them tell him what the star meant. Among the others, he had Merlin called, since Merlin had come along too, so the war could be run with his advice. When Merlin was brought in and stood in front of the commander, he was told to explain what the star meant.

He burst into tears at once. When he got his breath back, he cried out, "What a loss, and nothing can make it good! The people of Britain are orphans. Aurelius Ambrosius, the noblest and most famous king of the Britons, is dead, and with that death we'll all die, unless Yahweh sends help. So hurry, my lord Uther, go quickly, and don't put off fighting the enemy. Victory will be in your hands, and you'll be king of all Britain. That star means you, and so does the fiery dragon under it. The ray stretching towards Gaul means you'll have a son, mighty above all, whose power will hold every land it reaches. The other means a daughter. Her descendants will rule Britain one after another."

### Uther fights Pasgen and Gillomanius near St Davids

Uther wasn't sure Merlin had told the truth, but he kept marching against the enemy as he'd begun. He'd come within half a day's march of St Davids. When Gillomanius, Pasgen and the Saxons with them heard he was coming, they went out to meet him.

<a id="page-147"></a>[\[Giles 147\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-147) As soon as they caught sight of each other, both armies drew up their lines, closed in and fought at close quarters. Soldiers died on both sides, as happens in such a battle. At last, late in the day, Uther got the upper hand, killed Gillomanius and Pasgen, and won. The barbarians fled and hurried to their ships, but the citizens chased them and cut them down as they ran. So, with Christ's favour, the victory was entirely the commander's. After so much hard work, he went back to Winchester as fast as he could.

Messengers had come to tell him the king was dead, and that the country's bishops had already buried him near the monastery of Ambrius, inside the Giants' Dance, which he'd ordered made while he lived. On news of his death, the abbots and all the clergy of that province gathered with them in the city of Winchester, and gave him a funeral fit for so great a king. Since he'd ordered while still alive that he be laid in the cemetery he'd made ready, they carried his body there, and put it in the ground with royal rites.

### Uther is made king and has two golden dragons made

Then Uther, his brother, called together the clergy and people of the kingdom and took the island's crown. Everyone agreed, and Uther was raised to the throne. Remembering how Merlin had explained the star, he ordered two dragons made of gold, like the one he'd seen at the end of the star's ray. When they were finished with wonderful skill, he gave one to the cathedral of Winchester, and kept the other for himself, to carry into battle.

<a id="page-148"></a>[\[Giles 148\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-148) From that day on he was called Uther Pendragon, which in the British language means "dragon's head." He took the name because Merlin had predicted through the dragon that he'd be king.[^148-1]

### Octa and Eosa break the treaty, and Gorlois advises a night attack

Meanwhile Hengist's son Octa and his relative Eosa, thinking themselves freed from the treaty they'd made with Aurelius Ambrosius, set about making trouble for Uther and ravaging his lands.[^148-2] They took on the Saxons Pasgen had hired, and sent messengers to Germany for the rest. Backed by a huge host, Octa invaded the northern provinces, and kept up his rampage until he'd destroyed every city and fort from Albany to York.

At last, as he began to besiege York, Uther Pendragon came up with the kingdom's whole strength and fought him. The Saxons held out like men. They stood up to the Britons' charges and put them to flight, then chased them as they fell back, cutting them down, as far as Mount Damen, until the sun went down. That mountain was steep, with a hazel wood on its top, and in its middle sheer rocks where wild animals had their dens. The Britons took it, and stayed all night among the boulders and thickets.

In the small hours, when the stars had begun to wheel, Uther had his earls and princes called to him, to work out with their advice how to attack the enemy.[^148-3] They all gathered quickly in front of the king. He told them to say what they advised, and they had Gorlois, duke of Cornwall, give his opinion first, since he was a man of great judgement and ripe age.

"There's no need," he said, "for empty talk or long speeches, while we see there's still some night left. We have to use our daring and strength, if you want to enjoy life and freedom any longer.

<a id="page-149"></a>[\[Giles 149\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-149) "The pagans are many, and eager to fight, and we're fewer. If we wait for daylight, I don't think it'll pay us to take them on. Come on, then! While it's still dark, let's go down in close ranks and fall on them in their camp with a sudden charge. They suspect nothing, and won't expect us to come this way. If we all rush them together and dare it, we'll win, no doubt about it."

The king and the rest liked his plan, and they followed his advice. Formed into troops and armed, they made for the enemy camp, set on charging them as one. But as they came near, the lookouts saw them coming, and woke their sleeping comrades with the sound of horns. Thrown into confusion and dazed, some of the Saxons hurried to arm themselves, while others, gripped by fear, fled wherever their feet took them.

The Britons, marching in close ranks, reached the camp quickly and attacked. They found a way in, and ran at the enemy with drawn swords. Caught by surprise like this, the Saxons didn't fight back like men, while the Britons had come ready and bold. They pressed the attack fiercely, bent on slaughter, and killed the pagans by the thousand. At last Octa and Eosa were captured, and the Saxons scattered completely.

### Uther falls in love with Igraine at his Easter feast

After that victory Uther went to the city of Alclud, put that province in order and renewed the peace everywhere. He toured all the lands of the Scots too, and tamed that rebellious people.

<a id="page-150"></a>[\[Giles 150\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-150) He did more justice in his kingdom than any king who'd ruled ahead of him, so in his day all criminals were afraid, since they were punished without mercy. When the northern provinces were at peace, he went to London, and had Octa and Eosa kept in prison there.

As Easter came, he ordered the kingdom's nobles to gather in that city, so he could wear his crown and celebrate the great day with honour.[^150-1] They all obeyed, coming from their different cities, and gathered as the feast drew near. So the king held the celebration as he'd planned, and enjoyed himself with his nobles. Everyone was in high spirits, since Uther had welcomed them so warmly. Plenty of noblemen had come with their wives and daughters, all worthy of such a happy gathering.

Gorlois, duke of Cornwall, was there with his wife Igraine, whose beauty was greater than any other woman's in all Britain. When the king caught sight of her among the others, he suddenly burned with love for her, so much that he forgot everything else and turned all his attention to her. She was the only one he kept sending dishes to, and he sent her golden cups by his close friends. He smiled at her often, and slipped joking words in among his talk.

> **In the Arthur story:** Igraine is Arthur's mother in every telling. Wace, Layamon, the French *Merlin* and Malory all tell of her night with Uther in her husband's shape, and Malory has her learn the truth only after Uther marries her.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

When her husband saw this, he grew angry at once, and left the court without permission. Nobody could have called him back, since he was afraid of losing what he loved most. Furious, Uther ordered him to come back, so he could get satisfaction from him for the insult. When Gorlois refused to obey, the king lost his temper, and swore he'd lay waste the duke's land unless Gorlois hurried to make amends.

With that anger still between them, Uther soon gathered a great army, marched into Cornwall and set fire to its cities and towns. Gorlois didn't dare meet him, since he had fewer armed men. So he chose to strengthen his defenses until he could get help from Ireland. He was more anxious for his wife than for himself, so he put her in the fort of Tintagel, by the sea, which he thought the safest refuge.

> **In the Arthur story:** Geoffrey says only that Arthur was conceived at Tintagel, but later tellings make it his birthplace, and the Tristan romances make it King Mark's castle. The ruined castle on the headland today was built in 1233 by Richard, Earl of Cornwall, because of Geoffrey's story.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

<a id="page-151"></a>[\[Giles 151\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-151) Gorlois himself went into the castle of Dimilioc, so that if disaster came, they wouldn't both be in danger at once. When the king was told, he marched on Dimilioc, besieged it and cut off every way in.

A week passed. Then, remembering his love for Igraine, Uther said to one of his close friends, named Ulfin of Ricaradoch, "I'm burning with love for Igraine. I don't think I can be happy, or escape the danger to my own body, unless I have her. So give me advice on how to get what I want, or the torment inside me will kill me."

> **In the Arthur story:** Ulfin is Uther's friend and go-between in the French *Merlin*, where he's called Urfin, and in Malory he's Sir Ulfius, the knight Uther sends to find Merlin.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

Ulfin said, "Who could help you, when no force can get us to her in the fort of Tintagel? It stands in the sea, surrounded by it on every side, and the only way in is along a narrow ledge of rock. Three armed men could hold that against you, even if you stood there with the whole kingdom of Britain. But if Merlin would set his mind to it, I think he could get you what you want."

Uther believed him, and ordered Merlin called, since he'd come to the siege too.[^151-1] Merlin came at once. When he stood in front of the king, he was told to give advice so Uther could have his desire for Igraine. Seeing the torment Uther was in, Merlin was moved by how much he loved her.

Merlin said, "To get your wish, you'll have to use new arts, unheard of in your day. I know how to give you Gorlois's shape with my drugs, so you'll look just like him in every way. If you'll do as I say, I'll make you exactly like him, and Ulfin like Jordan of Tintagel, his close friend. I'll take another form myself and come as the third. You'll be able to go safely into the fort to Igraine, and be let in."

The king did as he said, and put his whole mind to it. At last he left the siege to his close friends, gave himself up to Merlin's drugs and was turned into Gorlois's double. Ulfin was changed into Jordan, and Merlin into Bricel, so none of them looked like who they'd been.

<a id="page-152"></a>[\[Giles 152\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-152) Then they set out for Tintagel, and reached the fort at dusk. The porter was told at once that the duke had come, so the gates were opened and the men let in. What else would have happened, when they all thought it was Gorlois?

Igraine took Uther for her husband. He'd tricked her with the false shape he'd put on, and with lies he dressed up well. He told her he'd slipped out of the besieged castle to make sure his fort and the woman he loved so much were safe. Believing him, she refused him nothing he asked. So Uther stayed that night with Igraine, and had what he'd longed for. That same night she conceived the famous Arthur, who later won his fame by his remarkable courage.

### Gorlois rides out against the besiegers, and Uther takes Tintagel and Igraine

Meanwhile, when the army outside Dimilioc found the king wasn't there, it acted without thinking. The soldiers tried to break down the walls, and challenged the duke inside to battle. He was just as reckless, and came out with his comrades, believing he could hold off so many armed men with a small band. As they fought on both sides, Gorlois was among the first killed, and his followers were scattered. The castle they'd besieged was taken too, and the wealth found inside wasn't shared out fairly, since everyone grabbed with greedy claws whatever luck and strength handed them.

When this bold work was done at last, messengers came to Igraine to tell her of the duke's death and how the siege had ended. But when they saw the king sitting beside her looking like Gorlois, they blushed, amazed that the duke they'd seen fall at Dimilioc had got there unharmed. They didn't know what drugs Merlin had made.

<a id="page-153"></a>[\[Giles 153\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-153) At such news Uther smiled, and put his arms around the duchess, saying, "I'm not dead at all, as you see. I'm alive. But I'm sorry my castle's been destroyed, and my comrades killed. So we have to fear the king will come and catch us here. I'll go out first to meet him and make peace with him, so nothing worse happens to us."

So he went out and made for his army, and putting off Gorlois's shape, became Uther Pendragon again. When he learned all that happened, he was sorry for Gorlois's death, but glad that Igraine was free of her marriage. Back at Tintagel, he captured the fort, and Igraine with it, and had his wish. From then on they lived together, bound by great love, and had a son and a daughter. The son's name was Arthur, and the daughter's Anna.

> **In the Arthur story:** Anna is Arthur's sister. The romances call her Morgause and make her Igraine's daughter by her first husband, Arthur's half-sister, and in Malory she's the mother of Gawain and his brothers, and of Mordred by Arthur himself.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

### Octa and Eosa escape and come back, and Lot leads the Britons

As days and seasons passed, the king fell ill, and the illness troubled him for a long time.[^153-1] Meanwhile the guards of the prison where Octa and Eosa, the two I mentioned above, were living out a weary life, fled with them to Germany, and spread fear through the kingdom. Rumour said they'd already stirred up Germany and got a vast fleet ready, to come back and destroy the island. That's just what happened. They came back with those ships and countless allies, and landed in Albany. There they attacked its cities and people with fire and slaughter.

So the army of Britain was put under Lot of Lothian, to keep the enemy far off. He was an earl and a very bold knight, mature in wisdom and years.[^153-2] Because of his courage, the king had given him his daughter Anna, and the care of his kingdom while he was ill.

> **In the Arthur story:** Lot is Arthur's brother-in-law in every telling. Malory makes him King Lot of Lothian and Orkney, one of the kings who rise against the young Arthur, and the father of Gawain, Agravain, Gaheris and Gareth.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

<a id="page-154"></a>[\[Giles 154\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-154) When Lot went out against the enemy, they often drove him back, so that he took refuge in the cities. But more times than not he routed and scattered them, and forced them to flee, now to the woods, now to their ships. The fighting swung back and forth, so nobody knew who'd win. The Britons' pride did them harm, since they wouldn't lower themselves to obey the earl's orders. So they went out weaker, and couldn't beat the enemy pressing on them.

### Uther, sick, has himself carried against the enemy on a stretcher

When the king was told that nearly the whole island had been laid waste like this, he was angrier than his illness could stand. He ordered all the nobles to gather, so he could scold them for their pride and their weakness. When he saw them all in front of him, he insulted them and scolded them hard, and swore he'd lead them against the Saxons himself. So he had a stretcher made to carry him, since his illness let him travel no other way.[^154-1] He told everyone to be ready too, to march on the enemy when the chance came. It was made at once, the army stood prepared, and the right day was at hand.

### Uther on his stretcher besieges the Saxons at Verulamium

They put the king on it and went to Verulamium, where the Saxons were crushing all the people. When Octa and Eosa learned the Britons had come, and Uther brought on a stretcher, they wouldn't lower themselves to fight him, since he'd come in a cart.

<a id="page-155"></a>[\[Giles 155\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-155) They said he was half dead, and that it wasn't right for such men to do battle with a man like that. So they withdrew into the city, and left the gates open, as if they feared nothing.

When Uther was told, he ordered Verulamium besieged at once and the walls attacked on every side. The Britons did as he said. Slaughtering the Saxons, they'd almost torn down the defenses and got in, when the Saxons at last began to fight back. With the citizens gaining the upper hand, the Saxons were sorry for the pride they'd started with, and set about defending themselves. They climbed the walls and drove the Britons back with every kind of weapon. At last, as both sides fought on, night came and called them all from their arms to rest.

Many longed for sleep, but more wanted a plan to destroy their enemies. When the Saxons saw their pride had harmed them while the Britons won, they decided to come out at dawn and challenge them to a battle in the open field. They did just that. As soon as the sun brought the day, they drew up their companies and came out to do what they'd planned.

Seeing this, the Britons divided their soldiers into troops, went to meet them and attacked first. The Saxons held their ground, the Britons charged, and there was slaughter on both sides. At last, late in the day, the king of the Britons had the victory. Octa and Eosa were killed, and the Saxons turned and ran.

Uther was so overjoyed that, though before he couldn't lift himself without help, he raised himself easily and sat up, as if he'd suddenly got well. He burst out laughing, and said in a cheerful voice, "These savages called me the half-dead king, because I lay sick on a stretcher, and so I was. But I'd rather beat them in that state than lose to them healthy and whole, and go on living after that. It's better to die with honour than to live in shame."

### The Saxons poison Uther's spring at Verulamium

<a id="page-156"></a>[\[Giles 156\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-156) Beaten as they were, the Saxons didn't give up their malice for that. They went into the northern provinces and harassed the people without letup. King Uther wanted to chase them, as he'd planned, but his princes talked him out of it, since a worse illness had taken hold of him after the victory. So the enemy grew bolder and pressed on, trying every way to bring the kingdom under them. Turning to betrayal, as they always did, they plotted how to kill the king by a trick. When nothing else worked, they decided to poison him, and that's what happened.

While he lay in the city of Verulamium, they sent envoys dressed as poor men to learn how things stood at court. Once they knew everything, they found one thing among the rest they chose for their betrayal. Near the hall there was a spring of very clear water, which the king used to drink, since his illness made him loathe anything else. The wicked traitors poisoned it all over, so all that flowed from it was spoiled. So when Uther drank from it, he soon died. A hundred men after him lost their lives too, until the trick was found out and the spring was filled in with earth.

When the king's death was made known, the bishops came with the kingdom's clergy. They carried his body to the monastery of Ambrius, and buried it with the rites of a king, inside the Giants' Dance beside Aurelius Ambrosius.

## Part 9: Arthur is crowned at fifteen, fights the Saxons, and holds court at Caerleon

### Dubricius crowns Arthur at fifteen, and he marches on Colgrin

<a id="page-157"></a>[\[Giles 157\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-157) Once Uther Pendragon was dead, the nobles of the Britons came together from their different provinces in the city of Silchester, and urged Dubricius, archbishop of the City of the Legions, to crown the king's son Arthur king.[^157-1] They had no choice. When the Saxons heard of the king's death, they'd invited their countrymen over from Germany, and under Colgrin they were trying to wipe the Britons out. They'd already taken the whole part of the island that runs from the river Humber to the sea of Caithness.

Heartbroken at his country's disaster, Dubricius called the bishops to him, and set the crown of the kingdom on Arthur's head. Arthur was fifteen, a young man of unheard-of courage and generosity. His goodness was born in him, and it won him such favour that nearly every people loved him. Once he had the crown, he kept the old custom and gave freely. So many knights came flocking to him that he ran out of things to give them.

But a man who's generous by nature, and brave as well, may be short for a while, yet never stays poor. Arthur's courage went with his generosity. So he decided to make war on the Saxons, to reward his household with their wealth. It was his right too, because he ought to hold the whole island by inheritance. He called up the young men under him and marched on York.

When Colgrin learned of it, he gathered the Saxons, the Scots and the Picts, and met Arthur with a huge army beside the river Douglas.[^157-2]

<a id="page-158"></a>[\[Giles 158\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-158) The two armies clashed there, and most of both came close to destruction. Still, Arthur won, chased the fleeing Colgrin, and besieged him once he was inside York.

When Baldulf heard his brother had fled, he made for the siege with six thousand men to free him. At the time of the battle he'd been on the coast, waiting for Duke Cheldric, who was coming from Germany to help them. When he was ten miles from the city, he decided to march by night and attack by surprise.

Arthur learned of the plan, and ordered Cador, duke of Cornwall, to meet him that same night with six hundred horsemen and three thousand foot soldiers. Cador found the road the enemy were taking and fell on them when they didn't expect it. He cut the Saxons to pieces, killed them, and made the rest run.

> **In the Arthur story:** Cador of Cornwall stays Arthur's ally in Wace and Layamon. In Malory he's Sir Cador of Cornwall, who welcomes the war with Rome, and his son Constantine takes the crown after Arthur.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

Baldulf was desperate that he couldn't help his brother, and wondered how he could talk to him. He thought the two of them could work out a way to save themselves, if only he could get to him. There was no other way in. So he shaved his hair and beard and dressed as a musician with a harp. He walked around the camp playing the tunes he made up on it, and passed for a harper.

Nobody suspected him. Little by little Baldulf got close to the city's defenses, keeping up the act he'd begun. At last the men inside recognised him. They pulled him up on ropes and took him to his brother. Seeing him again, Colgrin hugged and kissed him, and was himself again.

After talking it over every way, they'd given up hope of getting out, when their envoys came back from Germany. They'd brought Duke Cheldric to Albany with six hundred ships full of strong soldiers. When Arthur's advisers heard this, they urged him not to hold the siege any longer, since he'd be risking a battle he might lose if so huge an enemy army came.

### Arthur withdraws to London, and Hoel brings fifteen thousand men from Brittany

<a id="page-159"></a>[\[Giles 159\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-159) So Arthur took his household's advice, and withdrew into London. There he called together the clergy and the leading men of all his lands. He asked them what was best and safest to do against the pagans' raids. At last they all agreed, and messengers were sent to Brittany, to King Hoel, to tell him of Britain's disaster.

> **In the Arthur story:** Hoel stays Arthur's nephew and ally from Brittany in Wace and Layamon. In Malory he's King Howell of Brittany, the father of the Isolde of the White Hands that Tristan marries.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

Hoel was the son of Arthur's sister by Dubricius, king of the Britons of Brittany.[^159-1] When he heard of the attacks on his uncle, he ordered his fleet made ready and gathered fifteen thousand armed men. With the next good wind he landed at Hamo's Port. Arthur welcomed him with the honour he was owed, and they hugged again and again.

### Arthur fights the Saxons at Lincoln and Celidon Wood, and they break their treaty

A few days later they marched on the city of Kaerliudcoit, which the pagans I mentioned above were besieging. It stands on a hill between two rivers, in the province of Lindsey, and its other name is Lincoln. Once they'd arrived with their whole army, they fought the Saxons and slaughtered them in numbers never heard of. Six thousand of them fell that day. Some drowned in the rivers, and spears killed the rest.

The others were stunned, gave up the siege and fled. Arthur kept after them until they reached Celidon Wood.[^159-2] There they gathered from every side as they ran, and tried to stand against Arthur.

<a id="page-160"></a>[\[Giles 160\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-160) Battle was joined, and they defended themselves bravely and killed many Britons. The trees helped them, since they kept off the Britons' spears. When Arthur saw that, he had that part of the forest cut down, and the trunks laid in a ring, so there was no way out. He meant to keep them shut in until they starved. Once that was done, he had his troops surround the wood, and stayed there three days.

The Saxons had nothing to eat. So they wouldn't die of hunger, they offered terms: they'd leave all their gold and silver behind, and go back to Germany with only their ships. They also promised to send him tribute from Germany, and to leave hostages. Arthur took his advisers' advice, and agreed. He kept their wealth, and the men they'd given as pledges, and granted them nothing but leave to go.

But as they plowed the waves on their way home, they were sorry they'd made the bargain. They turned their sails, came back to Britain and landed on the shore at Totnes. Once ashore, they plundered the land as far as the Severn sea, killing the farmers. From there they marched towards the district of Bath,[^160-1] and besieged the city.[^160-2]

When the king was told, he was more amazed than he could say at what they'd done. He ordered judgement passed on their hostages, and had them hanged without delay. He dropped the war he'd begun on the Scots and the Picts, and hurried to break the siege. It tormented him that he was leaving his nephew Hoel sick in the city of Alclud.

At last he came into Somerset, and when he saw the besiegers close up, he said, "These Saxons, the most wicked of men, whose very name is hateful, didn't care to keep their word to me. I'll keep faith with my god, and today I'll try to make them pay for my countrymen's blood. Arm yourselves, all of you, and go at these traitors bravely. With Christ's help, we'll beat them for sure."

### Dubricius preaches to the army, and Arthur arms with Excalibur against the Saxons

<a id="page-161"></a>[\[Giles 161\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-161) As Arthur said this, Saint Dubricius, archbishop of the City of the Legions, climbed to the top of a hill and called out in a loud voice. "You men marked as Christians, keep your love for your countrymen and your homeland. The pagans' betrayal has wiped your people out, and they'll be your shame forever unless you set out to defend them. So fight for your country, and if death comes, take it willingly for its sake. That's victory itself, and the cure of the soul.

"Anyone who dies for their brothers gives themselves to Yahweh as a living sacrifice.[^161-1] That person surely follows Christ, who stooped to lay down life itself for others.[^161-2] So if any of you falls in this war, that death will be your penance and your pardon for all your sins, so long as you don't run from it."[^161-3]

Cheered at once by the holy man's blessing, they all hurried to arm themselves and do what Dubricius said. Arthur put on a coat of mail worthy of so great a king, and set on his head a gold helmet with a dragon carved on it. On his shoulders he carried his shield, called Pridwen, painted with an image of holy Mary, Christ's mother, which made him think of her often.[^161-4] At his side he wore Excalibur, the best of swords, forged on the island of Avalon.[^161-5] In his right hand was a spear called Ron, long and broad, made for slaughter.

> **In the Arthur story:** Geoffrey's Caliburnus is the sword the romances call Excalibur. In Malory the Lady of the Lake gives it to Arthur, and Bedivere throws it back into the water as Arthur dies.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

> **In the Arthur story:** Avalon is the island Arthur is carried to after his last battle, to be healed of his wounds. In Malory queens take him there in a barge, and Glastonbury's monks claimed it as their own once they found a grave they said was Arthur's in 1191.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

> **In the Arthur story:** In the Welsh tales Prydwen is Arthur's ship, which carries him to the otherworld in the poem *The Spoils of Annwn* and to Ireland in *Culhwch and Olwen*. Geoffrey gives its name to his shield, Pridwen.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

> **In the Arthur story:** Ron is short for Rhongomyniad, Arthur's spear in *Culhwch and Olwen* and the Welsh Triads.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

Then he set his troops in order, and boldly attacked the Saxons, who'd drawn up in wedges, as they always did. All day they held out bravely, charging the Britons again and again. At last, as the sun went down, they took the nearest hill to use as a camp. They trusted in their numbers, and that height alone seemed enough.

<a id="page-162"></a>[\[Giles 162\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-162) But when dawn brought back the day, Arthur climbed to the top with his army, and lost many of his men on the way up. The Saxons ran down at them from the summit, and wounded them more easily, since they came down faster than the Britons could climb. Still, the Britons reached the top with a huge effort, and fought them at close quarters. The Saxons met them face on and resisted with all their strength.

Most of the day went by like this. Arthur was furious that the Saxons were doing so well, and that the victory didn't come. So he drew Excalibur, shouted holy Mary's name, and threw himself fast into the thick of the enemy's lines. Calling on Yahweh, the king cut down each man within reach with a single blow. Arthur kept up the charge until four hundred seventy men lay dead from Excalibur alone.[^162-1]

When the Britons saw it, they followed him in close ranks, killing on every side. Colgrin and his brother Baldulf fell there, with many thousands of others. Seeing his men's danger, Cheldric turned and fled at once.

### Cador chases the Saxons, and Arthur hurries north to Hoel at Alclud

Once the king had won, he ordered Cador, duke of Cornwall, to go after them, while he hurried to Albany himself. He'd been told that the Scots and the Picts were besieging Hoel in the city of Alclud, where I said Hoel lay sick. So he rushed to help him, so the savages wouldn't take him.

The duke of Cornwall, with ten thousand men, didn't want to chase the fleeing Saxons yet. Instead he raced to get to their ships first, to keep them from boarding. As soon as he had the fleet, he put his best soldiers on it, to keep the pagans off if they ran there.

<a id="page-163"></a>[\[Giles 163\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-163) Then he went after the enemy, to kill without mercy all those he caught, as Arthur had ordered. The Saxons, who'd lately blazed with savage fury twice over, now fled in fear. They made for the hidden parts of the woods, or the mountains and caves, to live a little longer. At last, since nothing kept them safe, their tattered band crossed into the island of Thanet. The duke of Cornwall followed them there and slaughtered them as before. He didn't stop until he'd killed Cheldric, forced every man left to surrender and taken hostages.

### Arthur besieges the Scots and Picts on the islands of Loch Lomond

Once peace was made, Cador went to the city of Alclud, which Arthur had already freed from the savages.[^163-1] Then Arthur led his army to Moray, where the Scots and the Picts were besieged. They'd fought three times against the king and his nephew, and fled as far as that province. They went into Loch Lomond and occupied its islands, looking for a safe refuge.

The loch holds sixty of them and takes in sixty rivers, but only one of those runs out of it to the sea.[^163-2] On them, everyone knows, there are sixty rocks, each holding an eagle's nest. The eagles gathered every year, and with a loud cry all together, they gave notice of any wonder that was coming in the kingdom.

The enemy had fled to these islands so the loch would protect them, but it did them little good. Arthur gathered boats and went around the rivers. He besieged them for fifteen days, and starved them so badly that they died by the thousands.

While he was crushing them like this, Guillamurius, king of Ireland, arrived with a fleet and a huge force of savages to help the besieged. So Arthur dropped the siege and turned his arms against the Irish.

<a id="page-164"></a>[\[Giles 164\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-164) He cut them to pieces without mercy and forced them home. Once he'd won, he set out again to wipe out the Scots and the Picts, and gave himself up to a cruelty nothing could match.

Arthur spared nobody. So all the bishops of that miserable country came to him, with all the clergy under them, barefoot. They carried the saints' relics and the church's sacraments, and pleaded with the king for mercy for their people. As soon as they stood in front of him, they knelt and begged him to pity a people already crushed. He'd done them harm enough, they said, and there was no need to kill the few left to the last man. He should let them keep some small part of their country, and they'd bear the yoke of slavery forever, of their own free will. At their pleading, pity moved the king to tears. He gave in to the holy men's request and pardoned the people.

### Hoel is amazed by the loch, and Arthur tells him of two stranger pools

After that, Hoel looked over the loch, amazed that it held so many rivers, islands, rocks and eagles' nests.[^164-1] As he wondered at it, Arthur came up and told him there was another pool in the same province, more amazing still. It wasn't far from there, and it was about six metres wide, as long again, and a metre and a half deep. It was square, whether people or nature made it so. In its four corners it bred four kinds of fish, and none was ever found in a part but its own.

Arthur added that there was another pool in Wales, near the Severn, which the people of the place call Linligwan.[^164-2] When the sea flows into it, it swallows the water like a whirlpool. However many waves it takes in, it never fills up enough to cover its banks.

<a id="page-165"></a>[\[Giles 165\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-165) But when the tide ebbs, it spits out what it swallowed in a heap as high as a mountain, and floods and splashes its shores with it. Then, if anyone from that whole country stood close by facing it, the spray soaked their clothes, and they could hardly ever get away before the pool swallowed them. But with their backs to it, they had nothing to fear, even standing on the bank.

### Arthur keeps Christmas at York and rebuilds its ruined churches

Once he'd pardoned the Scots, the king went to York, to keep the feast of Christmas, which was coming.[^165-1] When he came into the city, he saw the churches in ruins, and it pained him. The Saxons had driven out the blessed Archbishop Samson and everyone in holy orders. In those half-burned buildings, no services for Yahweh were held anymore. That's how far the pagans' madness had gone.

So he called the clergy and the people together, and appointed his chaplain Pyramus to the archbishop's seat. He rebuilt the churches that had been knocked flat to the ground, and filled them with communities of monks and nuns. To the nobles the Saxons' raids had driven out, he gave back the lands and offices of their fathers.

### Arthur restores Auguselus, Urien and Lot, and marries Guinevere

There were three brothers there of royal birth, Lot, Urien and Auguselus, who'd ruled those parts before the Saxons got the upper hand. Arthur wanted to give them what their fathers had held, as he had the others. So he gave back to Auguselus the kingship of the Scots, and honoured his brother Urien with the rule of the people of Moray.

> **In the Arthur story:** Urien is Malory's King Uriens of Gore, the husband of Morgan le Fay and the father of Ywain. Welsh poems praise the real Urien, king of Rheged.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

> **In the Arthur story:** Auguselus is the king of Scotland who promises Arthur twenty thousand men against Rome in Malory too.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

<a id="page-166"></a>[\[Giles 166\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-166) Lot had married Arthur's sister in the days of Aurelius Ambrosius, and with her he'd fathered Gawain and Mordred.[^166-1] Arthur made him earl of Lothian again, with the other provinces that went with it.

> **In the Arthur story:** Gawain is Arthur's nephew in every telling, Gwalchmai in the Welsh tales and the hero of *Sir Gawain and the Green Knight*. In Malory he's the oldest of Lot's sons, whose feud with Lancelot breaks the Round Table.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

> **In the Arthur story:** Mordred is Arthur's nephew here, the traitor who takes his crown in Wace and Layamon. The French *Death of Arthur* and Malory make him Arthur's son by his half-sister.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

At last, once he'd brought the whole country back to its old dignity, Arthur married Guinevere.[^166-2] She was born of a noble Roman family, had been raised in Duke Cador's household, and was the most beautiful woman in the island.

> **In the Arthur story:** Guinevere is Arthur's queen in every telling. The French romances and Malory make her Lancelot's lover, and their love brings down the Round Table.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

### Arthur sails to Ireland and Iceland, and Gotland and Orkney submit to him

When the next summer came, he got his fleet ready and sailed to Ireland, which he wanted to bring under him.[^166-3] As soon as he landed, King Guillamurius, the one I mentioned above, met him with a countless people to fight him. Once the battle began, his people, naked and unarmed, were cut down terribly, and fled wherever they could find refuge. Before long Guillamurius was captured too, and forced to surrender. The other princes of the country were stunned, and followed their king and surrendered.

Once every part of Ireland was his, Arthur sailed his fleet to Iceland, beat its people and brought it under him. Then the rumour spread through the other islands that no land could stand against him. So Doldavius, king of Gotland, and Gunvasius of Orkney came of their own accord, promised tribute and submitted. When the winter was over, Arthur went back to Britain. He made the peace firm at home, and stayed there twelve years.

### Arthur's fame frightens Europe, and he wars on Norway and Gaul

<a id="page-167"></a>[\[Giles 167\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-167) Then he invited all the bravest men from distant kingdoms, and began to make his household larger. He kept such refined ways at his court that faraway peoples tried to match them. So every man of high birth was spurred on, and thought himself nothing unless he dressed and carried arms the way Arthur's knights did.

At last the fame of Arthur's generosity and courage spread to the farthest ends of the world. The rulers across the sea were struck with a great fear that he'd attack them, and they'd lose the peoples under them. Gnawed by worry, they rebuilt their cities and the towers on their walls, and built forts in the right places. So if Arthur attacked them, they'd have a refuge if they needed one.

When Arthur was told, he was proud that everyone feared him, and set his heart on bringing all Europe under him. He got his ships ready, and went first to Norway, to crown his brother-in-law Lot there. Lot was the nephew of Sichelm, the Norwegians' king, who'd died about then and left Lot his kingdom. But the Norwegians refused to take him. They'd already raised a man named Riculf to the throne, and had fortified their cities, and they thought they could hold out against Arthur.

At that time Gawain, Lot's son, was a boy of twelve. His uncle had sent him to serve in the household of Pope Sulpicius, who gave him his arms.

So, as I'd begun to say, when Arthur landed on the coast of Norway, King Riculf met him with all the people of the land and fought him. After plenty of blood was spilled on both sides, the Britons won out at last. They charged, and killed Riculf and many with him. Once they'd won, they set the cities on fire with heaped-up flames and scattered the country people.

<a id="page-168"></a>[\[Giles 168\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-168) They didn't stop their cruelty until they'd brought all Norway, and Denmark too, under Arthur's rule.

Once these were beaten and Arthur had raised Lot to be king of Norway, he sailed to Gaul. He formed his men into troops and began to lay the country waste far and wide. At that time Gaul was a province of Rome, put in the charge of the tribune Frollo, who ruled it under the emperor Leo.

When Frollo learned that Arthur had come, he gathered every armed soldier under his command, and fought him, but he couldn't hold out. The young men of all the islands Arthur had conquered went with him, so his army was said to be too big for anyone to beat. The better part of the knights of Gaul served him too, since his generosity had won them over.

So when Frollo saw he was getting the worst of the battle, he quickly left the field and fled to Paris with a few men. There he gathered his scattered people again, fortified the place, and set out to fight Arthur a second time. But while he was trying to make his army stronger with his neighbours' help, Arthur came on him suddenly and besieged him in the city.

A month went by, and Frollo couldn't bear to see his people starving. So he challenged Arthur to fight a duel with him alone, and the winner would take the other's kingdom. He was a tall man, bold and very strong. He trusted too much in these, and made the offer hoping it would save him.

When Arthur was told, Frollo's challenge pleased him enormously, and he answered that he was ready to keep the agreement. Both sides gave their pledges, and the two of them met on the island outside the city, with the people waiting to see what would happen to them.

Both were armed as they should be, and sat on horses of amazing speed, and nobody could easily tell which of them would win. They stood at opposite ends with their lances raised, then spurred their mounts at once and struck each other huge blows. But Arthur carried his more carefully.

<a id="page-169"></a>[\[Giles 169\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-169) He drove it into the top of Frollo's chest, dodged Frollo's weapon, and threw him to the ground as hard as his strength allowed. Arthur drew his sword and was hurrying to strike him, when Frollo got up fast and ran at him with his lance out. He gave Arthur's horse a deadly wound in the chest, and brought down mount and rider together.

When the Britons saw their king on the ground, they were afraid he'd been killed. They could barely be kept from breaking the pact and charging the Gauls as one. But just as they were about to cross the line of the truce, Arthur jumped up. He held his shield out in front of him, and ran at Frollo as Frollo came at him. Close together now, they hammered at each other, each set on killing the other.

At last Frollo found an opening and hit Arthur on the forehead. If the blade hadn't been blunted on the helmet, he might have given him a deadly wound. The blood flowed, and when Arthur saw his mail and shield turning red, he burned with a fiercer anger. He raised Excalibur with all his strength and brought it down through the helmet into Frollo's head, and split it in two. At that wound Frollo fell, beat the ground with his heels, and breathed his last.

When the news spread through the army, the citizens ran together, opened the gates and handed the city over to Arthur. Then, after his victory, he split his forces in two. He gave one half to Duke Hoel and ordered him to go and fight Guitardus, duke of the Poitevins, while Arthur set about conquering the other provinces with the rest.

Hoel soon marched into Aquitaine, attacked the country's cities, and wore Guitardus down in many battles until Guitardus surrendered. He laid Gascony waste with fire and sword too, and made its princes submit.

Nine more years went by. Once Arthur had brought every part of Gaul under his power, he came back to Paris and held court there. He summoned the clergy and the people. Then he settled the kingdom's affairs in peace, under the law. Then he gave Neustria, which is now called Normandy, to his butler Bedivere, and a province to his steward Kay.

> **In the Arthur story:** Bedivere is Bedwyr in the Welsh tales, always at Cei's side, and in Malory the last knight left with Arthur, who throws Excalibur back into the lake.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

> **In the Arthur story:** Kay is Cei, one of Arthur's first companions in the Welsh tales. In the French romances and Malory he's Sir Kay, Arthur's foster brother and steward, known for his sharp tongue.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

<a id="page-170"></a>[\[Giles 170\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-170) Kay's was Anjou.[^170-1] Arthur handed out plenty of other lands to the nobles who'd served him. At last, when every city and people was at peace, he went back to Britain as spring began.

### Arthur calls his kings to a Pentecost court in the City of the Legions

When the feast of Pentecost drew near, Arthur, overflowing with joy after such a triumph, wanted to hold court at once and set the crown of the kingdom on his head.[^170-2] He meant for the kings and dukes under him to come to the feast too, so he could keep it with due honour and renew a firm peace among his nobles. He told his close friends what he had in mind, and took their advice to do it in the City of the Legions.

It stands in Glamorgan, on the river Usk, not far from the Severn sea, in a lovely spot. It was richer than any other city, and suited to so great a feast. On one side the Usk flowed past it, so the kings and princes who'd be coming from overseas could arrive by ship. On the other, ringed by meadows and woods, it was splendid with royal palaces, whose golden roofs made it look like Rome.

It was famous for two churches. One, built in honour of the martyr Julius, was made beautiful by a choir of nuns given to Yahweh. The other, founded in the name of the holy Aaron, the martyr's companion, was served by a community of priests, and held the third archbishop's seat in Britain.

The city also had a school of two hundred scholars, learned in astronomy and the other arts. They watched the courses of the stars carefully, and by true reasoning they predicted to King Arthur the strange things that would come at that time.

<a id="page-171"></a>[\[Giles 171\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-171) Famous for so many delights, the city was made ready for the feast he'd announced. Envoys were sent to different kingdoms, and everyone who ought to come to court was invited, from Gaul as well as from the nearby islands of the Ocean.

So the guests arrived. Auguselus was there, king of Albany, which is now called Scotland. So was Urien, who ruled the people of Moray. With them was Cadwallon Lawhir, of the people of Gwynedd, known today as the North Welsh. Sater was there, ruler of the people of Dyfed, that is, the South Welsh, and Cador of Cornwall. The three archbishops came, from London, from York, and Dubricius from the City of the Legions. Dubricius was the pope's first envoy to Britain, and so famous for his holiness that his prayers healed anyone weighed down by sickness.

The earls of the great cities came. There were Morvid of Gloucester, Mauron of Worcester, Anaraut of Salisbury, and Arthgal of Cargueit, which is called Warguit. There were Jugein of Leicester, Cursalem of Kaicester, Kimmare, duke of Canterbury, Galluc of Salisbury, Urgennius of Bath, Jonathal of Dorchester and Boso of Ridoc, that is, Oxford. Besides the earls came heroes of no lower rank. There were Danaut Mappapo, Cheneus Mapcoil, Pereder Maberidur, Guisul Mapnogoit, Regin Mapclaut, Eddelein Mapeledauc and Kincar Mabbagan. There were Kimmare, Gorboniam Mapgoit, Clofaut, Rupmaneton, Kimbelim Maptrunat, Cathleus Mapcatel and Kinlich Mapneton, and plenty of others whose names would take too long to list.[^171-1]

From the nearby islands their kings came: Guillamurius of Ireland, Maleagant of Iceland, Doldavius of Gotland, Gunvasius of Orkney, Lot of Norway and Aschillius of the Danes. From overseas there were Holdinus, ruler of the Ruteni, Leodegarius, earl of Boulogne, and Bedivere the butler, who held Normandy now. With them were Borellus of Le Mans, Kay the steward, now lord of Anjou, and Guitardus of Poitou. The twelve peers of Gaul were there too, led by Guerinus of Chartres.[^171-2] Hoel, duke of the Britons of Brittany, was there as well, with the nobles under him.

<a id="page-172"></a>[\[Giles 172\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-172) They arrived with more show of jewels, mules and horses than anyone could describe. Besides these, not a prince of any worth on this side of Spain failed to answer the summons. That's no wonder. Arthur's generosity was known through the whole world, and had won everyone's love.

### Dubricius crowns Arthur, and the queen is led to her own church

Once everyone had gathered in the city and the feast had come, the archbishops were brought to the palace to crown Arthur. The court was held in Dubricius's diocese, so he took charge, ready to lead the service. Once Arthur was crowned, he was taken in state to the archbishop's church, with an archbishop holding him on each side. Four kings, of Albany, Cornwall, Dyfed and Gwynedd, whose right it was, walked ahead of him carrying four golden swords. A company of clergy of every order sang in front of him with wonderful music.

Meanwhile, archbishops and bishops led the queen, crowned with her own crown, to the church of the nuns given to Yahweh. The wives of the four kings carried four white doves ahead of her, as the custom was, and the women who were there followed her with great joy.

When the procession was over, there was so much music of organs and singing in both churches that the knights didn't know which to go to first, it was so sweet. They hurried in crowds, now to one, now to the other, and even if the whole day had been given to the service, it wouldn't have bored them.

When the masses in both churches were over, the king and queen took off their crowns, and put on lighter robes.

<a id="page-173"></a>[\[Giles 173\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-173) Then they went to eat, he to his palace with the men and she to another with the women. The Britons kept an old custom of Troy, and held their feast days apart, each sex with its own.

When everyone was seated as their rank required, Kay the steward, dressed in ermine, served the dishes. With him were a thousand young men of the highest birth, all wearing it too. On the other side, as many in squirrel fur followed Bedivere the butler, and with him poured drinks of all kinds into cups of every sort. In the queen's palace, countless servants, richly dressed, did their own service in their own way. If I went on to describe it all, I'd make the history far too long.

By then Britain had risen to such greatness that it outdid all other kingdoms in its wealth, its fine clothes and its people's courtesy. Every warrior there famous for his courage wore a single colour, in his robes and his armour. The elegant women dressed alike too, and wouldn't give their love until a suitor had proved himself three times in war. So the ladies were chaste, and the knights were better men for their love.

### The court holds games outside the city, and Arthur gives out lands and offices

When the meal was over, they went out to the fields beyond the city, to play at different games. Soon they staged a battle on horseback. The women watched from the tops of the walls, and the sport made them fan the riders' love into a mad flame. Others competed with javelins,[^173-1] with spears, throwing heavy weights or stones, at dice and at every other kind of game, and spent the rest of the day with no quarrel.

<a id="page-174"></a>[\[Giles 174\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-174) Arthur rewarded each winner with rich gifts. The first three days went by like this. On the fourth, everyone who served Arthur for the offices they held was called in. Each was given lands, cities, castles, dioceses, abbeys or other honours.

### Dubricius retires, and envoys bring a letter from Lucius Tiberius summoning Arthur to Rome

The holy Dubricius longed for a hermit's life, and gave up the archbishop's seat. In his place Saint David, the king's uncle, was consecrated. His life was an example of every goodness to those he'd taught. In place of Saint Samson, archbishop of Dol, Chelianus was appointed, a famous priest of Llandaff, with King Hoel of the Britons of Brittany agreeing, since Chelianus's life and good ways spoke for him. Silchester's bishop's seat was given to Mauganius, and that of Winchester to Diwanius. Alclud's went to Eledanius.

While Arthur was sharing these out, twelve men of mature age and solemn faces came in to the king. They carried olive branches in their right hands as the sign of envoys, and stepped forward slowly, speaking in measured voices.[^174-1] They greeted him, and handed him a letter from Lucius Tiberius, which said:

> **In the Arthur story:** Lucius Tiberius is the Roman ruler Arthur fights in Wace, Layamon, the English alliterative *Morte Arthure* and Malory's Book V, where he's called emperor.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

"Lucius Tiberius, procurator of the Republic, to Arthur, king of Britain, what he deserves.[^174-2] I'm amazed, deeply astonished, at how arrogant your tyranny is. I'm stunned, I say, at the wrong you've done Rome. When I think it over, I'm angry that you've gone so far beyond yourself that you refuse to see it. You don't hurry to consider what it means to offend with unjust acts the senate, which, as you know, the whole world owes service.

"The senators ordered you to pay the tribute of Britain, since Gaius Julius and other Romans of rank had it for a long time.

<a id="page-175"></a>[\[Giles 175\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-175) "Ignoring the command of so great a body, you've dared to keep it back. You've robbed us of Gaul as well. You've taken the province of the Allobroges, and all the islands of the Ocean, whose kings paid tribute to our ancestors while Rome's power held in those parts.

"So, since the senate has decided to seek justice for this heap of wrongs, I'm setting you a date, the middle of August next year, and I order you to come to Rome. There you'll satisfy your masters and accept the sentence their judgement has passed. If you don't, I'll come to your lands myself, and with swords I'll try to give the Republic back whatever your madness has taken from it."

Once it was read out in front of the kings and earls, Arthur withdrew with them into the giants' tower at the entrance, to decide what to do about such a demand. As they began to climb the stairs, Cador, duke of Cornwall, being a cheerful man, burst out laughing in front of the king.

"Until now," Cador said, "I've been afraid that the Britons, idle in a long peace, would grow lazy with leisure, and lose all their fame in war, which makes them more famous than other peoples. Nobody seems to use their arms anymore. Dice and the fire of women and other pleasures are all around. So there's no doubt that cowardice stains whatever there was of courage, honour, daring and fame. It's been nearly five years now that we've given ourselves to these delights and gone without the training of war. So Yahweh has stirred the Romans to this, to free us from laziness and bring our courage back to what it was."

As Cador said this and more like it to the others, they came at last to the seats, and when each of them had sat down, Arthur spoke to them.

### Arthur asks his lords' advice, and argues that Rome owes Britain tribute

<a id="page-176"></a>[\[Giles 176\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-176) "My comrades in bad times and good," Arthur said, "I've tested your courage up to now, both in giving advice and in waging war. So put your minds together now, and work out wisely what you think we should do about such a demand. When a wise man has looked ahead with care, what comes is easier to bear. So we'll face Lucius Tiberius's attack better if we plan together how to weaken it.

"I don't think we need fear it much, since his ground for demanding tribute from Britain makes no sense. He says it's owed him because it was paid to Julius Caesar and those who came after.
