The quarrels of the Britons of old invited the Romans in, and they landed in Britain with an army. While the country was shaken by its own troubles, they brought it under them by force and violence. Having got it that way, they had no right to its payments. Nothing gotten like that rightly belongs to whoever grabbed it. So his claim that we owe him by law makes no sense.

"But he dares to demand of us what's unjust. So let's ask Rome for tribute on the same grounds, and let the stronger take what he wanted. Julius Caesar and other Roman rulers once conquered Britain. If that's his reason for deciding we should pay now, then I rule the same way that Rome owes me. My ancestors held Rome long ago.

"Belinus, that most glorious king of the Britons, had the help of his brother Brennius, duke of the Allobroges.

<a id="page-177"></a>[\[Giles 177\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-177) "The two of them hanged twenty of the noblest Romans in the middle of the forum, took the city and kept it for a long time. Constantine, too, Helena's son, and Maximianus, both close relatives of mine, were crowned in Britain one after the other, and each won the throne of the Roman Empire. So don't you think we should demand tribute of the Romans? As for Gaul and the nearby islands of the Ocean, no answer's needed, since he didn't bother to defend them when we took them from Rome's power."

When Arthur had said this and more like it, Hoel, king of the Britons of Brittany, who'd been asked to speak first, answered him.

### Hoel urges the march on Rome and recalls the Sibyl's prophecy

"Even if each of us searched his whole mind," Hoel said, "and weighed all sides of every question, I don't think he could find better advice than what your good judgement has just laid out. Your thinking, sweetened with Cicero's honey, has seen well ahead for us.[^177-1] So we should never stop praising the feeling of a steady man, the work of a wise mind, the gain of the best advice.

"If you'll go to Rome on these grounds, I've no doubt we'll win. We'd be defending our freedom, and demanding of our enemies, as is only right, what they've begun to demand of us unjustly. Anyone who tries to take another's goods rightly loses their own to the one they attack. So since the Romans want to take our lands, we'll surely take Rome's, if we're given the chance to fight them.

"Here's the battle all the Britons should long for! Here are the Sibyl's prophecies, which bear true witness that a third man of British birth will be born to hold the Roman Empire.[^177-2] Two of them have been fulfilled already, as you said, in the famous princes Belinus and Constantine.

<a id="page-178"></a>[\[Giles 178\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-178) "It's plain that both ruled the Roman Empire. Now we have you as the third, and the high honour is promised to you. So hurry to take what Yahweh doesn't wait to give. Be quick to conquer what wants to be conquered. Rush to raise us all up. To see you raised, we won't run from wounds or from losing our lives. To help you do it, I'll go with you myself with ten thousand armed men."

### Auguselus longs to fight Rome and offers two thousand horsemen

Once Hoel finished, Auguselus, king of Albany, went on to say what he wanted in the matter. "Ever since I gathered that my lord wants what he's said," Auguselus said, "such joy has come into my heart that I can't put it into words here. We seem to have done nothing in all the wars we've fought against so many great kings. The Romans and the Germans stay unharmed, and we haven't bravely paid them back for the slaughter they once did on our people.

"But now that we're allowed to fight them, I'm overjoyed. I'm burning for the day we meet, and I thirst for their blood the way I'd long for a spring if I'd been kept from drinking for three days. Oh, if I see that day, how sweet the wounds will be, whether I take them or give them, when we come to blows! Even death will taste good, if I suffer it paying back our fathers, defending our freedom and raising up our king.

"So let's attack those half-men, and keep at them, so we beat them and take their honours in a happy victory. I'll add two thousand armed horsemen to our army, besides foot soldiers."

### The kings and lords pledge their men, and Arthur's army is counted

<a id="page-179"></a>[\[Giles 179\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-179) After the rest had said what needed saying, each promised Arthur the service he owed. Not counting the duke of Brittany's troops, sixty thousand men in full armour were counted from Britain alone. The other islands' kings weren't in the habit of keeping horsemen, so they pledged as many foot soldiers as each of them owed. From the six of them, Ireland, Iceland, Gotland, Orkney, Norway and Denmark, a hundred twenty thousand were counted.

From the lands of Gaul's dukes, the Ruteni, the Portunienses, the Estrusienses and the men of Le Mans, Anjou and Poitou, came eighty thousand. The twelve earls who'd come with Guerinus of Chartres brought twelve hundred. Altogether they numbered a hundred eighty-three thousand two hundred, besides the foot soldiers, who weren't easy to count.

### Arthur sends his kings home to raise their armies, and refuses Rome its tribute

Seeing them all ready to serve him as one, King Arthur ordered them to go home quickly and gather the armies they'd promised. They were to hurry to the port of the river Barba by the first of August, so they could march from there with him to the borders of the Allobroges and meet the Romans.

He sent a message to the emperors by their own envoys. He'd never pay them tribute, nor go to Rome to accept their sentence on it. Instead, he'd ask of them what they'd decided by their judgement to demand of him. So they departed, and so did the kings and the nobles. None of them was slow to do what he'd been ordered.

## Part 10: Arthur sails against Rome, fights a giant at Mont Saint-Michel, and meets Lucius

### Lucius gathers the kings of the East against Britain

<a id="page-180"></a>[\[Giles 180\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-180) Once Lucius Tiberius heard Arthur's answer, the senate had him call on the rulers of the East to raise armies and come with him to conquer Britain.[^180-1] They came quickly: Epistrophius of the Greeks, Mustensar of Africa, Alifantinam of Spain, Hirtacius of the Parthians, Boccus of the Medes, Sertorius of Libya, Serses of Iturea, Pandrasus of Egypt and Micipsa of Babylon, all kings; Polytetes of Bithynia and Teucer of Phrygia, both dukes; Evander of Syria, Aethion of Boeotia and Hippolytus of Crete; and with them the lords under each.[^180-2]

From the senate came Lucius Catellus, Marius Lepidus, Gaius Metellus Cotta, Quintus Milvius Catulus and Quintus Carutius, and so many others that forty thousand one hundred sixty were counted in all.

### Arthur leaves Britain to Mordred and Guinevere and sails from Southampton

When everything they needed was ready, they set out for Britain at the start of August. Arthur learned they were coming. He left Britain in the care of his nephew Mordred and Queen Guinevere,[^180-3] and marched with his army to Hamo's Port, where a good wind took him out to sea.

<a id="page-181"></a>[\[Giles 181\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-181) Ships past counting sailed around him. He was crossing the deep happily on a good course, when at about midnight he fell fast asleep.[^181-1] In a dream he saw a bear flying through the air, its growl shaking every shore. A terrible dragon flew in from the west, lighting up the country with the glare of its eyes, and met it in an amazing battle. Again and again it charged, until it burned its enemy with fiery breath and threw it to the ground.

When Arthur woke, he told the men around him what he'd dreamed. They explained that the dragon meant Arthur himself, and the bear some giant he'd fight. Their struggle was the battle to come, and its end Arthur's victory. But Arthur read it another way. He thought the dream had come to him because of the war between him and the emperor.

At last, after the night's sailing, dawn turned the sky red, and they landed at the port of the river Barba. They soon pitched their tents there, and waited for the kings of the islands and the dukes of the provinces to come.

### A giant carries Helena off to Mont Saint-Michel, and Arthur goes after him

Meanwhile Arthur was told that a giant of enormous size had come from the lands of Spain. He'd snatched Helena, Duke Hoel's niece, from her guards, and run off with her to the top of the mountain now called Mont Saint-Michel.[^181-2] The knights of the country had gone after him, but gotten nowhere. Whether they came at him by sea or by land, he either sank their ships with huge rocks or killed them with weapons of every kind. He caught many of them too, and ate them half alive.

> **In the Arthur story:** Arthur's fight with the giant of Mont Saint-Michel stays one of his famous victories. Wace, Layamon and the English alliterative *Morte Arthure* all tell it again, and in Malory's Book V Arthur climbs the mountain alone while Kay and Bedivere wait below.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

So the next night, two hours after dark, Arthur took Kay the steward and Bedivere the butler, left the tents without the others knowing, and set out for the mountain.

<a id="page-182"></a>[\[Giles 182\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-182) He was so strong that he didn't bother to lead an army against monsters like this. Going this way would put heart in his men, and he was enough by himself to destroy such creatures.

As they came close, they saw a fire burning on top of it, and another not far off, on a smaller hill. They weren't sure which of the two the giant lived on, so they sent Bedivere to find out. He found a little boat and sailed there first, since it stood in the sea and there was no other way to reach it.

As he began to climb, he heard a woman screaming above him. At first he shuddered, afraid the monster was there. He quickly got his courage back and drew his sword. On the summit he found nothing but the fire he'd seen from below. He also saw a grave freshly dug, and beside it an old woman crying and moaning. The moment she noticed him, she spoke through her sobs.

"You poor man," she said, "what bad luck has brought you here? You'll die a death too terrible to tell. I pity you, because tonight that hateful monster will destroy you, young as you are. That evil giant, whose very name people hate, will be here soon. He carried the duke's niece up this mountain, and me with her, the nurse who raised her. I've just buried her here. He'll kill you without a moment's wait, by some death never heard of.

"How cruel this is! My dear girl was so terrified, while that monster held her in his arms, that her heart gave out. She died, who deserved a much longer life. She was my other soul, a second life to me, my sweetest joy. Since he couldn't rape her, he burned with his filthy lust and forced himself on me against my will. Yahweh and my old age are my witnesses.

"Run, my dear man, get away! If he comes to have me again, the way he always does, and finds you here, he'll tear you to pieces."

Bedivere was as moved as anyone can be.

<a id="page-183"></a>[\[Giles 183\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-183) He calmed her with kind words, promised her that help would come quickly, and went back to Arthur and told him everything he'd found. Arthur was heartbroken for the girl. He told the two of them to leave the giant to him alone, but if they were needed, to come and help him and attack like men. Then they made for the larger mountain. They left their horses with the squires and climbed, Arthur in front.

The monster was at his fire, his face smeared with the blood of half-eaten pigs. He'd devoured some of them, and was roasting the rest on spits over the coals. He didn't expect anything like this. As soon as he saw them, he hurried to grab his club, which two young men could hardly have lifted off the ground. Arthur drew his sword, held his shield out in front of him, and ran as fast as he could to stop him before he got hold of it.

But the giant knew what Arthur meant to do, and already had it in his hands. He struck Arthur's shield so hard that the sound of the blow filled the shores, and it left the king half deaf. Burning with rage, Arthur raised his sword at the giant's forehead and wounded him. The wound wasn't deadly, because the giant had put his club in the way of the blow. But blood poured down into the monster's face and eyes, so he couldn't see.

Blinded, he rose up fiercer still. Like a boar charging up the spear at the hunter, he rushed onto the king's sword, caught him around the waist and forced him to his knees. Arthur pulled himself together and slipped quickly out of his grip. He struck at the monster from this side and that, fast, and didn't stop until he'd given him a mortal wound, driving the whole blade into his head where the skull covers the brain. The hated giant gave a shout, and fell with a huge crash, like an oak torn up by the winds.

Arthur burst out laughing at once. He ordered Bedivere to cut off the head and give it to one of the squires to carry back to the camp, for everyone to see.

<a id="page-184"></a>[\[Giles 184\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-184) He told the people looking at it to be quiet. He said this was the strongest enemy he'd met since he killed the giant Rhitta on Mount Aravius, who'd challenged him to a fight.[^184-1] Rhitta had made himself a cloak out of the beards of kings he'd beaten, and he'd ordered Arthur to skin off his own carefully and send it to him. Since he ruled over the others, he'd honour Arthur by setting it at the top of them all. If Arthur refused, Rhitta challenged him to fight, and the winner would take everything the loser had. They fought, and Arthur won. He took Rhitta's beard and spoils, and he'd never met a stronger man after, as he'd said.

After this victory, as was told, they went back to their tents with the head just before dawn. People came running in crowds to stare at it, praising the man who'd freed the country from such a glutton. But Hoel was sad at what had happened to his niece. He ordered a church built over her body on the mountain where she lay. It took its name from the girl's grave, and is called Helena's Tomb to this day.[^184-2]

### Arthur sends Gawain to Lucius, and a quarrel in Lucius's camp starts the fighting

When all the troops he'd been waiting for had arrived, Arthur marched from there towards Autun, where he thought the emperor was.[^184-3] At the river Aube he was told that Lucius had made his camp not far off, with an army so big, people said, that no one could stand against it. That didn't frighten Arthur off his plans. He settled on its bank, where he could lead his troops out freely, and if he needed to, fall back behind his defenses.

<a id="page-185"></a>[\[Giles 185\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-185) He sent two earls, Boso of Oxford and Guerinus of Chartres, to Lucius Tiberius with his nephew Gawain.[^185-1] They were to tell him to leave the lands of Gaul, or come the next day to find out which of them had the better right to it. The young men of the court were thrilled. They egged Gawain on to start something in the emperor's camp, so they'd get the chance to take on the Romans.

The envoys went to Lucius and told him to leave Gaul, or come out to battle the next day. He answered that he had no reason to go. He'd come to rule it. Then his nephew Gaius Quintilianus broke in. The Britons, he said, were better at boasting and threats than at courage and daring.

Gawain was furious. He drew the sword he wore, rushed at him and cut off his head, then made for the horses with his companions. The Romans went after them, some on foot and some mounted, trying with all their strength to avenge their countryman on the escaping envoys.

One of the Romans was just catching up with Guerinus of Chartres, when Guerinus suddenly turned. He aimed his lance, ran it through the man's armour and body, and threw him to the ground as hard as he could. Boso of Oxford was jealous that the man from Chartres had done such a brave thing. He turned his horse, drove his lance into the first man he met,[^185-2] and wounded him so badly that he had to leave the saddle he'd been chasing them in.

Meanwhile Marcellus Mutius, eager to avenge Quintilianus, was right behind Gawain, and had begun to hold him back. Gawain turned at once, and with the sword in his hand split the man's helmet and his head down to his chest. Then he told him to go to hell and report to the man he'd killed in the camp that this was how the Britons were full of threats and boasting.

Gawain gathered his companions again, and urged them all to turn together and each do his best to bring down a Roman. They did what he asked, and each of them brought down his man. But the Romans kept up the chase, striking at them now with lances, now with swords.

<a id="page-186"></a>[\[Giles 186\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-186) Still, they couldn't stop them or catch them. As they chased them near a wood, about six thousand Britons burst out of it. These had heard the earls were in flight and had hidden among the trees to help them. They rode out spurring their horses, filled the air with shouts, held their shields up in front of them, and fell on the Romans by surprise. They soon had them running.

Chasing them together, they knocked some of the Romans off their mounts with their lances, captured others and killed others. When the senator Petreius heard of it, he hurried to help his side with ten thousand more. He forced the Britons back to the wood they'd come out of, and they lost men doing it. But the Britons fought as they fell back, using ground they knew, and cut down huge numbers of their pursuers.

As the Britons gave way like this, Hider came up fast with five thousand in support. So they stood firm, and turned to face the men they'd shown their backs to a moment before, working hard to strike strong blows. The Romans held too. Sometimes they brought the Britons down, and at other times went down themselves.

The Britons longed with all their hearts for a fight, but they didn't care much how it would end once they'd started it. The Romans acted more wisely. Petreius Cotta led them like a good general, teaching them when to attack and when to fall back, and so did the others great harm.

When Boso saw this, he took aside many of the men he knew were boldest, and spoke to them. "We've started this battle without Arthur knowing," he said. "So we have to be careful not to come off worse. If we do, we'll lose a lot of soldiers, and make our king curse us. Get your nerve back, and follow me through the Roman ranks.

<a id="page-187"></a>[\[Giles 187\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-187) "With luck, we'll kill Petreius or take him prisoner."

They spurred their horses, broke through the enemy's wedges together, and came to the place where Petreius was giving orders. Boso went for him at once, threw his arms around his neck, and fell to the ground with him, just as he'd planned. The Romans rushed in to rescue Petreius, and the Britons to help Boso.

The slaughter between them was terrible, with shouting and confusion everywhere, as one side tried to free its leader and the other to hold on to him. They gave wounds and suffered them, struck each other down and fell. There you could see who was best with the spear, the sword or the javelin.

At last the Britons closed their ranks and charged. They took the Romans' attack, and pulled back with Petreius into the strength of their own line. Then they turned on the Romans at once. Their leader was gone, most of them were weakened, and plenty had already broken away and were turning to run. The Britons fell on them, cut them down from behind, stripped them as they lay, and passed on to chase the rest. They took a lot of prisoners too, who they meant to present to the king.

When they'd put the Romans through enough danger, they went back to the camp with their spoils and captives, and told what had happened to them. Then they brought Petreius Cotta and the others they'd taken to Arthur, happy at their victory. Arthur thanked them, and promised them honours and more besides, because they'd shown such courage while he was away. He wanted the prisoners put in jail. So he called some men to take them to Paris the next day and hand them to the city's guards, until he decided what else to do with them.

He ordered Duke Cador, Bedivere the butler and two earls, Borellus and Richerius, to escort them there with their followers, until they reached a place where the Romans couldn't trouble them.

### The Romans ambush the escort taking the prisoners to Paris

<a id="page-188"></a>[\[Giles 188\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-188) But the Romans happened to learn of the plan. At the emperor's command, they picked fifteen thousand of their soldiers to get ahead of the escort that night, fight them and free their friends. They put the senators Vulteius Catellus and Quintus Carutius in charge of them, with Evander, king of Syria, and Sertorius of Libya. These four set out at once with the troops on the road they'd been told to take. They found a good hiding place where the Britons were sure to go by, and lay in wait there.

In the morning the Britons started out with the prisoners, and were soon near the place, with no idea what tricks their clever enemies had laid for them. As they began to pass through it, the Romans burst out on them. The Britons weren't expecting anything like it, and the Romans overran them and broke through. Though they'd been taken by surprise, the Britons got together at last and fought back like men.

They put some of their own around the prisoners, and sorted the rest into companies to fight the enemy. Richerius and Bedivere took charge of the troop guarding the captives, and Cador, duke of Cornwall, and Borellus of the others. But the Romans had come out in no order at all. They didn't bother to arrange their men in units. They pressed on with all their strength instead, and cut the Britons down while they worked at forming ranks and defending themselves.

The Britons were so weakened that they'd have lost the prisoners in disgrace, if luck hadn't sent them the help they needed just then. Guitardus, duke of the Poitevins, had learned of the trap and come up with three thousand. Relying on his help, the Britons won at last, and paid the shameless robbers back for the slaughter. Still, they'd lost many of their own in the first clash. Among the dead was Borellus, the famous earl of Le Mans.

<a id="page-189"></a>[\[Giles 189\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-189) He was fighting Evander, king of Syria, whose lance went into his throat, and he coughed up his life with his blood. Four nobles died too, Hirelgas of Periron, Mauricius Cadorcanensis, Aliduc of Tintagel and Hider's son.[^189-1] It wasn't easy to find bolder men. Still, the Britons didn't lose their nerve or give up hope. They pressed on with all their strength, set on guarding the prisoners and bringing their enemies down.

At last the Romans couldn't stand up to their attack. They quickly began to leave the field and make for their camp. The Britons kept on chasing them, cutting them down and taking many captive. They didn't stop until Vulteius Catellus and Evander, king of Syria, were dead, and they'd scattered the rest completely. After winning, they sent the prisoners they'd been escorting on to Paris. Then they went back to Arthur with the men they'd just captured. They promised him the greatest victory to come, since so few of them had beaten so many enemies.

### Lucius makes for Langres, and Arthur draws up his army at Suesia

Lucius Tiberius took these losses hard. His mind was tormented. He turned it this way and that, unable to decide whether to fight the battle he'd begun with Arthur, or go back inside Autun and wait for help from the emperor Leo. At last he gave in to his fear. The next night he set out for Autun, and on the way he entered Langres with his armies.

When Arthur learned of it, he meant to get ahead of him on the road. That same night he went around the city, leaving it on his left, and came into a valley called Suesia, which Lucius would have to pass through.[^189-2] Arthur wanted to arrange his men for battle. He ordered one legion, which he'd put under Morvid, earl of Gloucester, to stay in reserve.

<a id="page-190"></a>[\[Giles 190\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-190) That way, if it was needed, the army would know where to fall back, gather its companies again, and attack once more. He split the others into seven divisions of five thousand five hundred fifty-five, all fully armed. Some of each was mounted, and the rest weren't. Between them the plan was that when the infantry attacked, the horsemen would come in at once from the side in close order and try to scatter the enemy.

The divisions on foot were drawn up in squares in the British way, each with two wings. Auguselus, king of Albany, and Cador, duke of Cornwall, commanded one, the first on its right and the other on its left. Two famous earls, Guerinus of Chartres and Boso of Richiden, which the Saxons call Oxford, were over another.[^190-1] Aschillius of the Danes and Lot of the Norwegians, both kings, led a third, and Hoel of Brittany and Arthur's nephew Gawain the fourth.

Behind these were four more. Kay the steward and Bedivere the butler had one, and Holdinus of the Ruteni and Guitardus of the Poitevins, both dukes, the next. Jugein of Leicester, Jonathal of Dorchester and Cursalem of Kaicester took a third, and Urgennius of Bath the last.[^190-2]

Farther back, Arthur chose a place for himself and the one legion he wanted with him. There he set up the golden dragon he carried as his standard, so the wounded and the worn out could fall back to it as if to a camp if they had to. His own held six thousand six hundred sixty-six.

### Arthur reminds his men of their victories and promises them Rome's riches

<a id="page-191"></a>[\[Giles 191\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-191) With everything in order, Arthur spoke to his comrades.

"Men of my household," he said, "you've made Britain the ruler of thirty kingdoms. I'm glad of your courage. I see it hasn't failed, and has even grown stronger. For five years you've been out of practice, given more to the pleasures of peace than to the business of war. Yet you haven't fallen short of the goodness you were born with. You've kept it, and you've driven the Romans to run. Their pride pushed them to try to take away your freedom. They came at us with greater numbers and started the fighting. Then they couldn't stand up to you, and took shelter in disgrace in that city.

"Soon they'll come out of it, and go through this valley on their way to Autun. You can be there to meet them, and catch them like cattle, when they don't expect anything like it. They must have thought you as slow as the peoples of the East, when they tried to make your country pay them tribute and conquer you. Haven't they heard of the wars you fought against the Danes, the Norwegians and the dukes of Gaul? You put them under my power and freed them from Rome's shameful rule.

"We won a harder struggle, so there's no doubt we'll carry this easier one, if we work as hard to crush those half-men. Think of the honours each of you'll have, if you do what I want and what I order, as loyal comrades. Once we've beaten them, we'll march straight on Rome. We'll capture it and keep it. Then you'll have its gold, its silver, its palaces, its towers, its towns and its cities, and all the other riches of the winners."

While he was still saying this, they all shouted together that they agreed. They'd be ready to die rather than leave the field while he lived.

### Lucius stands his ground and urges his men to remember their fathers

<a id="page-192"></a>[\[Giles 192\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-192) But Lucius Tiberius learned of the trap being set for him. He gave up the flight he'd planned and found his nerve again. He decided to go against the Britons in the same valley, called his generals together, and spoke to them.

"Honoured fathers," he said, "the kingdoms of the East and the West ought to be under your rule. Remember your ancestors. To beat the enemies of the Republic, they didn't shrink from shedding their blood. They left their descendants an example of courage and skill in war. They fought as if Yahweh hadn't ordained that they'd die in battle.[^192-1] So they often won, and their victories kept them alive, because nothing would happen to anyone but what came down from Yahweh's plan.

"So the Republic grew, and so did their courage. All the honesty, honour and generosity that noble men used to have lived on in them for a long time. It raised them and their descendants to rule the whole world. I want to stir the same thing in you. So I urge you to call up the goodness of old and stay true to it. Go after our enemies in the valley where they're lying in wait for us, and fight to make them give us back what's ours.

"Don't think I took shelter in this city because I was afraid of them or of meeting them. I thought they'd follow us foolishly, and we could turn on them suddenly as they came, and slaughter them as they rushed at us in scattered bands. They've done what we didn't expect, so let's change our plan too. Let's go after them and attack boldly. If they get the upper hand, let's stand firm together and bear their first charge. That way we'll win without a doubt.

<a id="page-193"></a>[\[Giles 193\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-193) "In many battles, the side that could hold out in the first clash has nearly always come away with the victory."

When he finished saying this and more, they all agreed with one voice, swore to fight together, and hurried to get their weapons. Once armed, they marched out of Langres and came to the valley where Arthur had drawn up his divisions. They formed twelve wedges, all on foot, ordered in the Roman way. Each held six thousand six hundred sixty-six soldiers.[^193-1] They gave each its leaders, so it would attack and stand up to the enemy's charge at their command.

Each of the first four wedges had a king and a senator over it: Alifantinam of Spain with Lucius Catellus, Hirtacius of the Parthians with Marius Lepidus, Boccus of the Medes with Gaius Metellus, and Sertorius of Libya with Quintus Milvius. These four made up the front line. Behind them came four more, under Serses, king of Iturea, Pandrasus of Egypt, and the dukes Polytetes of Bithynia and Teucer of Phrygia. After these stood a last four, given to the senator Quintus Carutius, Laelius Hostiensis, Sulpicius Subuculus and Mauricius Silvanus.[^193-2]

Lucius himself went back and forth among them, advising them and showing them how to act. In the middle he had the golden eagle he'd carried for a standard set up firmly. Anyone their luck cut off was to try to make their way back to it.

### The armies meet, and Bedivere and Kay ride against the Medes

<a id="page-194"></a>[\[Giles 194\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-194) The Britons on one side and the Romans on the other stood facing each other with their spears raised. As soon as the trumpets sounded, the wedge under Alifantinam and Lucius Catellus boldly charged the division of Auguselus and Cador. But it couldn't break that line, which held tight as it met them. Then the troops of Guerinus and Boso came on. While the Romans were kept busy there, as was said, these charged them at a gallop, cut through, and ran into the wedge Hirtacius was leading against Aschillius, king of the Danes.

At once the lines clashed on both sides, broke into each other's ranks, and fought a huge battle. There was a dreadful slaughter between them, and the shouting was deafening. Men beat the earth with their heads and heels as they died, and everywhere they coughed up their lives with their blood.

But the Britons took the first loss. Bedivere the butler was killed, and Kay the steward was fatally wounded. Bedivere had met Boccus, king of the Medes, whose lance ran him through, and he fell dead among the enemy. Kay tried to avenge him, but the Medes' companies surrounded him, and there he took his death wound. Still, like a good knight, he cut a way out with the troop he was leading. 

He killed and scattered the Medes, and would have gotten back to his own side with his unit whole, if he hadn't met the wedge of the king of Libya. Its charge broke up all the men Kay had with him. He fell back as best he could with a few, and escaped to the golden dragon with Bedivere's body.

How Normandy mourned, when they saw their duke's corpse torn by so many wounds! How the men of Anjou cried, as they treated their earl Kay's injuries in all the ways they knew![^194-1] But there was no time for grief.

<a id="page-195"></a>[\[Giles 195\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-195) The bloody lines were rushing at each other on every side, and gave them no space for tears, but forced them to defend themselves.

Bedivere's nephew Hirelgas was beyond himself at his uncle's death. He gathered three hundred of his men. Like a boar among a pack of hounds, he galloped through the enemy ranks, heading for the place where he'd seen the standard of the king of the Medes. He hardly thought what might happen to him, as long as he got revenge. When he reached the spot, he killed Boccus, carried his corpse back to his companions, and beside the butler's body tore it to pieces.

Then with a great shout Hirelgas fired up his countrymen's companies. He urged them to rush at the enemy and attack them again and again. Their own courage was fresh, he said, and the Romans' hearts were shaking with fear. The Britons stood close to the Romans, and were better ordered in their ranks than the others, so they could hurt them more often and harder.

Stirred by his words, the Britons attacked from all directions, and both armies suffered a huge slaughter. The Romans lost countless men, among them Alifantinam, king of Spain, Micipsa of Babylon, and the senators Quintus Milvius and Marius Lepidus. On the Britons' side fell Holdinus of the Ruteni, Leodegarius of Boulogne, and three earls of Britain, Cursalem of Kaicester, Galluc of Salisbury and Urgennius of Bath.

The companies these men had led were so weakened that they gave way, until they reached the line of the Britons of Brittany, under Hoel and Gawain. That force flared up like fire and charged the enemy. It rallied the retreating, and made the ones who'd just been chasing them run. It kept after them, bringing some down as they fled and killing others, and didn't stop the slaughter until it came to the emperor's own company. Seeing what was happening to his men, Lucius hurried to help them.

### Hoel and Gawain cut their way towards the emperor's wedge

<a id="page-196"></a>[\[Giles 196\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-196) When the two sides met, the Britons grew weaker. Kimarcocus, earl of Tréguier, fell, and two thousand with him. Three famous nobles died too, Richomarcus, Bloccovius and Lagivius of Bodloan.[^196-1] If they'd been princes of kingdoms, later ages would have sung their fame for their courage. In the charge they made with Hoel and Gawain, no enemy they came at got away alive, whether by sword or by lance. But once they'd come into Lucius's line, the Romans surrounded them, and they fell with the earl and his followers.

Hoel and Gawain were better than any men the past had ever bred. When they saw their companions killed, they pressed on harder. They rode this way and that, one on each side, harassing the emperor's wedge. Gawain burned with a courage always fresh, and tried to find a way to fight Lucius. He charged like the boldest of knights, and as he went in he struck enemies down, and as they fell he killed them.

Hoel was no less than Gawain, and flashed like lightning on the other side. He urged his men on, cut into the enemy and took their blows without fear. Not an hour went by when he didn't strike and get hit again and again. It wasn't easy to say which of the two was the stronger.

### Gawain meets Lucius, and Arthur draws Excalibur and rallies his men

<a id="page-197"></a>[\[Giles 197\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-197) Gawain kept attacking the companies, as was said, and at last he found the way in he'd hoped for. He rushed at the emperor and fought him. Lucius was in the first flower of youth, with plenty of daring, strength and courage. He wanted nothing more than to face a knight like Gawain, who'd make him find out what he was worth in war. He'd heard so much of Gawain's fame that he was glad to stand up to him, and boasted of it. They kept at it a long time, striking hard blows and holding up their shields against them, each working to kill the other.

While they were at it so fiercely, the Romans suddenly got their strength back. They attacked the Britons of Brittany and rescued their emperor. Then they drove Hoel, Gawain and their companies back, cutting them down, until they ran into Arthur and his division without warning. When Arthur heard of the slaughter of his troops a little before, he'd charged with his legion. He'd drawn Excalibur, the best of swords, and was firing up his comrades in a loud voice with these words.

"What are you doing, men?" Arthur said. "Why let these women get away unhurt? Don't let one of them leave alive. Remember your right hands, trained in so many battles, that put thirty kingdoms under my power. Think of your grandfathers, made to pay Rome tribute when the Romans were the stronger. Your freedom is at stake too: these half-men, weaker than you, are trying to take it away. Not one of them gets away. What are you doing?"

Shouting this and more, he rushed at the enemy, bringing them down and cutting them to pieces. He killed every man he met, or the man's horse, with one blow.

<a id="page-198"></a>[\[Giles 198\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-198) So they ran like cattle from a fierce lion, when a savage hunger drives it to eat whatever comes its way. Their armour was no use to them. Excalibur, swung by the right hand of so brave a king, made them cough up their souls with their blood. Bad luck brought Sertorius of Libya and Polytetes of Bithynia against him. He cut off their heads and sent them down to hell.

When the Britons saw Arthur fighting like this, they found bolder heart. They went at the Romans together and pressed forward in close ranks. While the foot soldiers attacked from one side, the horsemen tried to bring them down and break through from the other. Still the Romans resisted fiercely. At the urging of Lucius, a famous king, they worked to pay the Britons back for the slaughter. Both armies fought as hard as if they'd only just met.

On one side Arthur struck the enemy again and again, as was said, and urged the Britons to hold out. On the other Lucius Tiberius spurred his Romans on, and often led them into great acts of courage. He kept on fighting himself. He rode around his companies everywhere, and whatever enemy his luck brought him, he killed with lance or sword. So there was a horrible slaughter on both sides, because now the Britons had the upper hand, and now the Romans in their turn.

At last, while the battle went on like this, Morvid, earl of Gloucester, came up at a gallop with the legion I said was in the hills. He hit the enemy from behind when they didn't expect anything like it. He broke right through them, scattered them, and killed great numbers. Then many thousands of Romans fell. Then the emperor Lucius too, caught among the companies, was run through by someone's lance and died. The Britons kept up the chase, and won the victory, though it cost them great effort.

### The Romans scatter or give themselves up

<a id="page-199"></a>[\[Giles 199\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-199) The Romans broke up. Driven by fear, some took to the back roads and woods. Others made for the cities, the towns and whatever places were safest. The Britons hunted them with all their strength, killed them miserably, caught them and stripped them. Most of the Romans held out their hands of their own accord, like women, to be tied, so they'd have a little longer to live.

It was Yahweh's doing. Long ago the Romans' ancestors had harassed these Britons' grandfathers with unjust attacks. Now these Britons stood up to defend the freedom the Romans were trying to take from them, and refused the tribute wrongly demanded of them.

### Arthur buries his dead, then hears that Mordred has taken his crown and queen

Once the victory was won, Arthur ordered the bodies of his nobles set apart from the enemy dead. Then they were prepared in the royal way and taken to the abbeys of their own lands, to be buried there with honour. The men of Normandy took Bedivere the butler with loud mourning to his city of Bayeux, which his great-grandfather Bedivere the First had built. There they put him in a cemetery beside the wall, on the south side of the town.

Kay was carried, badly wounded, to Caen, which he'd founded.[^199-1] He died of the same wound a little later, and was buried in a wood, in a house of hermits not far from there, as a duke of Anjou ought to be. Holdinus of the Ruteni went to Flanders, and was given a grave in his city of Thérouanne.

<a id="page-200"></a>[\[Giles 200\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-200) The other earls and nobles were taken to the abbeys nearby, as Arthur had commanded. He took pity on his enemies too, and told the people of the country to bury them. He had Lucius's body carried to the senate, with a message that no other tribute was owed from Britain.

He spent the following winter in those parts, and gave his time to conquering the cities of the Allobroges. When summer came, he meant to march on Rome. He'd begun to cross the mountains, when news reached him that his nephew Mordred, who he'd left in charge of Britain, had put on Arthur's crown by tyranny and treason.[^200-1] Queen Guinevere had broken the vows of her marriage and was living with Mordred in a wicked union.

## Part 11: Mordred takes the crown and the queen, and Arthur comes home to fight him

### Geoffrey names his sources, and Arthur leaves Hoel in Gaul and sails home

About this crime, my noble earl,[^200-2] Geoffrey of Monmouth will say nothing.[^200-3] He'll tell briefly, though, if in a poor style, what battles that famous king fought with his nephew after he came home to Britain. He tells them as he found them in the British book, and as Walter of Oxford, a man very learned in many histories, told them to him.[^200-4] As soon as word of the crime reached Arthur, he put off the war he'd planned against the Roman emperor Leo. He left Hoel, duke of the Britons of Brittany, with the army of Gaul to settle those parts. Then he hurried back to Britain, taking only the island kings and their armies.

<a id="page-201"></a>[\[Giles 201\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-201) Mordred had sent Cheldric, leader of the Saxons, to Germany to raise as many men there as he could and sail back with them as fast as possible. In return Mordred had promised him the part of the island that stretches from the river Humber to Scotland, and everything Horsa and Hengist had held in Kent in Vortigern's time. Cheldric did as he was told and landed with eight hundred ships full of armed pagans. He made a treaty with Mordred and obeyed him from then on as if he were his king.

Mordred had also won over the Scots, the Picts and the Irish, and all his uncle's known enemies. There were about eighty thousand of them, pagans and Christians together. Trusting in their help, with their numbers behind him, he attacked Arthur at Richborough as his men came ashore, and killed many of them.[^201-1] That day Auguselus, king of Albany, and Gawain, Arthur's nephew, fell with countless others. Auguselus's kingdom went to Owain son of Urien, whose father was Auguselus's brother. The young man later won fame for his courage in these wars.

> **In the Arthur story:** Owain son of Urien becomes Yvain, the Knight with the Lion, in Chrétien de Troyes's romance, and Sir Uwain in Malory, a knight of the Round Table and Morgan le Fay's son by Urien.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

At last, though it cost them great effort, Arthur's men reached the shore. They paid back the slaughter and drove Mordred and his army to flight. Long practice in battle had taught them to set out their companies wisely. Some fought on foot and some on horseback. Whenever the infantry moved to attack or stand firm, the horsemen charged at once from the side and tried with all their strength to break through the enemy. That's how they forced them to run.

Mordred gathered his men from all over and entered Winchester that night. When Queen Guinevere heard it, she gave up all hope. She fled from York to the City of the Legions, and there, in the church of the martyr Julius, she became a nun and vowed to stay chaste among the sisters.

### Arthur follows Mordred into Cornwall, and they meet in battle at Camlann

<a id="page-202"></a>[\[Giles 202\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-202) Arthur burned with greater anger now, because he'd lost so many hundreds of his comrades.[^202-1] On the third day, once the dead were buried, he went to the city and besieged Mordred, who'd taken refuge inside. Mordred still wouldn't give up what he'd started. He stirred up his followers every way he could, marched out with his troops and made ready to fight his uncle. When the battle began, there was a great slaughter on both sides. It fell harder on his, and at last it forced him to leave the field in disgrace. He hardly cared how his dead were buried. He took ship quickly and fled towards Cornwall.

Arthur was tormented that Mordred had got away so often. He followed him straight into that country, as far as the river Camlann, where Mordred was waiting for him.[^202-2] Mordred was as bold as anyone alive and always first to attack. He split his troops into companies at once, wanting to win or die rather than keep running like this.

Sixty thousand of his men were still left. He made three divisions of them, putting six thousand six hundred sixty-six armed soldiers in each, then made a fourth from all the rest. He gave each of the first three a commander, and kept the fourth under his own command. Once they were in place, he went from one to the next to put heart in them, promising them their enemies' lands if they held on to win.

<a id="page-203"></a>[\[Giles 203\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-203) Arthur set his own army on the other side. He split it into nine companies of foot soldiers, drawn up square, with a right and a left wing, and gave each a leader. He urged them to kill the robbers who'd broken their oaths, the men Mordred had called over from foreign lands to take their homes and honours from them. These barbarians from different kingdoms were no soldiers, he told them, and knew nothing of war. They'd never stand against fighters with so many battles behind them, if his army attacked boldly and pressed hard.

With the commanders on each side urging on their troops, the lines suddenly charged, and the fighting started, both raining down blows.[^203-1] At once there was such slaughter everywhere, such groaning of the dying and such fury in the attackers, that it's hard to describe and painful to hear. Men gave wounds and took them, killed and died.

After much of the day had gone this way, Arthur at last charged with one company, the one holding six thousand six hundred sixty-six soldiers, into the division where he knew Mordred was. He cut a road through it with swords, broke through and did dreadful slaughter. Mordred fell, and many thousands with him. Still the rest didn't run at his death. They gathered from all over the field and tried to hold out with whatever courage they had.

So a fierce fight followed, and nearly every leader on both sides went down with his troops. Mordred lost the Saxons Cheldric, Elafius, Egbrictus and Bunignus,[^203-2] the Irish Gillapatric, Gillamor, Gislafel and Gillarium,[^203-3] and the Scots and the Picts with almost all their people. Arthur's dead included Olbrictus, king of Norway, Aschillius of Denmark, Cador of Limetia and Cassivellaunus,[^203-4] with many thousands of men, the Britons and the other peoples who'd come with them.

Even the famous King Arthur was fatally wounded. He was carried from there to the island of Avalon to have his wounds healed.[^203-5] He gave the crown of Britain to Constantine, his relative, whose father was Cador, duke of Cornwall, five hundred forty-two years after Christ became man.[^203-6]

### Mordred's sons and the Saxons rise against Constantine, and Saint David is buried

<a id="page-204"></a>[\[Giles 204\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-204) Once Constantine was crowned, the Saxons rose against him, and so did Mordred's two sons.[^204-1] They couldn't beat him. After many battles they fled, one to London and the other to Winchester, and began to take those cities over.

At that time Bangor lost its devout bishop, Saint Deiniol, and Theon of Gloucester was chosen archbishop of London. Then David, the holy head of the church of the City of the Legions, died at St Davids, in his own abbey. He'd loved it more than any other monastery in his diocese, because Saint Patrick, who'd predicted his birth, founded it.[^204-2] He was staying there with his brother monks when he fell suddenly ill and died. On the orders of King Maelgwn Gwynedd he was buried in that same abbey. Kincus, bishop of Llanbadarn, took his place in the archbishop's seat.[^204-3]

### Constantine hunts Mordred's sons to the altars, and is buried at Stonehenge

Constantine went after the Saxons and brought them under his rule. He also captured Mordred's two sons. One young man had run into the church of Saint Amphibalus at Winchester, and Constantine cut him down in the sanctuary. The other had hidden in a London monastery. When he was found at last beside its altar, Constantine put him to a cruel death.[^204-4]

In the third year after that, Constantine was killed by Aurelius Conanus, struck down by Yahweh's judgement. Constantine was buried next to Uther Pendragon inside the ring of stones built with wonderful skill not far from Salisbury, which the English call Stonehenge.[^204-5]

### Aurelius Conanus puts his uncle in prison and takes the throne

<a id="page-205"></a>[\[Giles 205\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-205) After him came Aurelius Conanus, his nephew, a young man of remarkable courage.[^205-1] He ruled the whole island, and he'd have deserved its crown if he hadn't loved civil war. He turned on his own uncle, who should have reigned after Constantine, and threw him into prison. He killed the old man's two sons and took the kingdom, and died in the second year of his reign.

### Vortiporius drives back the Saxons and rules four years in peace

Vortiporius came after Conanus. The Saxons rose against him, bringing their countrymen over from Germany in a huge fleet. But he fought them and beat them. Then he became sole ruler of the island, and governed its people carefully and in peace for four years.

### Maelgwn, the handsomest man in Britain, rules the island and six more

After him came Maelgwn Gwynedd, nearly the best-looking man in all Britain. He drove out many tyrants, was strong in arms and more generous than anyone. He ruled all Britain too, and in very hard fighting he added the six neighbouring islands of the ocean to his rule: Ireland, Iceland, Gotland, Orkney, Norway and Denmark. Maelgwn's courage was beyond measure, and would have made Maelgwn famous, if the sin of Sodom hadn't made Yahweh hate the man.[^205-2]

### Gormund comes over from Ireland and drives Careticus beyond the Severn

<a id="page-206"></a>[\[Giles 206\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-206) Careticus came after Maelgwn. A lover of civil war, Careticus was hated by Yahweh and the Britons.

The Saxons saw how little anyone could rely on Careticus, and went to Ireland for Gormund, king of Africa, who'd sailed there with a great fleet and conquered its people.[^206-1] Then the Saxons broke faith with the Britons and called him over, and Gormund crossed to Britain with a hundred sixty-six thousand men from Africa. On one side the Saxons, now enemies, were laying the country waste. On the other its own people did the same, never stopping their wars among themselves.

Gormund made a treaty with the Saxons and attacked King Careticus. After many battles he chased him from one town to the next, until he drove him into Cirencester and besieged him there. There Isembard came to him, the nephew of King Louis of the Franks. He made a pact of friendship with Gormund and gave up his Christian faith out of love for him. In return Gormund was to help him take the kingdom of Gaul from his uncle, who, he said, had driven him out by force and against the law.

At last Gormund took Cirencester and burned it. Then he fought Careticus and drove him beyond the Severn into Wales. Straight away he laid the fields waste and set fire to every town nearby, and the blaze didn't die down until it had swept nearly the whole face of the island, from one sea to the other.[^206-2] All the settlements fell to battering rams. Farmers and the church's priests were struck down together, among flashing swords and roaring flames. The few who were left, broken by such disasters, fled wherever they could find shelter.

### Geoffrey turns on the Britons for their wars among themselves

<a id="page-207"></a>[\[Giles 207\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-207) Why, you lazy people, weighed down by your monstrous crimes? Why, always thirsty for civil war, have you worn yourselves out with fighting at home? You once brought kingdoms far away under your rule. Now, like a good vine gone wild and turned bitter, you can't defend your country, your wives and your children from your enemies.[^207-1]

Go on, then, keep up your civil war. You hardly understand the Gospel's words: "Every kingdom divided against itself will be laid waste, and its houses will fall one on another."[^207-2] Yours was split against itself. The fury of quarreling and the smoke of envy dulled your mind. Your pride wouldn't let you obey one king. So now you see your country ruined by the most wicked pagans, and its homes collapsing on one another, and your descendants will mourn it. They'll see the barbarian lioness's cubs take the towns, the cities and everything else that was theirs.[^207-3] Driven out in misery, they'll never, or barely, win back the standing they had.

### The Saxons take Loegria, and the Britons withdraw into Cornwall and Wales

As I said, that ill-fated tyrant laid waste nearly the whole island with countless thousands of men from Africa. Then he gave most of it, called Loegria, to the Saxons, whose betrayal had brought him there. The Britons who were left withdrew into the west of the kingdom, Cornwall and Wales. From there they kept up savage raids on their enemies.

<a id="page-208"></a>[\[Giles 208\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-208) The three archbishops, the one of the City of the Legions, Theon of London and Thadioceus of York, saw every church under them pulled down to the ground. With all the clergy who'd lived through that disaster, they fled with the saints' relics to the shelter of the woods in Wales. They were afraid the barbarians' raids would destroy the holy bones of so many great men of old, if they abandoned them in such danger and gave themselves up to a martyr's death on the spot. Others in large numbers sailed for Brittany. So the whole church of two provinces, Loegria and Northumbria, was emptied of its communities. But I'll tell of this elsewhere, when I translate the book about their exile.[^208-1]

### The Britons, under three tyrants, no longer hold the crown of the island

After that the Britons lost the crown of the kingdom and the rule of the island for a long time, and didn't try to win back their old standing. Instead they kept wasting what was still theirs in civil wars, since it was under three tyrants, not a single ruler. The Saxons hadn't won the island's crown yet either. They too were under three kings of their own, and troubled now one another, now the Britons.

### Pope Gregory sends Augustine to the English, and the British bishops refuse him

Meanwhile Pope Gregory the Great sent Augustine of Canterbury to Britain to preach Yahweh's word to the English.[^208-2] Blinded by pagan superstition, they'd wiped out Christianity in their own share of the island.

<a id="page-209"></a>[\[Giles 209\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-209) Among the Britons, though, the faith was still strong. It had lasted since the time of Pope Eleutherius, and had never failed them.[^209-1] When Augustine came, he found seven bishops in their land and an archbishop, all very devout men, and a number of abbeys where Christ's flock kept the right rule. One of these was a very famous church in the city of Bangor.[^209-2] So many monks are said to have been there that when the monastery was split into seven parts, each with its own head, none had fewer than three hundred. All of them lived by the work of their own hands. Their abbot was called Dinoot, and he was wonderfully learned in the liberal arts.

Augustine asked the British bishops to accept his authority, and urged them to share with him the work of preaching to the English. Dinoot answered with many arguments that they owed him no obedience and wouldn't waste their sermons on their enemies. They had an archbishop of their own, and the Saxons kept on taking their country from them. So the Britons hated them bitterly, thought nothing of their faith and religion, and had no more to do with the English than with dogs.

### Aethelberht of Kent sends Aethelfrith of Northumbria against the monks of Bangor

Aethelberht of Kent saw the Britons refusing to accept Augustine's authority and looking down on his teaching. He took it very badly. So he stirred up Aethelfrith, king of Northumbria, and the other rulers of the Saxons. He urged them to raise a great army and march on Bangor, to destroy Abbot Dinoot and the clergy who looked down on them.

<a id="page-210"></a>[\[Giles 210\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-210) They took his advice and gathered a huge force. They made for the land of the Britons and came to Chester, where Brocmail, its earl, was waiting for them.[^210-1] Countless monks and hermits had come there from every British province, most of them from Bangor, to pray for their people's safety. When the armies were gathered, Aethelfrith, king of Northumbria, fought Brocmail. Brocmail held out with fewer soldiers, but at last abandoned the place and fled, though not before he'd killed great numbers of the enemy. Once Aethelfrith had taken it and learned why the monks had come, he ordered his men to turn their weapons on them first. So twelve hundred of them died as martyrs that same day, and won a seat in the kingdom of heaven.[^210-2]

Then that tyrant of the Saxons made for Bangor itself. When the British leaders heard of his madness, they came from every side to meet him: Bledericus, duke of Cornwall, Margadud, king of Dyfed, and Cadfan of Gwynedd. They fought him, wounded him and put him to flight. They killed so much of his army that about ten thousand and sixty-six fell. The Britons lost Bledericus, duke of Cornwall, who'd led them through these battles.

## Part 12: Cadwallon fights the English kings, and an angel tells Cadwaladr to go to Rome

### The Britons crown Cadfan, who shares Britain with Aethelfrith at the Humber

<a id="page-211"></a>[\[Giles 211\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-211) Then all the British princes met in the city of Leicester.[^211-1] They agreed to make Cadfan their king, and to follow him across the Humber after Aethelfrith. Once Cadfan was crowned, they gathered from all over and crossed the river. When Aethelfrith heard of it, he brought in the rulers of the Saxons with him and marched out to meet Cadfan. Both armies were drawing up their companies when friends of the two came and made peace between them. Aethelfrith would hold Britain beyond the Humber, and Cadfan this side of it. They swore to the agreement and gave hostages. Such friendship grew up between them that they shared everything they had.

Meanwhile Aethelfrith put away his own wife and married another. He hated the one he'd sent away so much that he drove her out of the kingdom of Northumbria. She was pregnant, and went to King Cadfan to ask him to make peace between her and her husband. Aethelfrith wouldn't agree on any terms, so she stayed at Cadfan's court until her time came. There she gave birth to the child she was carrying. A little later Cadfan's queen also had a son, since she'd become pregnant at the same time. The boys were brought up as royal children should be. One was called Cadwallon, and the other Edwin.[^211-2]

<a id="page-212"></a>[\[Giles 212\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-212) When they were older, their parents sent them to Salomon, king of the Britons of Brittany. In his household they were to learn how to fight, and the other ways of a royal hall. He welcomed them warmly, and they grew close to him. Nobody their age there could talk with the king more privately or pleasantly. In the end they often fought the enemy in front of him, and their bold acts in battle made their courage famous.

### Edwin asks to wear a crown north of the Humber, and Brian cries

In time their fathers died, and they came back to Britain. They took up the rule of their kingdoms and kept up the friendship their fathers had had. Two years later Edwin asked Cadwallon for permission to wear a crown. He wanted to hold the set feasts in Northumbria, as Cadwallon did on this side of the Humber by old custom. Their wiser advisers arranged talks as well as they could, by the river Douglas. Cadwallon lay on the other bank, with his head in the lap of a nephew of his called Brian.

While envoys carried answers back and forth, Brian broke down. His tears ran down and wet the king's face and beard. Cadwallon thought it was raining and looked up. Seeing the young man crying, he asked why he was suddenly so sad.

"I have to cry," Brian said, "and so do the Britons, forever. Ever since Maelgwn's time the barbarians have attacked them. They still haven't found a prince to bring them back to their old standing.

<a id="page-213"></a>[\[Giles 213\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-213) "Even the little honour they've got left is getting smaller, and you're letting it. Now the Saxons, newcomers who've always betrayed them, are starting to wear a royal crown in one land with yours. Raised by the name of king, they'll be more famous in the country they came from. It'll be easier for them to bring over their countrymen, who'll work to wipe our people out. Treason is their habit, and they keep faith with no one. So I think we should hold them down, not raise them up.

"When King Vortigern first took them on, they lived quietly under a pretence of peace, as if they'd fight for the country. But once they could show their wickedness, they paid back good with evil. They betrayed him and slaughtered the kingdom's people. Next came Aurelius Ambrosius. They swore terrible oaths and ate at his table, then gave him poison to drink. They turned on Arthur too. They broke the promise that bound them and fought against him beside his nephew Mordred. Last of all they were false to Careticus, and brought Gormund from Africa, against him. Gormund's attacks took our people's country from them and drove that king shamefully out."

### Cadwallon's advisers refuse Edwin a crown of his own, and Edwin leaves angry

When Brian said this, Cadwallon was sorry for the agreement he'd begun. He told Edwin he couldn't get his advisers to let him grant the request. They said it was against the law and the old tradition for an island of one crown to be under two kings. Edwin was angry. He broke off the talks and went back to Northumbria, saying he'd be crowned without Cadwallon's permission.

<a id="page-214"></a>[\[Giles 214\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-214) When Cadwallon was told, he sent envoys with his answer. He'd cut off Edwin's head under the crown, if Edwin dared to wear one inside the kingdom of Britain.

### Edwin drives Cadwallon into Ireland, and a storm casts him on Guernsey

So a quarrel broke out between them, and their men attacked each other in many fights. At last the two kings met beyond the Humber. In the battle Cadwallon lost thousands of his soldiers and was put to flight. He made his way through Albany and crossed to the island of Ireland. Once Edwin had won, he led his army through the Britons' provinces. He burned their cities, and tortured the townspeople and farmers in every way he could.

While Edwin gave his cruelty free rein, Cadwallon kept trying to sail back to his country, but couldn't. Whatever harbour he made for, Edwin met him there with his host and stopped him landing. A very wise seer called Pellitus had come to Edwin from Spain. He'd studied the flight of birds and the courses of the stars, and warned Edwin of all bad luck before it happened. So Edwin learned of Cadwallon's return and met him. He wrecked Cadwallon's ships, drowned his men and shut him out of every port.

Cadwallon didn't know what to do, and nearly gave up hope of coming home. At last he decided to go to Salomon, king of the Britons of Brittany, and ask him for help and advice. As he set sail for Brittany, sudden storms broke and scattered his companions' ships. Soon no two stayed together.

<a id="page-215"></a>[\[Giles 215\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-215) The captain of the king's vessel lost his nerve, let go and left it to chance. All night it was driven this way and that among the waves, in danger of death. At dawn the next day they reached an island called Guernsey, and got ashore there with great effort.

Cadwallon was overcome with grief and anger at losing his companions. For three days and nights he wouldn't eat, and lay sick in bed. On the fourth day he began to crave the meat of a wild animal. He called Brian and told him what he wanted. Brian took his bow and arrows and went over the island, hoping chance would bring him something to hunt. He searched it all without finding what he was looking for, and was in agony, because he couldn't give his lord what he longed for. He was afraid the king's illness would kill him if he didn't get it.

So Brian tried something new. He cut a piece of flesh from his own thigh, cooked it over the fire, and took it to the king as game. Cadwallon thought it was from some wild animal and ate it, and felt stronger. He was amazed, since nothing else had ever been so good. Once full, he grew more cheerful, and three days later he was completely well.

When a good wind rose, they got the ship ready, raised the sail and put to sea. They landed at the city of Kidaleta.[^215-1] Then they went to King Salomon, who welcomed them kindly and gave them the honour they were due. When he learned why they'd come, he promised them help, and said this.

### Salomon of Brittany asks why the Britons have lost their land

"It hurts us, my fine young men, that a barbarian people has crushed your grandfathers' land, and that you've been driven out in disgrace.

<a id="page-216"></a>[\[Giles 216\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-216) "Other rulers can defend their kingdoms, so it's strange your people have lost so rich an island. It's odd too that they can't stand against the English, who our people think nothing of. While the Britons of Brittany and of Britain lived as one people, Britain ruled every kingdom around it. No people anywhere but the Romans could conquer it. Even they didn't do it by their own power, I've lately been told, but through a quarrel among the island's nobles. The Romans had it for a time, but they lost their governors, who were killed, and had to leave in shame.

"But after the Britons crossed over to this province under Maximianus and Conan, the ones who stayed behind never again held the crown for long. Many of their princes kept up their fathers' old standing. More often, though, weaker heirs came after them, and lost it altogether when enemies invaded. So your people's weakness pains me, since we're one race. We're called Britons just as you are, we of this kingdom, who bravely defend the country you see here against all its neighbours."

### Cadwallon answers that the Britons' sins cost them the island

Salomon finished saying this and more, and Cadwallon was a little embarrassed. "I thank you deeply, my lord, born of a line of rulers, for promising me help to win back my kingdom," he answered. "You said it's strange that my people haven't kept their grandfathers' standing since the Britons came to these provinces. I don't find it surprising at all. The noblest men of the whole island followed those leaders. The common people stayed behind, and took over the estates and titles of those who'd gone.

<a id="page-217"></a>[\[Giles 217\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-217) "Once they'd stepped into the nobles' place, they rose higher than their rank allowed. Proud of their great wealth, they gave themselves up to lust of a kind not heard of even among the pagans. As Gildas the historian says, they practised not only this vice but every one human nature is prone to.[^217-1] Above all, what overturns everything good grew strong in them. They hated the truth and those who spoke it. They loved lies and those who made them up. They took evil for good, and honoured wickedness as kindness. They welcomed Satan as an angel of light.[^217-2]

"Kings were anointed not for Yahweh's sake, but for being crueler than the rest. Soon after, with no inquiry into the facts, the same people killed them, and chose others still more savage. If any ruler seemed gentler and somewhat closer to the truth, everyone's hatred and weapons were turned on that one, as the ruin of Britain. In the end they weighed what pleased Yahweh and what didn't on the same scales, unless the bad side was heavier. So they did everything that worked against their own salvation, as if the true doctor of everyone offered no cure at all. It wasn't only ordinary people who did this. Christ's flock itself and its shepherds did it too, without telling right from wrong.

"So it's no surprise that such fallen people, hated by Yahweh for such crimes, lost the country they'd fouled that way. Yahweh meant to punish them, when it let a foreign people come in to drive us off our fathers' fields. Still, it would be right, if Yahweh allowed it, to bring our people back to their old standing. Then our race won't be shamed by having had weak leaders who did nothing about it in their time.

"We come from the same family, so I'm all the surer in asking your help.

<a id="page-218"></a>[\[Giles 218\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-218) "Maelgwn, that great king of Britain who reigned fourth after Arthur, had two sons, Ennianus and Runo.[^218-1] Ennianus was the father of Belin, Belin of Iago, and Iago of Cadfan, my father. Runo was driven out by the Saxons' attacks after his brother died. He came to this province, and gave his daughter to Duke Hoel, son of the great Hoel who conquered countries beside Arthur. She was the mother of Alan, and Alan the father of your own father Hoel. While he lived, Hoel struck great fear into all Gaul."

### Brian goes to York in a beggar's clothes to find Edwin's seer

Cadwallon spent the winter with Salomon. Together they decided that Brian should cross to Britain and somehow do away with King Edwin's seer. Then Pellitus couldn't use his usual art to warn Edwin of Cadwallon's coming. Brian landed at Hamo's Port and dressed as a beggar. He made himself a sharp iron staff, to kill the man with if chance brought them together. Then he made for York, since Edwin was staying there at the time. Once inside the city, he joined the poor who waited at the king's door for charity.

As Brian came and went, his sister walked out of the hall with a bowl in her hand, to fetch water for the queen. Edwin had carried her off from Worcester, when he raged through the Britons' provinces after Cadwallon fled. As she passed in front of Brian, he recognised her at once. He burst into tears and called her name softly. The girl turned her head at the sound, unsure at first who he was. But as he came closer, she knew her brother. She nearly fainted with fear that some bad luck would give him away, and the enemy would catch him.

They held back from kisses and fond words. As though talking of something else, she told him briefly how things stood at court. She also pointed out Pellitus, the man he was after. The man happened to be walking among the poor just then, while the gifts were handed out.

<a id="page-219"></a>[\[Giles 219\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-219) Once Brian knew Pellitus by sight, he told his sister to slip out of her rooms the next night. She was to meet him outside the city, by an old church, where he'd wait for her in its hidden corners. Then he pushed into the crowd of beggars, at the spot where Pellitus was setting them out. As soon as he had a chance to strike, he raised the staff I mentioned. He drove it into Pellitus under the breast, and killed him with one blow. At once he threw the weapon away and hid among the other beggars. Nobody standing there suspected Brian. With Yahweh's help, Brian then reached the hiding place they'd agreed on.

His sister tried many ways to get out as night came on, but couldn't. Edwin was frightened by Pellitus's murder, and had set guards around the court. They searched every hidden corner and wouldn't let her out. When Brian learned this, he left that place and went to Exeter. There he called the Britons together and told them what he'd done. Afterwards he sent envoys to Cadwallon and fortified the city. He ordered all the British nobles to keep defending their towns and cities. They were to look forward to Cadwallon's coming, since with Salomon's help he'd soon be there to protect them. When this spread over the whole island, Penda, king of Mercia, came to Exeter with the Saxons in a huge host and besieged Brian.

### Cadwallon lands with Salomon's ten thousand and takes Penda at Exeter

Meanwhile Cadwallon landed with the ten thousand soldiers King Salomon had given him, and hurried to the siege Penda was holding. When he got close, he split his force into four parts and went straight at the enemy. As soon as the battle was joined, Penda was captured and his army wiped out.

<a id="page-220"></a>[\[Giles 220\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-220) Having no other way to save himself, Penda submitted to Cadwallon and gave hostages. He promised to make war on the Saxons with him. After this victory, Cadwallon called together his nobles, who'd been scattered for a long time. He marched on Northumbria against Edwin, laying the country waste without stopping. When Edwin heard of it, he joined forces with all the lesser English rulers. He met the Britons in battle on a field called Heavenfield.[^220-1] The fight was quickly over. Edwin was killed, with nearly all the people he had. So were his son Osfrith, and Godbold, king of Orkney, who'd come to help them.

### Cadwallon rages through the English provinces and fights Osric and Áedán mac Gabráin

After his victory Cadwallon went through all the English provinces, raging against the Saxons. He was so savage that he spared neither women nor small children. He wanted to wipe the whole English race out of Britain. Every one of them he found, he tortured in ways nobody had heard of. Then he fought Osric, who'd succeeded Edwin, and killed him, and two of Osric's nephews, the heirs to his crown. Áedán mac Gabráin, king of the Scots, had come to help them, and died there too.[^220-2]

### Oswald becomes king of Northumbria and prays beneath the cross at Heavenfield

Once they were dead, Oswald took the throne of Northumbria. Cadwallon dealt with the others who'd fought him, then chased Oswald north. He drove him as far as the wall the emperor Severus once built between Britain and Scotland. Later he sent Penda, king of Mercia, and most of his forces to the same place to fight him.

<a id="page-221"></a>[\[Giles 221\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-221) One night Penda was besieging Oswald at a place called Heavenfield, which means the field of heaven. Oswald set up a cross there. His men were to shout these words at the top of their voices.

"Let's all get down on our knees together. Let's pray to Yahweh almighty, the living and true god, to defend us from the proud army of the British king and its wicked general Penda. It knows we've taken up a just war to save our people."[^221-1]

They all did as Oswald ordered. At dawn they marched on the enemy, and won the victory their faith deserved. When Cadwallon heard of it, he burned with anger, gathered his army and went after the holy King Oswald. They fought at a place called Burne, where Penda attacked Oswald and killed him.[^221-2]

### Oswiu buys peace from Cadwallon, and his brother and nephew flee to Penda

Oswald died there with thousands of his men, and his brother Oswiu succeeded him as king of Northumbria. Oswiu gave Cadwallon, who now ruled all Britain, rich gifts of gold and silver. That way he won peace with Cadwallon, and submitted to his rule. Soon after, two of Oswiu's own family rose against him: Alhfrith, born of the same father, and his nephew Oethelwald. When they couldn't hold out, they fled to Penda, king of Mercia. They begged him to raise an army and cross the Humber with them, to take King Oswiu's kingdom from him.

But Penda was afraid to break the peace King Cadwallon had set over all Britain. He kept from starting a war without Cadwallon's permission. He waited for a way to stir the British king either to attack Oswiu himself, or to let Penda fight him.

<a id="page-222"></a>[\[Giles 222\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-222) One Pentecost, Cadwallon held the feast in London, wearing the crown of Britain.
