All the English rulers were there except Oswiu, and the British dukes too. Penda went to Cadwallon and asked why Oswiu alone was missing, when the other princes of the Saxons had come. Cadwallon answered that Oswiu was ill. Penda said Oswiu had sent to Germany for Saxons, to take revenge on both of them for his brother Oswald. He went on to say that Oswiu had broken the kingdom's peace. Oswiu alone had started the fighting between them. He'd attacked Alhfrith, king of Northumbria, and his brother's son Oethelwald, and driven them out of their own country.[^222-1] Penda also asked permission either to kill Oswiu or to drive him out of the kingdom.

### Margadud advises Cadwallon to let the English destroy one another

Cadwallon's thoughts were pulled every way. He called his close advisers aside and told them to say what they thought. Many views were offered, and among the rest Margadud, king of Dyfed, spoke.

"My lord, you've set out to drive the English out of Britain. Why turn from your purpose and let them live here in peace? At least let them quarrel with each other, so they wear each other down and are wiped out of our country. There's no keeping faith with anyone who's always lying in wait to trap the one they promised it to. Ever since the Saxons first came into our land, they've plotted against our people and betrayed them. So what loyalty do we owe them? Give Penda permission at once to attack Oswiu. Then a civil war will break out between them, they'll kill each other, and they'll be wiped off our island."

### Oswiu meets Penda at the Winwaed, and Cadwallon's bronze rides over London's west gate

<a id="page-223"></a>[\[Giles 223\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-223) Moved by this and much more, Cadwallon let Penda fight Oswiu. Penda gathered a huge army and made for the Humber. He began to press Oswiu hard, laying waste his land. At last Oswiu, forced by need, promised him countless royal treasures and more gifts than anyone would believe. All Penda had to do was stop ruining the country, give up the war he'd started and go home. Penda wouldn't agree to any of it. Oswiu looked to Yahweh for help and, though outnumbered, fought Penda by the river Winwaed. Penda fell there with thirty dukes, and the victory was Oswiu's.[^223-1]

With Penda dead, Cadwallon gave the kingdom to Penda's son Wulfhere. Wulfhere joined with Eba and Edbert, two leaders of Mercia, and rebelled against Oswiu. But at last, at Cadwallon's order, he made peace with him.

When forty-eight years had passed, Cadwallon, that most noble and powerful king of the Britons, grew weak with age and sickness. He died on the seventeenth of November.[^223-2] The Britons treated his body with fragrant oils, to keep it, and set it with wonderful skill inside a bronze figure cast to his own height. They put this image, armed and very beautiful, on a horse of the same metal over the west gate of London. It stood there as a sign of his victory and to frighten the Saxons. Under it they built a church in honour of Saint Martin, where services are held for him and all the faithful dead.[^223-3]

### Cadwaladr succeeds his father and reigns in peace, until he falls ill

<a id="page-224"></a>[\[Giles 224\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-224) Cadwallon's son Cadwaladr, who Bede calls the young Oethelwald, took the rule of the kingdom after him. At first he governed it bravely and in peace.[^224-1] But twelve years after his crowning, he fell ill, and civil war broke out among the Britons. Cadwaladr's mother had been Penda's sister. They had the same father, but she was born to another woman, from a noble family of the Gewisse. After King Cadwallon made peace with her brother, he married her, and Cadwaladr was their son.

### Famine and plague drive the Britons overseas, and Cadwaladr mourns as he sails

While he lay sick, as I'd begun to say, the Britons turned on each other and ruined their rich country with hateful feuds.[^224-2] More trouble came on top of it. A terrible famine, remembered ever after, gripped the foolish people. Every province was stripped of all food except what hunting brought. A deadly plague followed it, and in a short time it killed so many people that the living couldn't bury them. So the miserable people who were left fled the country in bands, and made for lands overseas. Under the swelling sails they cried out and sang: "You've given us up, Yahweh, like sheep to be eaten, and scattered us among the nations."[^224-3]

<a id="page-225"></a>[\[Giles 225\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-225) King Cadwaladr himself, sailing for Brittany with a pitiful fleet, added to their mourning. "We sinners are lost, because of our monstrous crimes. We never stopped offending Yahweh while we still had time to repent. So the vengeance of its power is on us, tearing us out of our native soil. Neither the Romans long ago, nor the Scots and Picts after them, nor the cunning betrayals of the Saxons could wipe us out. 

"We won our country back from them so often for nothing, since it wasn't Yahweh's will that we should reign there forever. Yahweh, the true judge, saw we'd never give up our crimes, and that no one could drive our race out of the kingdom. Wanting to punish the foolish, it sent its anger, and that's why we're leaving our own country in crowds.

"Come back, then, Romans! Come back, Scots and Picts! Come back, Ambrones and Saxons![^225-1] Look, Britain lies open to you, emptied by Yahweh's fury, as you could never empty it yourselves. It isn't your strength that drives us out, but the power of the king of heaven, who we never stopped offending."

### Cadwaladr reaches Alan in Brittany, and the Saxons settle the empty island

Still mourning like this, Cadwaladr landed on the coast of Brittany. He came with all his people to King Alan, Salomon's nephew, who welcomed him with honour. So Britain was emptied of its people, except a few in parts of Wales who'd been spared, and for eleven years the Britons dreaded it. It was deadly for the Saxons too at that time, since they kept on dying there. When the sickness ended, those Saxons who were left followed their constant custom. They told their countrymen in Germany that the island of Britain had no native people, and would be easily theirs if they came to live in it.

<a id="page-226"></a>[\[Giles 226\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-226) When they heard this, that wicked people gathered a countless host of men and women. They landed in Northumbria, and settled the empty provinces from Albany to Cornwall. Nobody was there to stop them except the poor handful of Britons still alive, who lived hidden in the woods of Wales. From that time the Britons' power in the island ended, and the English began to reign.

### An angel's voice forbids Cadwaladr to sail home and sends him to Rome

Some time later, once that people had grown strong, Cadwaladr remembered his kingdom, now free of the plague. He asked Alan for help to restore him to his old power. Alan agreed. But as Cadwaladr was fitting out his fleet, an angel's voice thundered to him to give up what he'd begun.

Yahweh didn't want the Britons to reign in the island of Britain any longer, until the time came that Merlin had predicted to Arthur.[^226-1]

The angel also told Cadwaladr to go to Rome, to Pope Sergius. There, after doing penance, he'd be counted among the blessed.[^226-2] It said too that the Britons would win the island again someday through the merit of their faith, once the fated time came. But that wouldn't happen before the Britons had his relics and carried them from Rome to Britain. Then at last the bones of the other saints, hidden from the pagans when they invaded, would also be brought to light. Then they'd recover the kingdom they'd lost. When Cadwaladr heard this, he went at once to King Alan and told him what had been shown to him.

### Alan reads the old prophecies, and Cadwaladr goes to Rome

<a id="page-227"></a>[\[Giles 227\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-227) Then Alan took various books and searched them all. He read the sayings of the eagle that spoke at Shaftesbury, and the verses of the Sibyl and of Merlin.[^227-1] He wanted to see whether what Cadwaladr had been told agreed with what was written. Alan found nothing against it. So Alan urged Cadwaladr to obey Yahweh's plan, put Britain aside and do as the angel's warning said.

Alan also advised sending Cadwaladr's son Ivor and his nephew Ini to the island, to rule what was left of the Britons. Then a people born of their ancient line wouldn't lose its freedom to the barbarians.[^227-2]

Then Cadwaladr gave up worldly things for the sake of Yahweh and heaven.

He came to Rome and was confirmed by Pope Sergius. Not long after, a sudden illness struck him. He died on the twentieth of April, six hundred eighty-nine years after Christ became man, freed from the flesh to enter the hall of the kingdom of heaven.[^227-3]

### Ivor and Ini fight the English forty-nine years, and the Britons become Welsh

Ivor and Ini gathered ships, took with them whoever they could, and landed on the island. For forty-nine years they attacked the English savagely, but it did little good. The plague, the famine and their usual quarrels had made that proud people fall so far that they couldn't keep their enemies away any more.

<a id="page-228"></a>[\[Giles 228\]](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-latin.md#page-228) As they grew rough, they stopped being called Britons, and were known as Welsh. The name came from their leader Gualo, or from their queen Guales, or from their wild ways.[^228-1] The Saxons acted more wisely. They kept peace and harmony among themselves. They farmed the fields, and rebuilt the cities and towns. So they threw off the Britons' rule, and now held all Loegria, under Æthelstan, the first of them to wear the crown.[^228-2] But the Welsh, fallen from the Britons' nobility, never won back the island. Turning now against the Saxons, now against one another, they never stopped suffering slaughter at home.

### Geoffrey leaves the later kings to Caradoc, William of Malmesbury and Henry of Huntingdon

I leave the kings who came after them in Wales to Caradoc of Llancarfan, who lives in my own time, to write about.[^228-3] The rulers of the Saxons I leave to William of Malmesbury and Henry of Huntingdon.[^228-4] I tell those two to say nothing of the British kings. They don't have that book in the British language that Walter, archdeacon of Oxford, brought from Brittany. It gave a true account of the Britons' history, and I've taken care to translate it into Latin in this way, in honour of those princes.

---

## Sources

Giles, J. A., ed. *Galfredi Monumetensis Historia Britonum, nunc primum in Anglia, novem codd. msstis collatis*. London: D. Nutt, 1844. [Florence scan](https://archive.org/details/bub_gb_isqApyNTSs0C), [scans kept with this book](../geoffrey-of-monmouths-history-of-the-kings-scans.md).

Lewis, Charlton T., and Charles Short. *A Latin Dictionary, Founded on Andrews' Edition of Freund's Latin Dictionary*. Oxford: Clarendon Press, 1879. Cited as Lewis and Short, by page, for the sense of a Latin word Giles prints as a name.

San-Marte [Albert Schulz], ed. *Gottfried's von Monmouth Historia regum Britanniae, mit literar-historischer Einleitung und ausführlichen Anmerkungen, und Brut Tysylio, altwälsche Chronik in deutscher Uebersetzung*. Halle: Eduard Anton, 1854. [Michigan scan](https://archive.org/details/historiaregumbr00schugoog). Read beside Giles for a doubtful line, and cited as San-Marte for his notes on it.

## Endnotes

<a id="new-in-this-translation"></a>*\* New in this translation: written for it, not taken from Giles's edition of 1844.*

[^1-1]: In the whole story: [Brutus, the Picts and the Irish come to settle Britain and Ireland](../../story-map.md#the-first-peoples-settle-britain-and-ireland).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^1-2]: Gildas's [*On the Ruin of Britain*](../../de-excidio-britanniae/de-excidio-britanniae.md), written in the 500s CE, and Bede's *Ecclesiastical History*, finished in 731 CE, are the oldest histories of Britain, and neither names Arthur. Gildas tells of the victory at Mount Badon without naming its leader, and Bede follows him. The [*History of the Britons*](../../historia-brittonum/historia-brittonum.md), which Geoffrey uses without naming it, is the first book to name Arthur.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^1-3]: No one has ever found the very old book Walter is said to have given Geoffrey, and most historians take it for Geoffrey's own invention, a way to give his story authority. By "British," Geoffrey means the Britons' language, the ancestor of Welsh, Cornish and Breton.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^2-1]: Minerva was the Roman goddess of wisdom and the arts. Geoffrey means Robert's own learning, which he hopes will give the book its flavor, as salt gives food its taste.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^2-2]: Geoffrey takes the island's size, eight hundred miles by two hundred, from the opening of Gildas's [*On the Ruin of Britain*](../../de-excidio-britanniae/de-excidio-britanniae.md), and his praise of its fields, rivers and meadows follows Gildas closely.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^3-1]: Bede opens his history by naming the island's five languages: English, British, Irish, Pictish and Latin, the language of the Church. Geoffrey turns them into five peoples. His Scots are the Irish who settled the north of Britain, and Scotland is named after them.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^4-1]: The [*History of the Britons*](../../historia-brittonum/historia-brittonum.md#page-152) tells the same prophecy and the same death, but there Brutus kills his father while playing with other boys, not hunting.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^7-1]: Greek fire was a burning liquid the Byzantine navy used from the 600s CE, which water couldn't put out. Geoffrey gives it to Greeks of Trojan times, many centuries before anyone had it.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^11-1]: Danaans is the name Homer's *Iliad* gives the Greeks, and Mempricius uses it here for the Greeks around them.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^12-1]: Earlier the brother taken prisoner is Antigonus, and Anacletus is his companion. Here Geoffrey has Pandrasus call Anacletus his brother, and the English follows his words: "my brother Anacletus".<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^14-1]: Brutus's prayer and the goddess's answer are verse in the Latin, in the elegiac couplets of Ovid, a long line and then a shorter one. The English keeps their lines but not their rhythm, nor the answer's repeated opening, "There's an island in the ocean", which the Latin says twice.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^15-1]: In Homer's *Odyssey*, the Sirens are creatures whose singing draws sailors onto the rocks to die. Odysseus has his crew plug their ears with wax and tie him to the mast so he can hear them and live. Geoffrey makes them sea monsters that circle the ships.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^15-2]: Goffar is king of Aquitaine and leader of the Poitevins, the people of Poitou. His name "the Pict" most likely comes from the Pictavi, the old people of Poitiers, not from the Picts of Britain.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^18-1]: No poem of Homer's says anything of Brutus or Tours. Geoffrey borrows Homer's name to give the story weight, as he borrows Gildas's for Lud's quarrel a few pages on.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^20-1]: Geoffrey's "crooked Greek," *curvum Graecum*, is taken by scholars as a play on the Welsh name for Welsh, *Cymraeg*, as if it were *cam Roeg*, "crooked Greek".<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^20-2]: The horn is the Latin *cornu*, for the long peninsula Cornwall makes. Its name really comes from the Cornovii, the Celtic people who lived there, with the Old English *wealas*, "foreigners," added.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^21-1]: Geoffrey says twelve *cubiti*, each the length from elbow to fingertip, about 45 centimetres.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^21-2]: *Lam* is the Welsh *llam*, a leap. Giles's index puts the cliff at the Hoe in Plymouth.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^22-1]: Trinovantum comes from the Trinovantes, the people north of the Thames that Julius Caesar names, and Geoffrey reads it as *Troia Nova*, New Troy.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^22-2]: *Kaer* is the Welsh *caer*, a fort or walled city. At the end of Part 3, Geoffrey says Kaerlud was worn down into the name London.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^22-3]: No surviving book of Gildas's tells of Lud or Nennius. Geoffrey cites him for a story he never wrote, as he cites Homer for Tours.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^22-4]: Eli was the priest of the shrine at Shiloh who raised the prophet Samuel. The Philistines beat Israel in battle, took the Ark of the Covenant, the gold-covered chest that held the law Moses was given, and killed Eli's two sons. When Eli heard the news, he fell from his seat and died (1 Samuel 4). Geoffrey sets Brutus's founding beside it to date it.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^22-5]: Aeneas Silvius was Aeneas's son by Lavinia, so he was strictly Brutus's great-uncle, and Roman legend counts him the third king of Alba Longa, after Aeneas and Ascanius.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^23-1]: In the whole story: [Brutus's heirs rule Britain, and Leir divides his kingdom among his daughters](../../story-map.md#leir-divides-his-kingdom).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^23-2]: Loegria is Geoffrey's Latin for the Welsh name for England. The French romances turn it into Logres, the name they give Arthur's own kingdom.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^23-3]: The Welsh call themselves *Cymry* and their country *Cymru*. Geoffrey derives the name from Camber, but it really comes from an old British word for fellow countrymen.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^23-4]: Albany, *Albania* in the Latin, is from *Alba*, the Gaelic name for Scotland. In Geoffrey it means the north of Britain beyond the Humber.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^23-5]: The Huns came out of the steppes of Central Asia into Europe in the 370s CE, well over a thousand years after the time Geoffrey gives Humber. He makes the river Humber take its name from this king.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^26-1]: Giles prints the girl's name *Sabren*, the British form Geoffrey gives. In Welsh it's Hafren, and the Severn is still *Afon Hafren* in Welsh today. Sabrina is the river's Latin name, and the English Severn comes from it.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^26-2]: Samuel was the last of the judges who led Israel before it had kings. As a boy he served Eli the priest at Shiloh. When the people demanded a king, Samuel anointed Saul, and later the boy who'd become King David in Saul's place (1 Samuel 8–16).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^26-3]: Sodom was one of the cities the Bible says Yahweh destroyed with fire and sulfur from the sky. When two angels came to stay with Lot there, the men of the town surrounded Lot's house and demanded the visitors, meaning to rape them (Genesis 19). Medieval writers took Sodom's sin to be sex between men, and Geoffrey's Latin calls Mempricius's lust "Sodom's".<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^27-1]: Saul was Israel's first king, anointed by Samuel. He fought the Philistines all his reign and lost the throne to King David. Wounded in his last battle, on Mount Gilboa, he fell on his own sword rather than be taken (1 Samuel 31).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^27-2]: Eurystheus is the king who set Hercules his twelve labours, a king of Mycenae in the Greek myths, not of Sparta.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^27-3]: Kaerebrauc is York. Its Roman name was Eboracum, and its Welsh name is still *Efrog*. Geoffrey makes the name come from Ebraucus.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^27-4]: Gad and Nathan were the prophets at King David's court, and Asaph was the leader of his singers, whose name heads twelve of the Psalms.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^27-5]: Alclud is Dumbarton Rock on the river Clyde, which Giles's index calls Dunbritton. Agned is the hill where the [*History of the Britons*](../../historia-brittonum/historia-brittonum.md) puts one of Arthur's twelve battles, and later writers took the Castle of Girls for Edinburgh Castle. Where Giles prints *montem Dolosorum*, "the mountain of the deceitful," the English follows the name the place is known by, Mount Dolorous, "the mountain of grief." No one knows where it was.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^27-6]: These are the twenty sons and thirty daughters Geoffrey gives Ebraucus, many of them Welsh names, and they're known from his list alone. Bladud, Sisillius and Hector here aren't the kings of those names later on.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^28-1]: These are the thirty daughters Geoffrey gives Ebraucus, and like their brothers they're known from his list alone. Many are Welsh women's names in Latin spelling, as Angarad and Nest, and Geoffrey brings them in to send them all to Italy as brides.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^28-2]: The Sabines were the neighbours of early Rome. Roman legend says that Romulus's Romans had no wives, so they invited the Sabines to a festival and carried off their daughters. Geoffrey has the Latin and Sabine women refuse to marry the Trojans.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^28-3]: Giles prints the king's name as *Leir* and the city as *Kaerleir* here, but his own index names the king Leil and the city Kaerleil, now Carlisle. The English follows the index, so this king isn't confused with Leir, his great-grandson.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^28-4]: Solomon, King David's son, built the first temple in Jerusalem. The queen of Sheba heard of his wisdom and came from her own country with spices, gold and jewels to test him with hard questions. He answered every one, and she said what she'd been told wasn't half of it (1 Kings 10).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^29-1]: Geoffrey comes back to the eagle in Part 12, where its prophecy at Shaftesbury is one of those that tell the Britons they'll win back the island.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^29-2]: These prophets are out of order. Haggai preached at Jerusalem after the return from Babylon, centuries after Amos, and Geoffrey sets him beside prophets of the 700s BCE.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^29-3]: The Roman writer Solinus, in the 200s CE, tells of an undying fire in Minerva's temple at Bath whose embers turned into stony lumps. It's usually taken to be coal, which the Romans burned there.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^29-4]: Elijah was the prophet who told King Ahab of Israel that no rain would fall until he said so, because Ahab and Queen Jezebel had turned the people to the god Baal. The drought ended after Elijah beat Baal's prophets in a contest on Mount Carmel. The time of three years and six months comes from the Letter of James.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^29-5]: Kaerleir is Leicester, and *Leircestre* is its name in Old English, the Saxons' language. Geoffrey makes it come from Leir, but it comes from the Ligore, the people who lived on the Soar, whose old name was the Ligor.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^33-1]: Giles's index takes Karitia for Calais, with a "perhaps." No one knows for sure which city in Gaul Geoffrey meant.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^34-1]: Janus was the Roman god of doorways and beginnings, shown with two faces, one looking forward and one back. The month of January is named for him, which is why the workmen begin their year's work at his feast.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^35-1]: Margan is Margam, near Port Talbot, whose name Geoffrey makes come from Marganus.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^36-1]: Geoffrey dates it the Roman way, eleven days before the Kalends of May, the day Rome kept as its birthday. Roman legend says that the twins were suckled by a she-wolf, and that Romulus killed Remus and named the city after himself.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^36-2]: Geoffrey gives these kings nothing but their names and their order.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^36-3]: Giles prints her name *Widen*. Wikipedia's articles on the story call her Judon, and the English follows them.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^36-4]: Thomas Norton and Thomas Sackville made this story into *Gorboduc*, or *Ferrex and Porrex*, first played in 1561 and the first English tragedy written in blank verse.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^37-1]: Giles prints the name *Ymner*, and his index does too. Wikipedia's articles on the story call him Pinner, and the English follows them.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^38-1]: Welsh law books of the Middle Ages name Dyfnwal Moelmud, the Welsh form of Dunvallo Molmutius, as the king whose laws came before those of Hywel the Good in the 900s CE.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^38-2]: No surviving book of Gildas's tells of Dunvallo or his laws. Geoffrey cites him for a story he never wrote, as he did for Lud's quarrel in Part 1.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^38-3]: In the whole story: [Belinus and Brennius march on Rome, and their heirs rule Britain down to Lud](../../story-map.md#belinus-and-brennius-march-on-rome).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^39-1]: The Morini were a people of the coast of Gaul facing Britain, around what's now Boulogne. Julius Caesar sailed from their country when he invaded Britain, and Cheulfus is known only from this line of Geoffrey's.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^40-1]: Giles's index takes Calaterium for Galtres, the royal forest north of York. Since Brennius has landed in Albany, Geoffrey may have meant a wood further north, and no one knows for sure.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^42-1]: Dyfed is the far southwest of Wales, the land of the people the Romans called the Demetae, and Geoffrey's *Demetia*. St Davids, where Wales's patron saint had his church, stands on its coast.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^42-2]: Hamo's Port is Southampton, as Giles's index says. Geoffrey makes its name come from Hamo, a Roman commander who's killed there in Part 4.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^42-3]: These are the four great roads of medieval England: the Fosse Way, Watling Street, Ermine Street and the Icknield Way. The Romans built the first three. Henry of Huntingdon, writing in Geoffrey's time, names all four and says the king's peace protected them.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^42-4]: No book of Gildas's holds these laws, as Part 2's note on Dunvallo says. Alfred did gather older laws into his own law code, but the kings it names are English ones, Ine of Wessex, Offa of Mercia and Æthelberht of Kent, not any British king.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^43-1]: The Allobroges were a Gallic people between the Rhône and the Alps, around what's now Geneva and Grenoble, and Rome conquered them in 121 BCE. Seginus and his rule over them are Geoffrey's own.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^44-1]: Geoffrey calls it Neustria, the name of the western Frankish kingdom before the Norsemen settled there in the 900s CE and it became Normandy, as Giles's index says.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^46-1]: Lars Porsena was an Etruscan king who, Roman legend says, besieged Rome in the first years of its republic. Geoffrey borrows his name for a consul, and Rome never had a consul named Porsenna or Gabias; Giles prints them *Porsena* and *Gabius*, and this translation spells them as Wikipedia's article on Belinus does.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^47-1]: The Senones were a Gallic people, and about 390 BCE an army of Gauls led by Brennus, one of their chiefs, beat the Romans at the river Allia and took Rome. Geoffrey makes his Brennius that Brennus, and the brothers' march on Rome his own retelling of the sack.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^48-1]: The Roman historian Livy tells how Brennus's Gauls held Rome until the Romans agreed to pay them a thousand pounds of gold to leave. When the Romans complained that the Gauls' weights were false, Brennus threw his sword on the scale and said, "Woe to the conquered." No Roman history has him rule Italy or tells how he died.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^48-2]: The City of the Legions is Caerleon, on the Usk in south Wales, whose Welsh name means the fortress of the legion. Rome's Second Legion was based there from about 75 CE, so Geoffrey's account of the name is close to the truth, though he has Belinus found the city long before.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^48-3]: Billingsgate was a water gate and wharf on the Thames below London Bridge, later famous for its fish market. Its name probably comes from an Englishman called Billing, not from Belinus.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^49-1]: Giles prints *Gurgiunt Brabtruc*. Wikipedia's article on him calls him Gurguit Barbtruc, and the English follows it.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^49-2]: In the whole story: [Brutus, the Picts and the Irish come to settle Britain and Ireland](../../story-map.md#the-first-peoples-settle-britain-and-ireland).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^49-3]: Irish legend tells of Partholón too. In the *Book of Invasions*, he leads the first people to settle Ireland after the Flood, sailing there by way of Spain, and his people all die of a plague. The [*History of the Britons*](../../historia-brittonum/historia-brittonum.md) brings the Irish from Spain as well.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^50-1]: Geoffrey gives the English name as *Pa marchitie lage*, his Old English for "the Mercian law," the law of the Mercians of the English Midlands, which English lawyers of his day named beside the laws of Wessex and of the Danes. Geoffrey turns it into the law of a British queen, Marcia.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^50-2]: Giles prints *Kimarus*. Wikipedia's article on him calls him Kinarius, and the English follows it.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^51-1]: Giles prints *Moranorum*, and no people of that name is known. Wikipedia's article on Morvidus calls the invaders the Moriani, and the English follows it.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^52-1]: Giles prints *Arthgallo*. Wikipedia's article on him calls him Archgallo, and the English follows it.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^52-2]: Geoffrey's Latin calls him *Pius*, and *pietas* is the love and loyalty owed to family, not piety towards a god. So the English calls him the Dutiful.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^53-1]: Giles prints *Vigenius*. Wikipedia's article on him calls him Ingenius, and the English follows it.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^54-1]: From Runo on, Geoffrey gives these kings little but their names and their order, and they're known from his list alone. Where Giles prints a name another way, the English follows Wikipedia's article on the king, as Gerennus for *Geruntius*, Edadus for *Eldadus* and Digueillus for *Gligueillus*.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^55-1]: Geoffrey's Latin is *deus joculatorum*, the god of the *joculatores*, the singers, players and jugglers who entertained at courts in his own day, the jongleurs of France. Blegabred is known from Geoffrey alone.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^55-2]: Londres is London's name in French, which the Normans brought in 1066 CE. Its Roman name was Londinium, and no one knows for sure where that comes from, but it isn't from Lud.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^55-3]: Ludgate was the westernmost gate in London's walls, and the street running up to St Paul's, Ludgate Hill, keeps its name. Its name probably comes from an Old English word for a back gate, not from Lud.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^56-1]: The Ruteni were a Gallic people of the south, around what's now Rodez, far from the Channel. Caesar sailed for Britain from the country of the Morini, around Boulogne, and Geoffrey seems to have mixed up the two peoples.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^56-2]: In the whole story: [The Romans rule Britain from Julius Caesar until they leave](../../story-map.md#the-romans-rule-britain).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^58-1]: Giles's index takes Dorobellum for Deal, in Kent, near where historians think Caesar first landed in 55 BCE. No one knows for sure which town Geoffrey meant.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^58-2]: This Belinus isn't the king of Part 3, who'd been dead for generations. Geoffrey gives the name to Cassivellaunus's commander, and tells nothing more of him.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^59-1]: Caesar's own account of his second invasion tells of a tribune, Quintus Laberius Durus, killed fighting the Britons. Later histories, Bede's among them, turned his name into Labienus, the name of Caesar's best-known officer, and Geoffrey has it from them.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^60-1]: *Crocea mors* is Latin for "yellow death" or "saffron death." The sword and its name are Geoffrey's own, and Caesar's account of Britain names no Nennius.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^62-1]: No place called Odnea is known. Giles's index puts it near Boulogne, on the coast of the Morini that Caesar's fleets sailed from.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^63-1]: Giles prints *Evelinus*. Wikipedia's article on Mandubracius calls him Cuelinus, and the English follows it.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^64-1]: Androgeus tells the killing differently from the way Geoffrey has just told it. In Geoffrey's own account, Cuelinus cuts off Hirelglas's head after their quarrel; in the letter, Hirelglas falls on his own sword.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^65-1]: Geoffrey's *Rutupi portus* is Richborough, in Kent, the Romans' Rutupiae, as Giles's index says. Claudius's army landed there in 43 CE, and it became the Romans' chief port for the crossing to Gaul.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^66-1]: The Roman poet Lucan wrote the *Pharsalia*, an epic of the civil war between Caesar and Pompey, in the 60s CE. The line, from its second book, is spoken against Caesar and mocks him for running from the Britons whose island he'd gone looking for.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^67-1]: Pompey the Great was Caesar's ally and then his rival. Caesar marched into Italy against him in 49 BCE, beat him in Greece the next year, and Pompey was killed in Egypt as he fled.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^68-1]: Kymbelinus is Cunobelinus, a real king who ruled much of southeast Britain from Camulodunum, now Colchester, until about 40 CE, and struck coins with his name. Shakespeare took him from Geoffrey's story, by way of Holinshed's *Chronicles*, for his play *Cymbeline*.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^68-2]: Giles prints *Arviragus*, as Juvenal's line on page 72 does. Wikipedia's article on him calls him Arvirargus, and the English follows it.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^68-3]: Geoffrey says the Britons called him *Levis hamo*, as Giles prints it, and other editions read *Lelius Hamo*. No Roman of either name is known.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^69-1]: Southampton's name comes from the Old English *Hamtun*, not from Hamo. Geoffrey's Latin calls it *Hamtonia* here, and Hamo's Port in Part 3.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^71-1]: Giles prints *Genuissa*. Wikipedia's article on her calls her Venissa, and the English follows it. No daughter of Claudius married a British king, and she's Geoffrey's own.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^71-2]: Gloucester was Glevum to the Romans, a fort and then a town for retired soldiers, founded under the emperor Nerva in the 90s CE. Its name comes from the British word behind Glevum, which Geoffrey's Welsh *Kaerglou* keeps, not from Claudius.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^71-3]: Church tradition says Peter, the leader of Jesus's twelve apostles, led the church of Antioch before he went to Rome. There he led the church, the first of the line of bishops the popes count themselves from, and was killed under Nero.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^71-4]: Mark is the writer the second gospel is named for. Church tradition makes him the founder of the church of Alexandria, in Egypt, and its first bishop.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^72-1]: Vespasian commanded a legion in Claudius's invasion of Britain in 43 CE and conquered the southwest, taking more than twenty hill forts and the Isle of Wight. He was made emperor in 69 CE.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^72-2]: The Roman poet Juvenal wrote his *Satires* in the early 100s CE. In the fourth, the emperor Domitian, a bald Nero as Juvenal calls him, gathers his advisers over a huge turbot, and one of them says the fish foretells a victory: a king captured, or Arviragus thrown from his chariot. It's the only mention of an Arviragus older than Geoffrey.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^73-1]: Westmorland is a county of northwest England. Its name means the land of the people west of the moors, not Marius's land.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^73-2]: The Scots, the *Scoti* of the Latin writers, were Irish raiders and settlers who crossed to the west of what's now Scotland and gave it its name. Bede tells how the Picts, coming from Scythia, asked the Scots in Ireland for wives, and Geoffrey retells it with the Britons turning them down.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^74-1]: This Coillus is Marius's son, not the king of Part 3's list. Welsh tradition names Lucius's father Coel, and Wikipedia's article on Lucius gives him that name.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^74-2]: Bede tells of Lucius writing to Eleutherius, who led the Church in Rome from about 174 to 189 CE. Historians think the story grew from a misreading: the Lucius whose letter reached Eleutherius was probably Lucius Abgar, king of Edessa in Syria, whose fortress was called Britium.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^74-3]: Giles prints *Faganum et Duvianum*. Wikipedia's articles name them Fagan and Deruvian, and the English follows them. Welsh churches still honour both as saints.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^75-1]: Geoffrey's Latin calls them *flamines* and *archiflamines*, after the priests of Rome's gods, and makes them a pagan church with the same seats the bishops and archbishops later had. No such priests are known in Britain.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^75-2]: Caerleon stands in Gwent, now Monmouthshire, just east of Glamorgan, whose name Geoffrey gives the region. Glamorgan is the coast of south Wales from Cardiff west to Swansea.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^75-3]: No book of Gildas's on Aurelius Ambrosius's victory is known. His [*On the Ruin of Britain*](../../de-excidio-britanniae/de-excidio-britanniae.md) praises Ambrosius, but names neither Lucius nor his teachers. Geoffrey cites him for a story he never wrote, as he did for Dunvallo's laws.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^76-1]: In the whole story: [The Romans rule Britain from Julius Caesar until they leave](../../story-map.md#the-romans-rule-britain).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^76-2]: Bede dates Lucius's letter to Eleutherius to 156 CE, and Geoffrey puts Lucius's death in that year instead. Eleutherius led the Church in Rome only from about 174 CE.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^77-1]: This Fulgenius isn't the king of the same name in Part 3, Cherin's son. Geoffrey says a little later that he was Bassianus's uncle, his mother's brother.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^77-2]: Bede says Severus built a wall of turf across Britain from sea to sea, to keep out the tribes he hadn't conquered. The stone wall across the north, from the Solway to the Tyne, is Hadrian's, built nearly ninety years before him, and Severus had it repaired.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^77-3]: Giles's Latin doesn't say who was buried at York. The English takes it to be Severus, who really did die at York, in 211 CE.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^77-4]: Bassianus is the emperor known as Caracalla, born Lucius Septimius Bassianus, who ruled with his brother Geta and had him murdered in 211 CE. Their mother, Julia Domna, came from Syria; Geoffrey's British mother is his own.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^79-1]: Allectus was really Carausius's treasurer, not Rome's envoy. He murdered Carausius in 293 CE and ruled in his place until Constantius's commander Asclepiodotus beat him in 296. Geoffrey makes that Roman officer the Britons' king.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^80-1]: Giles prints its British name *Nautgallim* and its English one *Gallemborne*. It's the Walbrook, which ran through Roman London into the Thames and now flows under the City's streets. Its name more likely meant the Britons' stream or the stream by the wall, and Geoffrey made his story from it.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^80-2]: Maximian Herculius was Diocletian's fellow emperor, who ruled the west, Britain with it, from 286 to 305 CE. He isn't the Maximianus who comes later in this part.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^81-1]: Geoffrey copies his account of the martyrs almost word for word from Gildas's [*On the Ruin of Britain*](../../de-excidio-britanniae/de-excidio-britanniae.md#page-31), chapters 10 and 11, so the "us" in this passage is Gildas's.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^81-2]: The barbarians' partition is Gildas's word for the English conquest, which by his day had cut the Britons off from the martyrs' shrines in the east of the island, as Alban's at Verulamium.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^81-3]: Gildas doesn't name Alban's confessor. Geoffrey is the first to call him Amphibalus, a name he seems to have made from *amphibalus*, the Latin word for a cloak, since in the old story Alban saves the priest by trading clothes with him.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^81-4]: In John's gospel, Jesus calls himself the good shepherd, who lays down his life for his sheep rather than run from the wolf as a hired hand would, and he died on the cross for them. Alban gave his life for the man he hid in the same way.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^81-5]: The gates are heaven's. The book of Revelation, the last in the Bible, sees a new Jerusalem come down from heaven, a city with twelve gates of pearl where Yahweh lives among the saved, and Gildas and Geoffrey send the martyrs straight through them.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^81-6]: In the whole story: [Constantine is born in Britain to a British king's daughter](../../story-map.md#constantine-is-born-in-britain).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^81-7]: Welsh tradition knows Coel as Coel Hen, Coel the Old, a king in the north, and later readers made him the Old King Cole of the nursery rhyme. Geoffrey ties him to Colchester, whose name he seems to read as Coel's.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^82-1]: Helena, Constantine's mother, was no Briton. She was most likely born in Bithynia, in what's now Turkey, and in old age went to Jerusalem, where legend says she found the cross of Christ. Geoffrey makes her a British king's daughter.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^82-2]: Constantius did die at York, in 306 CE, and his soldiers there proclaimed Constantine emperor. Constantine was born in Naissus, now Niš in Serbia, not in Britain.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^83-1]: Constantine beat Maxentius at the Milvian Bridge, outside Rome, in 312 CE, and ruled the whole empire from 324 until he died in 337.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^83-2]: Helena's uncles, Coel's brothers, are known from Geoffrey's story alone, and this Marius isn't Arvirargus's son of Part 4.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^83-3]: Welsh tradition knows Octavius as Eudaf Hen, Eudaf the Old, whose daughter Elen marries the emperor Maximus in the Welsh tale *The Dream of Macsen Wledig*. The Gewisse, whose duke Geoffrey makes him, were the people of the West Saxon kings, known only from the 500s CE on.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^84-1]: Giles prints *comes oppidi municipii*. A *municipium* was a Roman town whose people were citizens, and the English takes the words in that sense, not as a town's name. The same word gives Gracianus Municeps his name at the end of this part.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^84-2]: In the whole story: [The Romans rule Britain from Julius Caesar until they leave](../../story-map.md#the-romans-rule-britain).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^85-1]: Maximianus is Magnus Maximus, a general born in Spain, whose army in Britain made him emperor in 383 CE. He took it across to Gaul and ruled the west from Trier until 388. Geoffrey calls him Maximianus, as the [*History of the Britons*](../../historia-brittonum/historia-brittonum.md) does, and makes his father a Briton.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^85-2]: Giles prints *Leoninus* here, and *Leolinum* on page 83. The English names him Leolinus both times.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^91-1]: This is Geoffrey's story of how Brittany got its Britons. The [*History of the Britons*](../../historia-brittonum/historia-brittonum.md) says the soldiers Maximianus took from Britain were given lands in Gaul and never came home, and Geoffrey gives them a king, Conan Meriadoc. Britons really did cross to Armorica, in the 400s and 500s CE.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^91-2]: Gratian was caught and killed at Lyon in 383 CE, after his army went over to Maximus. In 387 Maximus drove Valentinian out of Italy, where he ruled from Milan, not Rome.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^92-1]: Ursula is the saint of Cologne's legend, who sailed with eleven thousand virgins and was killed with them by the Huns. An old inscription in a Cologne church honours virgins martyred there, and the eleven thousand may come from a misreading of a smaller number.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^93-1]: Giles prints *Guanius*. Wikipedia's articles on Dionotus and Gracianus Municeps call him Wanius, and the English follows them. Neither king is known outside Geoffrey's story.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^93-2]: Giles prints *Ambrones*, the name of an ancient Germanic people, which Gildas had used for the raiders from the north as a word for savages. The English says what Geoffrey means by it.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^93-3]: This Gratian isn't the emperor. Geoffrey calls him *Municeps*, a citizen of a free town. Historians take him from Gratian, the soldier Rome's army in Britain made emperor in 406 CE.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^93-4]: Magnus Maximus wasn't killed in Rome. The eastern emperor Theodosius beat him in 388 CE and had him put to death at Aquileia, in northern Italy.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^94-1]: In the whole story: [The Picts and the Irish raid Britain after the Romans leave](../../story-map.md#the-picts-and-irish-raid-britain).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^94-2]: From here to the Britons' letter to Agitius, Geoffrey follows Gildas's [*On the Ruin of Britain*](../../de-excidio-britanniae/de-excidio-britanniae.md#page-33), chapters 14 to 20, often word for word: the legion, the wall, the Romans' farewell, the towers on the shore and the farmers on the wall.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^94-3]: Gildas says this first wall was built of turf, not stone, by a crowd with no sense and nobody to lead it, so it did no good. Geoffrey leaves that out.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^95-1]: No archbishop of London is known from these years, and Guithelin is known only from Geoffrey's story. His name is close to Vitalinus, the name the [*History of the Britons*](../../historia-brittonum/historia-brittonum.md#page-209) gives to Vortigern's grandfather and to the man who fought Ambrosius at Guoloph.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^95-2]: The sheep with no shepherd are a picture from the Bible. In Matthew's gospel, Jesus looks at the crowds following him and pities them, because they're harassed and helpless, like sheep without a shepherd to lead and guard them. Guithelin means the Britons were just as lost.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^96-1]: In the Sermon on the Mount, in Matthew's gospel, Jesus tells his followers not to give what's holy to dogs, or throw their pearls in front of pigs, which will only trample them and turn on whoever threw them. Geoffrey means the farmers can't value the teaching they're given.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^97-1]: Geoffrey quotes this letter from Gildas's [*On the Ruin of Britain*](../../de-excidio-britanniae/de-excidio-britanniae.md#page-36). Many take Agitius to be Aëtius, the Roman general who was consul for the third time in 446 CE, though Gildas's own form of the name is kept.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^97-2]: In the whole story: [The Britons bring Constantine from Brittany and make him king](../../story-map.md#constantine-comes-from-brittany).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^97-3]: Letavia is the Latin form of Llydaw, the Welsh name for Brittany. Armorica is its old Roman name.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^99-1]: "Christ conquers, Christ rules, Christ reigns" opens the *Laudes regiae*, the royal praises, a chant sung in the Middle Ages when a king was crowned or came to a church on a great feast. Guithelin greets Constantine as a king already.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^99-2]: Geoffrey's Constantine is the soldier the Roman army in Britain made emperor in 407 CE, as Constantine III. He took the army across to Gaul, as Maximus had done. Geoffrey makes him a prince of Brittany who comes to save Britain.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^100-1]: The real Constantine III gave himself up to the emperor Honorius's general at Arles in 411 CE, and was put to death on the way to Italy.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^100-2]: In the whole story: [Vortigern has King Moine killed and takes the throne](../../story-map.md#vortigern-takes-the-throne).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^100-3]: A new king was anointed with holy oil by a bishop when he was crowned, a rite the Church held made his rule sacred. A monk had vowed his whole life to his monastery, so no bishop would anoint one taken out of it.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^104-1]: In the whole story: [Vortigern takes in Hengist and Horsa and gives them Thanet](../../story-map.md#hengist-and-horsa-land-on-thanet).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^104-2]: Gildas tells of the three ships too, in [*On the Ruin of Britain*](../../de-excidio-britanniae/de-excidio-britanniae.md#page-38), and calls them by the Saxons' own word, keels, "we'd say long ships." Bede puts the Saxons' coming about 449 CE.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^104-3]: Geoffrey calls them *Germanos*, a Latin word that means both brothers and Germans, so his sentence says both.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^105-1]: The Romans named other peoples' gods after their own. Tacitus, writing about 98 CE, says the Germans worship Mercury above all, and he means Woden. The Romans' Mercury's day became Woden's day in English, our Wednesday.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^105-2]: Giles prints *Fream*. Frigg was Woden's wife in the old Germanic myths, and Friday is named after her, as the Romans' Venus's day turned into Frigg's day in English.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^106-1]: In the whole story: [Vortigern marries Hengist's daughter and gives the Saxons Kent](../../story-map.md#vortigern-marries-hengists-daughter).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^107-1]: Hengist's trick is Dido's. In Virgil's *Aeneid*, Dido, fleeing from Tyre, buys as much land in Africa as a bull's hide will cover, cuts the hide into thin strips and rings a whole hill with them, and there she builds Carthage.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^107-2]: Thancastre is Old English for "thong castle," a thong being a leather strap. The place is usually taken for Caistor, in Lincolnshire, in the old district of Lindsey, though no identification is secure.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^108-1]: Rowena's greeting is Geoffrey's spelling of the English *wæs hæl*, "be well," and the answer, *drinc hæl*, means "drink and be well." The word wassail, for a toast or a drinking feast, comes from the greeting, and Geoffrey tells this as the custom's beginning.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^108-2]: The words are the Bible's for Judas. In Luke's gospel, Satan enters Judas just before he goes to the priests and agrees to hand Jesus over to them for money, and Geoffrey uses the same words for Vortigern, who'll hand over his people. The [*History of the Britons*](../../historia-brittonum/historia-brittonum.md#page-177) says it too.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^109-1]: The [*History of the Britons*](../../historia-brittonum/historia-brittonum.md#page-179) names the king of Kent Guoyrancgonus, and Geoffrey's *Gorangonus* is the same name. The English follows the older book, since no other source names him.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^109-2]: Giles prints *Pascentius*. Welsh tradition knows Vortigern's son as Pasgen, and the English names him so.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^109-3]: Germanus, bishop of Auxerre, really did come to Britain with Lupus of Troyes, in 429 CE, to argue down the followers of Pelagius. His life, written about 480, tells the visit. Geoffrey sets it in Vortigern's reign.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^109-4]: Pelagius was a British monk who taught in Rome around 400 CE that people can choose to do good by their own will. Augustine answered that no one can without Yahweh's grace, and the Church condemned Pelagius's teaching as heresy in 418.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^109-5]: Gildas's [*On the Ruin of Britain*](../../de-excidio-britanniae/de-excidio-britanniae.md), the only book of his that survives, never mentions Germanus. Geoffrey sends the reader to a book of Gildas's again, as in Part 4, and no such book is known.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^110-1]: In the whole story: [Vortimer fights the Saxons in four battles](../../story-map.md#vortimer-fights-the-saxons).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^110-2]: The [*History of the Britons*](../../historia-brittonum/historia-brittonum.md#page-187) gives Vortimer four battles too: on the river Derguentid, usually taken for the Darent, in Kent; at Episford; by the inscribed stone on the shore of the Channel; and the sieges of Thanet. Geoffrey names three and the siege.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^111-1]: In the [*History of the Britons*](../../historia-brittonum/historia-brittonum.md#page-188), Vortimer asks to be buried at the port where the Saxons land, so his grave will keep them away, and his people ignore him too. Welsh tradition held that his bones were buried in the island's chief ports and guarded it while they lay there.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^112-1]: In the whole story: [Hengist's men murder Vortigern's lords at a peace meeting](../../story-map.md#hengists-men-murder-the-british-lords).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^113-1]: Hengist's signal is Geoffrey's spelling of the English for "Take your knives!" The seax was the Saxons' long knife, and some took the people's name from it. In the [*History of the Britons*](../../historia-brittonum/historia-brittonum.md#page-190), the signal is *Eu nimet saxas*.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^113-2]: The [*History of the Britons*](../../historia-brittonum/historia-brittonum.md#page-190) says three hundred of Vortigern's elders were killed. Welsh tradition calls the massacre the Treason of the Long Knives.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^113-3]: Ambrius's monastery is Amesbury, in Wiltshire, near Stonehenge, whose name Geoffrey reads as Ambrius's town. He'll set the Giants' Dance over these graves in Part 8.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^114-1]: Geoffrey says Eldol sent each man he hit straight to Tartarus, the pit of the underworld in Greek and Roman myth where the wicked were punished. The English says he killed them.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^115-1]: In the whole story: [Vortigern's tower keeps falling, and his clerks blame a fatherless child](../../story-map.md#vortigerns-tower-keeps-falling).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^115-2]: Geoffrey writes *Erir*, the Welsh Eryri, the mountains around Snowdon. The [*History of the Britons*](../../historia-brittonum/historia-brittonum.md#page-182) sets the fort there too, and the hill fort the story belongs to is still called Dinas Emrys, Emrys's fort.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^115-3]: In the whole story: [Vortigern's messengers find Merlin and bring him to the king](../../story-map.md#the-messengers-bring-merlin-to-vortigern).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^115-4]: Kaermodin is Geoffrey's spelling of Caerfyrddin, the Welsh name of Carmarthen, a town near the Welsh coast in the far southwest. It was read as Merlin's fort, though the town's name came from the Roman Moridunum, "sea fort," and Merlin's Welsh name, Myrddin, may come from the town's.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^117-1]: Apuleius was a Latin writer of the 100s CE from north Africa. His book on the god of Socrates, the spirit Socrates said warned him, holds that spirits live in the air between the moon and the earth, halfway between gods and people.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^117-2]: In the whole story: [Merlin shows Vortigern the two dragons under his tower](../../story-map.md#merlin-shows-vortigern-the-dragons).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^117-3]: In the [*History of the Britons*](../../historia-brittonum/historia-brittonum.md#page-183), the fatherless boy is found at the plain of Elleti, in Glywysing, and when the king asks his name he says, "I'm called Ambrosius," that is, Emrys Wledig. Geoffrey makes that boy Merlin and moves his finding to Carmarthen.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^118-1]: In the whole story: [Merlin weeps and tells Vortigern what the two dragons mean](../../story-map.md#merlin-tells-vortigern-what-the-dragons-mean).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^118-2]: Geoffrey put the prophecies out as a little book of their own, by 1135 CE, before he finished the history. This chapter and the letter that follows are left from that book, which he set into the history here as Book VII.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^119-1]: By "British," Geoffrey means the Britons' language, the ancestor of Welsh, Cornish and Breton. No British book of Merlin's prophecies has ever been found, and most historians think Geoffrey wrote them himself, drawing on the Welsh poems that made the prophet Myrddin foretell the Britons' wars.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^119-2]: Minerva was the Roman goddess of wisdom and the arts. By "your Minerva," Geoffrey means Alexander's own learning and taste, which a better poet could please.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^119-3]: The Muses were the nine goddesses of poetry and the arts in Greek myth. The cane was the stick a schoolmaster kept time and punished with, so Geoffrey is asking Alexander to correct him the way a teacher corrects a pupil.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^120-1]: Readers have always taken the boar of Cornwall as Arthur. This book tells how he's conceived at Tintagel, in Cornwall, in Part 8, and his conquest of the islands, his wars in Gaul and his march on Rome in Parts 9 and 10.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^120-2]: The worm of Germany is the Saxons, who came from Germany, as the white dragon is.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^120-3]: Geoffrey's own story fits the sea wolf. In Part 11 the Saxons send for Gormund, king of the Africans, who comes with a great fleet from Ireland, which he's conquered, and helps them take Britain from its king.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^120-4]: In Part 4, Britain's first Christians put archbishops at London, York and the City of the Legions. In Part 11 Augustine comes from Rome to convert the English, and in Geoffrey's day Canterbury, where Augustine had made his seat, was the head of the English Church.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^120-5]: Geoffrey's own story fits this line too. In Part 8 Aurelius makes Samson archbishop of York, in Part 9 the Saxons drive him out, and Part 9 later names Saint Samson as archbishop of Dol, in Brittany.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^120-6]: The pallium is the band of white wool the pope gives an archbishop as the sign of his office. The City of the Legions is Caerleon, an archbishop's seat in this book. Bishop Bernard of St Davids was claiming in Geoffrey's day that his church had been an archbishopric, a claim Geoffrey supported.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^120-7]: The [*Life of Gildas*](../../vita-gildae/vita-gildae.md#page-108) tells the story behind this line. Gildas, preaching in Wales, lost his voice because Saint Non, pregnant with David, was in the church, and an angel told him her child would be greater than him. In the same book Gildas goes on to Ireland.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^120-8]: Part 12 tells this. When Cadwallon dies, the Britons embalm his body and set it in a bronze statue of a horseman, above the west gate of London, to frighten the Saxons.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^121-1]: The Britons' wars among themselves after Arthur are told in Parts 11 and 12, where Geoffrey blames them for the loss of the island.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^121-2]: Geoffrey's word for the god is *tonans*, "the one who thunders," a title Roman poets gave Jupiter. The English names the god Geoffrey means, Yahweh.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^121-3]: Part 12 tells this. A famine and then a plague empty Britain, and the Britons who are left sail away to Brittany.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^121-4]: The blessed king is Cadwaladr, the last king of the Britons in this book. In Part 12 he sails for Brittany, then goes to Rome at an angel's word, and dies there among the saints.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^121-5]: Giles prints *in fructuosos*, "into fruitful," which reads best as *infructuosos*, "fruitless," since the kingdom has just been emptied. The English follows that: "barren woods."<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^121-6]: Part 12 tells this. Once the plague has passed, the Saxons who are left send word to their people in Germany that Britain is empty of its natives and theirs for the taking.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^121-7]: Readers have taken the people in wood and iron as the Normans, who crossed in ships and wore coats of mail when William the Conqueror took England in 1066 CE. San-Marte, in 1854, says the line can only mean him.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^121-8]: Their mother is the earth, which they'll plough and dig as slaves.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^121-9]: The lion of justice is Henry I, who was king of England from 1100 to 1135 CE, when Geoffrey was writing. Geoffrey's phrase became Henry's byname.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^122-1]: Readers have taken the cubs changed into fish as Henry I's children who drowned in 1120 CE, when the White Ship sank off Normandy with his only legitimate son, William, and many of the young nobles of his court.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^122-2]: Geoffrey doesn't say where Mount Aravius is. In Part 10, Arthur says he once killed the giant Ritho on it.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^122-3]: Readers have taken these lines as Henry I's death in Normandy, at the end of 1135 CE. His body was taken to Rouen and embalmed, his insides were buried there, and the rest was carried to England and buried at Reading.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^122-4]: Readers agree on no king these lines tell of after Henry I. After him the prophecies run on past Geoffrey's own day, and readers agree far less on what they foretell.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^123-1]: Welsh prophetic poems promised that Cadwaladr and Cynan would come back to lead the Britons against the English, most of all the *Armes Prydein*, "The Prophecy of Britain," of the early 900s CE. Geoffrey makes this Cynan his Conan Meriadoc, the founder of Brittany.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^123-2]: Venus was the Roman goddess of love, and Cupid was her son, whose arrows made people fall in love. Her castle and camps are love's.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^124-1]: Giles prints *Canuti* as a name, and San-Marte reads it as Cnut, king of England from 1016 to 1035 CE, so this line can't be older than his reign. *Canutus* is also a Latin word for "grey," as Lewis and Short 282 gives it, which would make it the city of the grey forest. No place of either name is known. The English follows San-Marte: "Cnut's forest."<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^127-1]: Panchaia was an island Greek and Roman writers placed in the sea east of Arabia, rich in incense and precious stones, which nobody has ever found.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^129-1]: Geoffrey calls Gloucester the city of Claudius. In Part 4 he says the emperor Claudius founded it and gave it his name.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^129-2]: The planets take the names of Roman gods: Mercury, the gods' messenger, Mars, the god of war, Venus, the goddess of love. Stilbon, "the gleaming one," is the Greeks' name for the planet Mercury, and the god was said to have been born in Arcadia, in Greece.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^129-3]: Phoebus, "the bright one," is Apollo, the Greek and Roman god of the sun. Geoffrey doesn't say why Phoebus belongs to the sea here.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^129-4]: Saturn was the Roman god of the harvest, shown with a curved sickle. The star-readers of Geoffrey's day held his planet to be cold and to bring harm.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^129-5]: The twelve houses are the twelve signs of the zodiac, the band of stars the sun, the moon and the planets travel through. The planets are their guests, running wild now through the signs.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^130-1]: Cancer, the Crab, is the fourth sign of the zodiac. In Greek myth the goddess Hera sent a giant crab against Heracles while he fought the many-headed Hydra, and set it among the stars after he crushed it. The sun enters the sign at midsummer, so Geoffrey's crab quarrels with the sun.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^130-2]: Ariadne was the Cretan princess who helped Theseus escape the Minotaur's maze. The Greeks said the god Dionysus set her wedding crown among the stars as the Northern Crown. Geoffrey doesn't say whether that crown is the closed door he means.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^130-3]: In the whole story: [Merlin shows Vortigern the two dragons under his tower](../../story-map.md#merlin-shows-vortigern-the-dragons).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^131-1]: The boar of Cornwall is the beast of Merlin's prophecy in Part 7 that brings the Britons help and tramples the foreigners' necks, and readers have always taken it as Arthur. Here Merlin says it'll devour Vortigern's descendants too.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^131-2]: In the whole story: [Constans's sons land, and Pendragon is made king](../../story-map.md#pendragon-is-made-king).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^135-1]: Part 6 tells how Eldol fought his way out of the [massacre at the peace meeting](#page-114) with a stake he happened to find, killing seventy men before he got away to Gloucester.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^137-1]: In the first book of Samuel, Yahweh sends Saul, the first king of Israel, to destroy the Amalekites, Israel's old enemy, and everything they own. Saul spares their king, Agag, and the best of the animals. Samuel tells Saul that Yahweh no longer wants Saul as king, since Saul disobeyed. Then Samuel has Agag brought out, says these words and hacks Agag to pieces. Eldad means Hengist has earned the same end, as a man who's killed so many mothers' sons. 1 Samuel 15.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^138-1]: A chain in the hand and sand or dust on the head were signs of a man giving himself up: the chain offered for his own neck, the dust a show of grief and of how low he'd fallen.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^138-2]: In the book of Joshua, the Gibeonites, a people of Canaan, hear how Joshua's Israelites are taking the land. They come to him in worn-out clothes with dry, moldy bread, pretend they've travelled from far away and ask for a treaty, and Joshua swears one. When the trick comes out, the Israelites keep their oath and spare them, putting them to work cutting wood and carrying water. Eldad's point is that even Israel kept faith with people who'd asked for mercy. Joshua 9.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^139-1]: In the whole story: [Merlin brings stones from Ireland and raises them at Salisbury](../../story-map.md#the-stones-of-salisbury).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^140-1]: The Giants' Dance is Stonehenge, the ring of standing stones on Salisbury Plain, two miles west of Amesbury. It's prehistoric, raised in stages thousands of years before Geoffrey, and he's the first writer known to say it was raised over the dead and that its stones could heal.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^142-1]: Pentecost, seven weeks after Easter, is the feast of the Holy Spirit coming down on Jesus's followers. Kings in Geoffrey's day wore their crowns in state and held court at the great feasts of the year, as Aurelius does here.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^143-1]: In the whole story: [The brothers fight the Saxons at Salisbury](../../story-map.md#the-battle-of-salisbury).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^144-1]: A centurion commanded a hundred men in the Roman army. Pasgen offers the rank as if it were still a great office.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^148-1]: The Welsh *pen* means "head" and *dragon* "dragon," and Geoffrey reads the name that way. Uther is Uthr Bendragon in older Welsh poems, so the name came first, and Geoffrey seems to have made up the dragon star to explain it.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^148-2]: In the whole story: [Gorlois advises Uther to attack the Saxons by night](../../story-map.md#gorlois-advises-a-night-attack).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^148-3]: Geoffrey says *Arctos*, the bear of the northern sky, had begun to turn its wagon's pole. Its seven bright stars were seen as a wagon wheeling around the pole star through the night, so their turn told the hour. He means the night was well on.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^150-1]: In the whole story: [Uther falls in love with Igraine and makes war on her husband](../../story-map.md#uther-falls-in-love-with-igraine). Malory tells this in [Book I, chapter 1](../../malory-sources/malory-map.tsv). There, Uther has long been at war with the duke of Tintagel, sends for him and his wife to make peace, and loves Igraine at that first meeting; she refuses him and tells her husband, and the two ride home by night.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^151-1]: In the whole story: [Merlin brings Uther to Igraine in her husband's shape](../../story-map.md#merlin-brings-uther-to-igraine). Malory tells this in [Book I, chapters 1–2](../../malory-sources/malory-map.tsv). There, Merlin names his price before the night at Tintagel, the child Uther will father, and the duke has been dead three hours and more when Uther comes to Igraine.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^153-1]: In the whole story: [Uther, sick, is carried into a last battle with the Saxons](../../story-map.md#uther-is-carried-into-his-last-battle).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^153-2]: Giles prints *Erat autem consul ille Leir*, "That earl was Leir." San-Marte notes that the Welsh *Brut Tysilio* says *Llyr* here, and takes Leir and Lot for one name, so the English names him once, as Lot.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^154-1]: Malory tells this in [Book I, chapter 4](../../malory-sources/malory-map.tsv). There, Merlin has Uther carried into battle, and after the victory Uther lies speechless for three days and dies once he's named Arthur his heir in front of his barons; nobody poisons a spring.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^157-1]: In the whole story: [Arthur is knighted and crowned at Pentecost](../../story-map.md#arthur-is-crowned).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^157-2]: In the whole story: [Arthur leads the Britons' kings in twelve battles against the Saxons](../../story-map.md#arthurs-twelve-battles).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^159-1]: Giles and San-Marte both print *ex Dubricio*, so Hoel's father shares his name with the archbishop. San-Marte thinks Geoffrey turned Budic, the king of Brittany [Part 6](#page-103) names, into Dubricius here, and the English keeps the name as printed: "Dubricius, king of the Bretons."<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^159-2]: The [*History of the Britons*](../../historia-brittonum/historia-brittonum.md#page-199) sets Arthur's second to fifth battles on the river Dubglas, in the region of Lindsey, and his seventh in Celidon Wood. Geoffrey makes them the first war of Arthur's reign.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^160-1]: Geoffrey's *pagus Badonis*, "the district of Badon," is the Mount Badon where Gildas and the *History of the Britons* say the Britons won their greatest victory over the Saxons. Geoffrey sets it at Bath, the city whose founding by King Bladud he tells in Part 2.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^160-2]: In the whole story: [The Britons and the Saxons fight by turns until the siege of Mount Badon](../../story-map.md#the-siege-of-mount-badon).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^161-1]: In his letter to the Romans, the apostle Paul begs the Christians of Rome to give their bodies to their god as a living sacrifice, holy and pleasing to it, and calls this the worship that makes sense. Dubricius turns his words to a soldier's death in battle. Romans 12.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^161-2]: The first letter of John says Christians know what love is because Jesus laid down his life for them, and so they in turn ought to lay down their lives for their brothers. Dubricius makes a death in this battle that same gift. 1 John 3.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^161-3]: Dubricius promises what the Church promised crusaders in Geoffrey's own day. When Pope Urban II called the First Crusade in 1095 CE, he said that those who died on the way would have their sins forgiven, as if the fighting were itself their penance.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^161-4]: The [*History of the Britons*](../../historia-brittonum/historia-brittonum.md#page-200) says that in his eighth battle, at Fort Guinnion, Arthur carried an image of Mary on his shoulders. Geoffrey puts her image on his shield.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^161-5]: Geoffrey calls the sword *Caliburnus*, a Latin form of its Welsh name, Caledfwlch. The French romances made it Escalibor, and it's been Excalibur in English since.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^162-1]: The [*History of the Britons*](../../historia-brittonum/historia-brittonum.md#page-200) says that at Mount Badon nine hundred sixty men fell in one charge of Arthur's, and nobody struck them down but Arthur himself.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^163-1]: In the whole story: [Arthur conquers Ireland, the northern islands, Norway and Gaul](../../story-map.md#arthur-conquers-ireland-and-the-north).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^163-2]: Geoffrey takes the loch's sixty islands, its sixty rocks with their eagles' nests and its sixty rivers, only one running to the sea, from the first of Britain's strange sights in the [*History of the Britons*](../../historia-brittonum/historia-brittonum.md#page-213).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^164-1]: In the whole story: [Strange sights of Britain include Arthur's dog's footprint and his son's grave](../../story-map.md#britains-strange-sights).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^164-2]: Linligwan is the pool the [*History of the Britons*](../../historia-brittonum/historia-brittonum.md#page-214) calls Oper Linn Liuan, which swallows the Severn's tide and then spits it back out in one wave as high as a mountain.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^165-1]: In the whole story: [Arthur conquers Ireland, the northern islands, Norway and Gaul](../../story-map.md#arthur-conquers-ireland-and-the-north).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^166-1]: Giles prints *sororem ipsius*, "his sister," which could mean the sister of Aurelius Ambrosius. The English takes it as Arthur's sister, since Part 8 tells how Uther gave his daughter Anna to Lot, though she wasn't born until after Aurelius died.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^166-2]: In the whole story: [Arthur marries Guinevere and receives the Round Table](../../story-map.md#arthur-marries-guinevere).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^166-3]: In the whole story: [Arthur conquers Ireland, the northern islands, Norway and Gaul](../../story-map.md#arthur-conquers-ireland-and-the-north).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^170-1]: Geoffrey calls Kay Arthur's *dapifer*, the officer who served the king's table, and Bedivere his *pincerna*, who served his wine. At a king's court in Geoffrey's day these were great offices, held by nobles.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^170-2]: In the whole story: [Arthur wears his crown at a Pentecost court at Caerleon](../../story-map.md#arthur-holds-court-at-caerleon).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>
