[^171-1]: The heroes' names are Welsh, and *map* is Welsh for "son of," so Danaut Mappapo is Danaut son of Papo. Giles prints each name apart from its *map*, with a comma between, and the English joins them. These men are known from this list alone.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^171-2]: The French epics of Geoffrey's day, as the *Song of Roland*, give Charlemagne twelve peers, the great lords who fight and die beside Roland. Geoffrey gives Gaul the same twelve in Arthur's time.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^173-1]: Giles prints *cum Celtibus*, a word nobody can read for sure. San-Marte prints *telis*, and the English follows him: "javelins."<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^174-1]: In the whole story: [Rome demands tribute, and Arthur sets out to fight the emperor](../../story-map.md#rome-demands-tribute). Malory tells this in [Book V, chapters 1–2](../../malory-sources/malory-map.tsv). There, the envoys come to a feast of the Round Table and give their message by word of mouth, Cador is the first to speak when Arthur asks his lords' advice, and the kings of Scotland and Brittany promise him armies.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^174-2]: A procurator was an officer of Rome who governed a small province or managed its revenues for the emperor. Geoffrey makes Lucius Tiberius the procurator of the whole Republic, the Roman state, though other passages of his give Lucius the title of emperor.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^177-1]: Marcus Tullius Cicero, the Roman speaker and statesman of the first century BCE, was the model of Latin speaking for every schoolboy of Geoffrey's day. Geoffrey writes *Tulliano liquore*, "with Tullius's liquid," so Hoel is praising Arthur's speech as worthy of Cicero.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^177-2]: The Sibyls were prophetesses of the ancient world. Rome kept books of their prophecies and read them in times of danger, and Christian writers later took them as foretelling Christ. Hoel counts Belinus and Constantine as the first two of the three.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^180-1]: In the whole story: [Rome demands tribute, and Arthur sets out to fight the emperor](../../story-map.md#rome-demands-tribute). Malory tells this in [Book V, chapter 2](../../malory-sources/malory-map.tsv). There, Lucius raises his army from every part of his empire, with fifty giants among it, and marches into France.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^180-2]: Geoffrey gives the kings and dukes of the East names out of older history and legend. Micipsa and Bocchus were kings in North Africa in the second and first centuries BCE, Sertorius was a Roman general, Serses is the Persian king Xerxes, and Teucer, Evander, Hippolytus and Epistrophus are heroes of Greek and Roman legend. The men in this list are Geoffrey's own, and Epistrophius, Mustensar, Aethion and Hippolytus are known from it alone.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^180-3]: Malory tells this in [Book V, chapter 3](../../malory-sources/malory-map.tsv). There, Arthur leaves the kingdom and Guinevere in the care of Baudwin and Constantine, names Constantine his heir, and sails from Sandwich.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^181-1]: In the whole story: [Arthur dreams of a dragon and kills the giant of Mont Saint-Michel](../../story-map.md#the-giant-of-mont-saint-michel). Malory tells this in [Book V, chapter 4](../../malory-sources/malory-map.tsv). There, the dragon fights a black boar from the east, and a wise man reads the dream.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^181-2]: Malory tells this in [Book V, chapter 5](../../malory-sources/malory-map.tsv). There, a farmer brings Arthur the news, and the woman the giant has carried off is the Duchess of Brittany, the wife of Arthur's cousin Howell.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^184-1]: Geoffrey's *Rithonem* is Rhitta, a giant of the Welsh tales. He asks Arthur for his beard to patch his cloak of beards, and Arthur kills him in single combat, in one telling up in Snowdonia, the mountains of north Wales.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^184-2]: Helena's Tomb is Tombelaine, a rock island in the bay a little north of Mont Saint-Michel. Wikipedia's Tombelaine says one folk story takes its name from "the tomb of Hélène", a daughter of King Hoel buried on the rock.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^184-3]: In the whole story: [Arthur's envoys defy Lucius, and the two armies meet at Soissons](../../story-map.md#the-battle-against-lucius). Malory tells this in [Book V, chapter 6](../../malory-sources/malory-map.tsv). There, Arthur sends Gawain with Bors, Lionel and Bedivere, and the Roman Gawain beheads is a kinsman of the emperor.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^185-1]: Giles prints Boso's land here as *Devadoboum*, *de Vado Boum*, Latin for "of the ford of oxen", Geoffrey's Latin for Oxford, which Book IX calls Ridoc.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^185-2]: Giles prints *in tragulam*, "into the javelin", which makes no sense of where the lance went. The English says only that it went into the man: "drove his lance into the first man he met".<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^189-1]: Geoffrey names these four only here. Giles prints the first as *Hirelgam Deperirum*, and the English reads it as Hirelgas of Periron. He isn't Bedivere's nephew Hirelgas, who avenges his uncle in the great battle on page 195.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^189-2]: Giles's index of names doesn't place the valley of Suesia, and San-Marte leaves it unplaced too. In Malory the battle is fought in the vale of Soissons.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^190-1]: Giles prints *Richiden*, Geoffrey's British name for Oxford, the town Book IX calls Ridoc.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^190-2]: Giles prints the earl of Leicester here as *Vigenis* and the earl of Bath as *Urbgennius*. They're the Jugein and Urgennius who came to Arthur's Pentecost court, and the English names them so.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^192-1]: Lucius means that Yahweh had already fixed the day each of them would die, so death in battle wasn't to be feared. It's the Christian teaching of providence, which Geoffrey puts in the mouth of a pagan Roman.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^193-1]: The Latin can be read as all twelve wedges holding six thousand six hundred sixty-six between them. The English takes the number for each wedge, as Arthur's own legion holds the same number.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^193-2]: Laelius Hostiensis and Sulpicius Subuculus are named only here, and nothing more is known of them.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^194-1]: Arthur gave Bedivere Normandy and Kay Anjou at his Pentecost court, so Normandy mourns Bedivere as its duke, and the men of Anjou mourn Kay as their earl.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^196-1]: Richomarcus, Bloccovius and Lagivius of Bodloan are named only here, and nothing more is known of them.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^199-1]: Giles prints Kay's name here as *Cheudo*, and San-Marte's note on it says Kay is meant.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^200-1]: In the whole story: [Mordred takes the crown, and Gawain dies at Dover](../../story-map.md#mordred-takes-the-crown). Malory tells this in [Book XXI, chapter 1](../../malory-sources/malory-map.tsv). There, Mordred forges letters saying Arthur is dead and is crowned at Canterbury, and Guinevere shuts herself in the Tower of London against him.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^200-2]: The earl is Robert of Gloucester, who Geoffrey offered the whole book to at its start.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^200-3]: In the whole story: [Mordred takes the crown, and Gawain dies at Dover](../../story-map.md#mordred-takes-the-crown).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^200-4]: Walter of Oxford is the archdeacon who, Geoffrey says at the start, gave him the very old book in British that this history comes from.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^201-1]: Malory tells this in [Book XXI, chapter 2](../../malory-sources/malory-map.tsv). There, Arthur lands at Dover rather than Richborough, Mordred is driven back, and Gawain is found dying in a boat of the old wound Lancelot gave him.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^202-1]: In the whole story: [Arthur fights Mordred's host and is carried to Avalon](../../story-map.md#the-last-battle).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^202-2]: Geoffrey's river has long been taken for the Camel, in north Cornwall, where local tradition places the battle near Camelford. The Welsh annals, the oldest record of Arthur's last battle, call it Camlann.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^203-1]: Malory tells this in [Book XXI, chapter 4](../../malory-sources/malory-map.tsv). There, Gawain's ghost has warned Arthur in a dream not to fight, and the battle starts by accident at a truce, when a knight draws his sword on an adder.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^203-2]: These Saxon leaders are known from Geoffrey's list alone, except Cheldric, Mordred's ally from Germany.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^203-3]: These Irish leaders are known from Geoffrey's list alone. Their names are built on the Irish *gilla*, "servant," as Gillapatric is *Gilla Pátraic*, "servant of Patrick."<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^203-4]: These are men of Arthur's side named only here, a Cador apart from the duke of Cornwall, and a Cassivellaunus apart from the king who fought Julius Caesar.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^203-5]: Malory tells this in [Book XXI, chapter 5](../../malory-sources/malory-map.tsv). There, Bedivere throws Excalibur into the water at Arthur's command, and three queens carry Arthur away in a barge to the vale of Avilion to heal his wound.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^203-6]: That is 542 CE. The Welsh annals put the battle of Camlann five years earlier, in 537 CE.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^204-1]: In the whole story: [Constantine and the kings after him rule Britain, and Gormund lays it waste](../../story-map.md#the-kings-after-arthur).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^204-2]: Rhygyfarch's *Life of Saint David*, written about 1090 CE, tells that Patrick wanted to settle in the valley where St Davids now stands, until an angel told him it was kept for a child to be born thirty years later. So Patrick went on to Ireland.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^204-3]: Llanbadarn Fawr, near Aberystwyth in west Wales, was a monastery and bishop's seat named after its founder, Saint Padarn.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^204-4]: Gildas, in [*On the Ruin of Britain*](../../de-excidio-britanniae/de-excidio-britanniae.md), condemns Constantine, king of Dumnonia in the southwest, for killing two royal boys in a church, in front of the altar, while he wore an abbot's cloak. Geoffrey makes the boys Mordred's sons.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^204-5]: This is the Giants' Dance, the ring of stones Merlin brought from Ireland in Part 8, where Aurelius and Uther were buried before him.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^205-1]: Gildas calls him Aurelius Caninus, the second of five British kings he condemns by name, and charges him with loving civil war. Geoffrey takes him, and the two kings after him, from Gildas's list.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^205-2]: Genesis 19 tells how the men of Sodom surrounded Lot's house and demanded the two angels staying there, and how Yahweh then destroyed the city with fire from the sky. Medieval writers called sex between men "the sin of Sodom," and Geoffrey means that here.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^206-1]: Gormund and Isembard come from an Old French poem, *Gormont et Isembart*, written by about 1100 CE, about a pagan king and a French traitor who fight King Louis. It remembers Louis III's victory over the Vikings at Saucourt in 881 CE.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^206-2]: Geoffrey takes these words from Gildas's [*On the Ruin of Britain*](../../de-excidio-britanniae/de-excidio-britanniae.md), where it's the Saxons' fire that burns the island from sea to sea.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^207-1]: Through Jeremiah, Yahweh tells Israel that it planted Israel as a choice vine from the best seed, and asks how Israel became a wild vine gone bad (Jeremiah 2). Geoffrey turns the image on the Britons, once strong and now spoiled.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^207-2]: When people said Jesus drove out demons by the power of Beelzebub, prince of demons, Jesus answered that a kingdom divided against itself is laid waste, and a divided house falls, so Satan couldn't be fighting Satan (Luke 11). Geoffrey turns the saying on the Britons' wars among themselves.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^207-3]: The lioness's cubs are the English, born of the Saxons who came over as Vortigern's soldiers.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^208-1]: No such book by Geoffrey survives, and he probably never wrote it.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^208-2]: In the whole story: [Augustine comes to the English, and Aethelfrith marches on the monks of Bangor](../../story-map.md#augustine-comes-to-the-english). Pope Gregory the Great sent Augustine with about forty monks in 597 CE. He landed in Kent, baptised its king, Aethelberht, and became the first archbishop of Canterbury.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^209-1]: Geoffrey told in Part 4 how King Lucius sent to Pope Eleutherius for teachers, and how Britain became Christian.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^209-2]: This Bangor is Bangor-on-Dee, near Wrexham, whose great monastery Bede describes. It isn't Deiniol's Bangor in Gwynedd.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^210-1]: Bede, who tells this battle in his *Ecclesiastical History*, sets it at Legacaestir, which is Chester, and the Welsh annals record it in 613 CE. Giles prints *Legecestriam*, Geoffrey's spelling elsewhere for Leicester, and the English follows Bede: "Chester".<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^210-2]: Bede tells that Augustine warned the British bishops that if they wouldn't preach to the English, they'd die at English hands, and that Aethelfrith's men killed about twelve hundred monks of Bangor who'd come to Chester to pray.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^211-1]: In the whole story: [Cadwallon and Penda fight the Northumbrians, and the Britons fight the Mercians](../../story-map.md#the-britons-fight-the-northumbrians-and-the-mercians).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^211-2]: Bede's Edwin was the son of Aelle, king of Deira, and spent his youth in exile from Aethelfrith, who had taken his father's kingdom. Geoffrey makes him Aethelfrith's son, raised at a British court beside Cadwallon.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^215-1]: Kidaleta is Aleth, the old walled town at Saint-Servan, beside Saint-Malo on the north coast of Brittany.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^217-1]: Geoffrey puts into Cadwallon's mouth, nearly word for word, the charge Gildas brings against the Britons of his own day in [*On the Ruin of Britain*](../../de-excidio-britanniae/de-excidio-britanniae.md), down to the anointed kings killed by those who anointed them.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^217-2]: Paul warns the Christians of Corinth against false apostles who pass themselves off as Christ's, and says it's no wonder, since Satan himself disguises himself as an angel of light (2 Corinthians 11). Gildas, and Geoffrey after him, use the words for people who welcome evil because it looks good.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^218-1]: This line runs from Maelgwn down to Cadwallon on one side and to Salomon on the other. The Welsh genealogies make Rhun, Geoffrey's Runo, the father of Beli and so the forefather of Cadfan; Ennianus, Alan and this second Hoel are known from Geoffrey alone.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^220-1]: Bede sets Edwin's death at Hatfield Chase, in 633 CE. Heavenfield, near Hexham, is where Oswald beat Cadwallon the next year, and Geoffrey uses its name for both battles.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^220-2]: Aedan was king of Dál Riata, the Scots' kingdom in what's now Argyll, and died about 609 CE, a generation before Osric's time.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^221-1]: Bede tells that Oswald set up a wooden cross at Heavenfield and prayed with his army before the battle, and that there, in 634 CE, Oswald's army killed Cadwallon. Geoffrey turns the battle into a defeat of Penda, and keeps Cadwallon alive.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^221-2]: Bede calls the place of Oswald's death Maserfield, and sets it in 642 CE. The Church remembers Oswald as a saint and martyr.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^222-1]: Giles prints Ethelfridum here, Aethelfrith, who had died long before. The man Oswiu drove out is the brother named a little earlier, Alhfrith, and the English follows the sense: "Alhfrith".<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^223-1]: Bede tells this battle, at the Winwaed in 655 CE, where Penda died with thirty royal commanders. In Bede, Cadwallon had been dead for over twenty years by then.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^223-2]: The Latin gives the date the Roman way, the fifteenth day before the first of December. The real Cadwallon was killed at Heavenfield in 634 CE.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^223-3]: The church of St Martin, Ludgate, still stands near the site of London's old west gate.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^224-1]: Bede names an Oethelwald, a king of Deira who sided with Penda, but nothing in Bede makes him Cadwaladr. The claim is Geoffrey's own.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^224-2]: In the whole story: [The Britons leave the island](../../story-map.md#the-britons-leave-the-island).<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^224-3]: Psalm 44 is a song of mourning for Israel, beaten in war though it had kept faith. It says Yahweh has handed Israel over like sheep to be eaten and scattered it among the nations, and begs Yahweh to wake and help. The fleeing Britons sing its eleventh verse.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^225-1]: The Ambrones were a Germanic people who fought Rome beside the Cimbri and Teutones in the 100s BCE. Geoffrey uses the name for the invaders of Britain.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^226-1]: In this book Merlin speaks his prophecies to Vortigern, in Part 7, and never to Arthur. Giles prints "Arthur" here.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^226-2]: Sergius I was pope from 687 to 701 CE.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^227-1]: Geoffrey told of the Eagle in Part 2, where it spoke while the walls of Shaftesbury were being built.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^227-2]: Ini recalls Ine, king of the West Saxons from 688 to 726 CE, who gave up his crown and went to Rome to die. Geoffrey makes him a Briton.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^227-3]: Bede tells this of Caedwalla, king of the West Saxons, who went to Rome, was baptised by Pope Sergius and died there on 20 April 689 CE. Geoffrey gives that death to Cadwaladr, who died in Britain, probably of plague, in 664 or 682 CE. Geoffrey's Latin says Cadwaladr was "freed from the flesh."<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^228-1]: The English word "Welsh" comes from the Old English *wealh*, "foreigner," the name the English gave the Britons. Gualo and Guales are Geoffrey's own.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^228-2]: Athelstan ruled from 924 to 939 CE, and was the first king of all England.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^228-3]: Caradoc of Llancarfan wrote a *Life of Gildas* and a *Life of Cadoc* in Geoffrey's time. No history of the Welsh kings by him survives.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>

[^228-4]: William of Malmesbury and Henry of Huntingdon were the leading English historians of Geoffrey's day, each the author of a history of the English kings.<sup>[\*](#new-in-this-translation)</sup>
